# Introduction to "Java Programming" by Davis Miller

Java has become an integral part of modern software development, powering everything from mobile applications to enterprise systems and even large-scale cloud-based services. "Java Programming" by Davis Miller offers an in-depth journey into this versatile programming language, structured to guide learners from the basics to advanced topics.

This book is designed for aspiring programmers, seasoned developers, and anyone looking to strengthen their understanding of Java. Whether you are learning programming for the first time or refining your skills, this comprehensive guide equips you with the knowledge and practical skills needed to excel in the world of Java development.

### Key Features of the Book

1.  Comprehensive Coverage: The book spans a wide range of topics, starting from Java’s history and basic syntax, moving through core programming concepts, and advancing to topics like multithreading, database connectivity, and web development.

1.  Hands-On Learning: Each chapter includes examples with explanation to help readers understand the concept.

1.  Focus on Best Practices: Emphasis is placed on writing clean, efficient, and maintainable code, supported by real-world examples and design patterns.

1.  Up-to-Date Content: Covers modern Java features such as functional programming, Streams API, and cloud integration, ensuring relevance in today’s technology landscape.

1.  Step-by-Step Learning: The progression of topics is logical and incremental, making it easy for readers to build on their knowledge as they progress.

### Structure of the Book

#### 1\. Introduction to Java

The book begins with an overview of Java, its history, features, and editions. It provides detailed guidance on setting up the development environment and writing your first Java program.

#### 2\. Basics of Java Programming

This section covers foundational concepts like syntax, data types, operators, control statements, and arrays, essential for writing basic Java programs.

#### 3\. Object-Oriented Programming (OOP) in Java

Delve into OOP principles like encapsulation, inheritance, polymorphism, and abstraction, along with practical implementations in Java.

#### 4\. Core Java Concepts

Learn about packages, exception handling, the collections framework, generics, and wrapper classes, providing the tools to manage complex applications.

#### 5\. Advanced Java Topics

Explore multithreading, lambda expressions, Streams API, and functional interfaces for advanced programming capabilities.

#### 6\. Java Development Tools

Understand debugging, build tools like Maven and Gradle, and unit testing frameworks like JUnit to streamline development workflows.

#### 7\. Working with Java Libraries

Master Java’s I/O and NIO APIs, date and time manipulation, and regular expressions for efficient data handling.

#### 8\. Database Connectivity

This chapter introduces JDBC and ORM frameworks like Hibernate for seamless database interaction.

#### 9\. GUI Development

Learn to create intuitive graphical interfaces using Swing and JavaFX, complete with event handling and CSS styling.

#### 10\. Web Development with Java

Understand server-side programming with servlets, JSP, and frameworks like Spring MVC for building dynamic web applications.

#### 11\. Java in the Cloud and Microservices

Explore deploying Java applications on cloud platforms and building microservices using Spring Boot.

#### 12\. Performance and Optimization

Discover Java’s memory management, garbage collection, and tools for profiling and optimization.

#### 13\. Java for Modern Applications

Uncover Java’s role in big data, machine learning, IoT, and Android development.

#### 14\. Best Practices and Design Patterns

Learn to write clean code and implement design patterns such as Singleton, Factory, and Observer for robust applications.

#### 15\. Project Development

The final chapter guides readers through developing a full-fledged Java application, integrating version control, testing, and deployment.

### Why This Book?

"Java Programming" by Davis Miller is not just a book; it’s a roadmap to mastering Java. With its blend of theory, practical exercises, and insights into real-world application, this book ensures that learners are prepared for both academic pursuits and professional challenges.

Start your Java journey today and unlock endless possibilities in software development!

# Introduction to Java

## History of Java

The development of Java began in the early 1990s by a team of engineers led by James Gosling at Sun Microsystems, a company later acquired by Oracle Corporation. Here's a timeline of Java's evolution:

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Early Beginnings (1991)

<bdi>●</bdi>Java's development started as part of the Green Project at Sun Microsystems, aimed at creating software for electronic devices.

<bdi>●</bdi>Initially, it was called Oak, named after an oak tree outside Gosling's office.

<bdi>●</bdi>Oak was designed to be a platform-independent language for consumer electronics like televisions and set-top boxes.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Renaming to Java (1995)

<bdi>●</bdi>The language was renamed Java after a type of coffee, reflecting the developers’ love for coffee.

<bdi>●</bdi>In 1995, Java was officially released with the slogan "Write Once, Run Anywhere" (WORA) to emphasize its platform-independent nature.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Key Milestones in Java’s History

<bdi>●</bdi>1995: Release of Java 1.0. It gained widespread popularity as a tool for web applets that could run on browsers.

<bdi>●</bdi>1998: Release of Java 2 (J2SE 1.2), introducing Swing and Collections Framework, and splitting Java into different platforms:

<bdi>○</bdi>J2SE (Standard Edition)

<bdi>○</bdi>J2EE (Enterprise Edition)

<bdi>○</bdi>J2ME (Micro Edition)

<bdi>●</bdi>2004: Java 5 introduced major updates like Generics, the enhanced for loop, and annotations.

<bdi>●</bdi>2006: Sun Microsystems made Java an open-source project under the GNU General Public License (GPL).

<bdi>●</bdi>2009: Oracle acquired Sun Microsystems, gaining control of Java.

<bdi>●</bdi>2011: Oracle released Java 7, introducing features like the try-with-resources statement.

<bdi>●</bdi>2014: Java 8 introduced lambdas, the Stream API, and functional programming concepts.

<bdi>●</bdi>2017: Introduction of a faster release cycle (one major version every six months).

<bdi>●</bdi>2021: Release of Java 17 as a Long-Term Support (LTS) version, focusing on stability and performance enhancements.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Impact of Java

Java's simplicity, portability, and robustness have made it one of the most popular programming languages globally. It is widely used in:

<bdi>●</bdi>Web applications

<bdi>●</bdi>Enterprise software

<bdi>●</bdi>Android app development

<bdi>●</bdi>Embedded systems

<bdi>●</bdi>Big Data and cloud computing

Java's history reflects its evolution into a mature, versatile language that remains a cornerstone of modern software development.

## Features of Java

Java is renowned for its robust design, simplicity, and platform independence. Here are the key features that make Java unique and widely used:

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Platform Independence

<bdi>●</bdi>Java follows the "Write Once, Run Anywhere" (WORA) philosophy.

<bdi>●</bdi>Java code is compiled into bytecode by the Java compiler, which can run on any device equipped with a Java Virtual Machine (JVM).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Object-Oriented

<bdi>●</bdi>Java is a fully object-oriented programming language that supports concepts like:

<bdi>○</bdi>Classes and Objects

<bdi>○</bdi>Inheritance

<bdi>○</bdi>Polymorphism

<bdi>○</bdi>Encapsulation

<bdi>○</bdi>Abstraction

<bdi>●</bdi>Even basic data types can be converted into objects using wrapper classes.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Simple

<bdi>●</bdi>Java has a syntax similar to C++ but removes complex features like pointers and multiple inheritance, making it easy to learn and use.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Secure

<bdi>●</bdi>Java provides several built-in security features:

<bdi>○</bdi>No explicit pointer manipulation.

<bdi>○</bdi>Bytecode verification by the JVM.

<bdi>○</bdi>Security APIs for cryptography and authentication.

<bdi>○</bdi>Sandboxing for applets to restrict unauthorized operations.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Robust

<bdi>●</bdi>Java is designed to eliminate errors during runtime by:

<bdi>○</bdi>Strong memory management.

<bdi>○</bdi>Automatic Garbage Collection.

<bdi>○</bdi>Exception handling.

<bdi>○</bdi>Type safety.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Multithreaded

<bdi>●</bdi>Java supports multithreading, allowing multiple threads to run simultaneously.

<bdi>●</bdi>This feature is essential for building highly responsive applications, such as games or real-time systems.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 7\. High Performance

<bdi>●</bdi>Java is faster than traditional interpreted languages due to the Just-In-Time (JIT) compiler.

<bdi>●</bdi>JIT compiles bytecode into native machine code during execution, improving performance.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 8\. Distributed

<bdi>●</bdi>Java makes it easy to create distributed systems through:

<bdi>○</bdi>Remote Method Invocation (RMI)

<bdi>○</bdi>CORBA

<bdi>○</bdi>Support for networked applications via its built-in networking libraries.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 9\. Dynamic and Extensible

<bdi>●</bdi>Java programs can adapt to evolving environments by dynamically loading classes during runtime.

<bdi>●</bdi>Its extensibility comes from the ability to integrate native methods written in C or C++ via the Java Native Interface (JNI).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 10\. Architecture Neutral

<bdi>●</bdi>Java ensures platform neutrality through bytecode, which is not tied to any specific architecture or operating system.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 11\. Portable

<bdi>●</bdi>Java bytecode and libraries can run consistently across platforms, as long as a JVM is available, making it a portable language.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 12\. Rich API

<bdi>●</bdi>Java provides a wide array of APIs for:

<bdi>○</bdi>Data structures (e.g., Collections Framework)

<bdi>○</bdi>Networking

<bdi>○</bdi>Multithreading

<bdi>○</bdi>File I/O

<bdi>○</bdi>GUI development (e.g., Swing, JavaFX)

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

These features collectively make Java a powerful, versatile language suitable for various applications, from web and mobile to enterprise systems.

## Java Editions

Java is divided into different editions to cater to various development needs and platforms. Each edition serves a specific purpose and provides a tailored set of libraries, tools, and APIs.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Java Standard Edition (JSE)

#### Purpose:

JSE, or Java SE, is the core edition of Java used for general-purpose programming. It provides all the fundamental APIs and tools needed to develop desktop, console, and basic server-side applications.

#### Key Features:

<bdi>●</bdi>Core Java libraries (e.g., java.lang, java.util, java.io)

<bdi>●</bdi>Object-Oriented Programming support

<bdi>●</bdi>Basic data structures (e.g., Collections Framework)

<bdi>●</bdi>Multi-threading and concurrency tools

<bdi>●</bdi>Networking libraries (e.g., sockets, HTTP)

<bdi>●</bdi>Java Database Connectivity (JDBC) for database interaction

<bdi>●</bdi>JavaFX and AWT/Swing for graphical user interface (GUI) development

<bdi>●</bdi>Security and encryption APIs

#### Use Cases:

<bdi>●</bdi>Desktop applications

<bdi>●</bdi>Console-based tools

<bdi>●</bdi>Foundation for learning Java

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Java Enterprise Edition (JEE)

#### Purpose:

JEE, or Java EE (now called Jakarta EE), is designed for developing large-scale, distributed, and multi-tiered enterprise applications.

#### Key Features:

<bdi>●</bdi>Advanced APIs and frameworks for web and enterprise development:

<bdi>○</bdi>Servlets and JavaServer Pages (JSP) for web applications

<bdi>○</bdi>Enterprise JavaBeans (EJB) for modular business logic

<bdi>○</bdi>Java Persistence API (JPA) for ORM (Object-Relational Mapping)

<bdi>○</bdi>Java Message Service (JMS) for asynchronous communication

<bdi>○</bdi>RESTful and SOAP-based web services

<bdi>○</bdi>Java Transaction API (JTA) for transaction management

<bdi>●</bdi>Application server support (e.g., GlassFish, WildFly, Apache TomEE)

#### Use Cases:

<bdi>●</bdi>Web applications (e.g., e-commerce websites)

<bdi>●</bdi>Enterprise-grade applications (e.g., ERP systems, banking software)

<bdi>●</bdi>Cloud-based applications

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Java Micro Edition (JME)

#### Purpose:

JME, or Java ME, is a stripped-down version of Java designed for resource-constrained devices like embedded systems and mobile devices.

#### Key Features:

<bdi>●</bdi>Lightweight runtime environment for constrained devices

<bdi>●</bdi>APIs for device-specific features (e.g., Bluetooth, sensors, user input)

<bdi>●</bdi>Configurations like Connected Limited Device Configuration (CLDC) and Mobile Information Device Profile (MIDP)

<bdi>●</bdi>Support for small-footprint devices with limited memory and processing power

#### Use Cases:

<bdi>●</bdi>Embedded systems (e.g., smart cards, IoT devices)

<bdi>●</bdi>Mobile applications for feature phones

<bdi>●</bdi>Consumer electronics

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Comparison Table

| Feature | Java SE (JSE) | Java EE (JEE) | Java ME (JME) |
| Primary Use | General-purpose apps | Enterprise and web apps | Embedded and mobile apps |
| Target Platforms | PCs, desktops, servers | Servers, distributed systems | Resource-constrained devices |
| APIs | Core APIs | Advanced APIs for web/enterprise | Device-specific APIs |
| Examples | Desktop GUIs, CLI apps | Web apps, cloud systems | IoT devices, feature phones |

By dividing Java into these editions, developers can choose the tools and libraries best suited for their application's requirements.

## Java Development Environment

To start developing Java applications, you need a properly set up environment that includes tools for writing, compiling, debugging, and running Java programs. Here are the key components of a Java development environment:

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Java Development Kit (JDK)

The Java Development Kit (JDK) is the core component for Java development. It provides tools and libraries needed for building, debugging, and running Java applications.

#### Key Components of JDK:

<bdi>●</bdi>Compiler (javac): Converts Java source code into bytecode.

<bdi>●</bdi>Java Runtime Environment (JRE): Executes Java programs, including the Java Virtual Machine (JVM).

<bdi>●</bdi>Libraries: Predefined classes and APIs for common tasks.

<bdi>●</bdi>Tools:

<bdi>○</bdi>java: Runs Java bytecode.

<bdi>○</bdi>javadoc: Generates documentation from Java source code.

<bdi>○</bdi>jdb: A Java debugger.

<bdi>○</bdi>jar: A tool for creating and managing JAR files.

#### Download:

<bdi>●</bdi>Download the latest JDK version from Oracle's Java Downloads or OpenJDK.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Integrated Development Environment (IDE)

An IDE simplifies Java development by integrating tools for coding, debugging, and testing into a single application.

#### Popular Java IDEs:

<bdi>●</bdi>IntelliJ IDEA (Community Edition is free)

<bdi>●</bdi>Eclipse IDE

<bdi>●</bdi>NetBeans

<bdi>●</bdi>BlueJ (for beginners)

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Setting Up the Environment

#### Steps to Install and Configure:

1.  Download and Install JDK:

<bdi>○</bdi>Choose the appropriate JDK version for your operating system.

<bdi>○</bdi>Follow the installation instructions provided.

1.  Set Environment Variables:

<bdi>○</bdi>On Windows:

<bdi>■</bdi>Add the JDK bin directory to the PATH variable.

<bdi>■</bdi>Example: C:\Program Files\Java\jdk-<version>\bin

<bdi>○</bdi>On macOS/Linux:

<bdi>■</bdi>Add the JDK path to your shell configuration file (.bashrc, .zshrc, or .bash_profile).

<bdi>■</bdi>Example: export PATH=$PATH:/usr/lib/jvm/java-<version>/bin

1.  Verify Installation:

Open a terminal/command prompt and type: java -version

<bdi>○</bdi>It should display the installed Java version.

1.  Choose and Install an IDE:

<bdi>○</bdi>Download and install your preferred IDE.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Workflow in a Java Development Environment

#### Step-by-Step Process:

1.  Write Code:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use an IDE or a text editor to create .java files.

Example: public class HelloWorld {

public static void main(String[] args) {

System.out.println("Hello, World!");

}

}

1.  Compile Code:

<bdi>○</bdi>Compile using the command line or IDE.

Command: javac HelloWorld.java

<bdi>○</bdi>This generates HelloWorld.class (bytecode).

1.  Run Code:

Execute the program: java HelloWorld

Output: Hello, World!

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Optional Tools for Java Development

<bdi>●</bdi>Build Tools:

<bdi>○</bdi>Maven: For managing dependencies and building projects.

<bdi>○</bdi>Gradle: For modern build automation.

<bdi>●</bdi>Version Control:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use Git with platforms like GitHub, GitLab, or Bitbucket.

<bdi>●</bdi>Testing Frameworks:

<bdi>○</bdi>JUnit: For unit testing.

<bdi>○</bdi>Mockito: For mocking objects in tests.

<bdi>●</bdi>Code Quality Tools:

<bdi>○</bdi>Checkstyle: For enforcing coding standards.

<bdi>○</bdi>FindBugs/SpotBugs: For detecting potential bugs.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

A properly set up development environment ensures an efficient and seamless Java development experience.

## Installing and Setting Up JDK and IDEs

Here’s a step-by-step guide to installing the Java Development Kit (JDK) and setting up an Integrated Development Environment (IDE) for Java development.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

## Installing the JDK

### Step 1: Download the JDK

1.  Visit the Oracle JDK Downloads page or OpenJDK. Search in your web browser.

1.  Select the appropriate version of the JDK for your operating system (Windows, macOS, or Linux).

1.  Download the installer.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Step 2: Install the JDK

<bdi>●</bdi>Windows:

1.  Run the downloaded .exe file.

1.  Follow the installation wizard.

1.  By default, the JDK is installed in C:\Program Files\Java\jdk-<version>.

<bdi>●</bdi>macOS:

1.  Run the downloaded .dmg file.

1.  Follow the prompts to install the JDK.

1.  The JDK is installed in /Library/Java/JavaVirtualMachines.

<bdi>●</bdi>Linux:

1.  Use a package manager (e.g., apt or yum) or download and extract the .tar.gz file.

Example for Debian-based systems: sudo apt update

sudo apt install openjdk-<version>-jdk

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Step 3: Configure Environment Variables

To use Java commands from the terminal or command prompt, you need to set the PATH environment variable.

#### Windows:

1.  Search for "Environment Variables" in the Start Menu and open it.

1.  Under System Variables, find and edit the Path variable.

1.  Add the JDK bin folder path (e.g., C:\Program Files\Java\jdk-<version>\bin).

1.  Save and close.

#### macOS/Linux:

Open the terminal and edit your shell configuration file: nano ~/.bashrc # or ~/.zshrc

Add the following line: export PATH=$PATH:/path/to/jdk/bin

Save the file and reload the terminal: source ~/.bashrc

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Step 4: Verify Installation

Run the following commands to verify your installation:

java -version

javac -version

You should see the installed version of Java and the compiler.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

## Setting Up an IDE

### Popular Java IDEs

<bdi>●</bdi>IntelliJ IDEA (Community Edition is free)

<bdi>●</bdi>Eclipse IDE

## Writing Your First Java Program

To get started with Java programming, we’ll create a simple program that prints "Hello, World!" to the console. Here's a step-by-step guide:

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Step 1: Create the Source Code

Write the Java code in a text editor or an IDE. Save the file with a .java extension.

#### Code Example:

public class HelloWorld {

public static void main(String[] args) {

System.out.println("Hello, World!");

}

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Step 2: Compile the Program

Java programs need to be compiled into bytecode before they can run. Use the javac command to compile the program.

#### Command:

javac HelloWorld.java

This will generate a file named HelloWorld.class in the same directory. This file contains the bytecode, which the JVM can execute.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Step 3: Run the Program

Use the java command to execute the compiled program.

#### Command:

java HelloWorld

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Expected Output

Hello, World!

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Detailed Explanation

1.  public class HelloWorld:

<bdi>○</bdi>Defines a class named HelloWorld. The class name must match the filename.

1.  public static void main(String[] args):

<bdi>○</bdi>The entry point of any Java program.

<bdi>○</bdi>public: Indicates that the method is accessible from outside the class.

<bdi>○</bdi>static: Means the method can be called without creating an instance of the class.

<bdi>○</bdi>void: Specifies that the method does not return any value.

<bdi>○</bdi>String[] args: Accepts command-line arguments.

1.  System.out.println("Hello, World!");:

<bdi>○</bdi>Prints the text "Hello, World!" followed by a new line.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Using an IDE

If you're using an IDE (e.g., IntelliJ IDEA, Eclipse, or NetBeans), the process is even simpler:

1.  Create a New Project:

<bdi>○</bdi>Open the IDE and create a new Java project.

1.  Add a Class:

<bdi>○</bdi>Create a new class named HelloWorld and paste the code above.

1.  Run the Program:

<bdi>○</bdi>Click the "Run" button in the IDE.

The IDE will handle the compilation and execution steps automatically.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

With this simple program, you've successfully written, compiled, and executed your first Java program.

## Java Compilation Process

The Java compilation process is the series of steps that transform Java source code into bytecode, which can be executed by the Java Virtual Machine (JVM). Here's a detailed breakdown of the process:

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Stages of the Java Compilation Process

#### 1\. Writing the Source Code

<bdi>●</bdi>The programmer writes Java code in a text editor or an IDE.

<bdi>●</bdi>The source file is saved with a .java extension.

Example: public class HelloWorld {

public static void main(String[] args) {

System.out.println("Hello, World!");

}

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### 2\. Compilation

The source code is compiled into bytecode using the Java compiler (javac).

Command:

javac HelloWorld.java

<bdi>●</bdi>Input: HelloWorld.java (source file).

<bdi>●</bdi>Output: HelloWorld.class (bytecode file).

##### What Happens During Compilation?

<bdi>●</bdi>Lexical Analysis:

<bdi>○</bdi>The source code is tokenized (e.g., keywords, identifiers, operators).

<bdi>○</bdi>Syntax errors, such as missing semicolons or unmatched braces, are detected.

<bdi>●</bdi>Parsing and Semantic Analysis:

<bdi>○</bdi>The tokens are arranged into a syntax tree.

<bdi>○</bdi>Semantic rules (e.g., variable declarations, type compatibility) are checked.

<bdi>●</bdi>Intermediate Code Generation:

<bdi>○</bdi>The syntax tree is converted into intermediate bytecode.

<bdi>●</bdi>Optimization:

<bdi>○</bdi>Some optimizations are applied to improve the efficiency of the generated bytecode.

<bdi>●</bdi>Bytecode Generation:

<bdi>○</bdi>The compiler generates a .class file containing platform-independent bytecode.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### 3\. Loading the Bytecode

The JVM loads the .class file into memory.

<bdi>●</bdi>Class Loader:

<bdi>○</bdi>Loads bytecode from the file system or network into memory.

<bdi>○</bdi>Verifies the correctness and security of the bytecode.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### 4\. Execution by JVM

The JVM interprets or compiles the bytecode into machine code and executes it.

##### Execution Modes:

<bdi>●</bdi>Interpretation:

<bdi>○</bdi>The JVM reads and executes bytecode instructions one by one.

<bdi>●</bdi>Just-In-Time (JIT) Compilation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Frequently executed parts of the bytecode are compiled into native machine code for faster performance.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Diagram of the Compilation Process

Source Code (.java)

↓

Compiler (javac)

↓

Bytecode (.class)

↓

JVM Class Loader

↓

Bytecode Verification

↓

Just-In-Time Compilation

↓

Machine Code Execution

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Components Involved in Compilation

1.  Java Compiler (javac):

<bdi>○</bdi>Converts Java source code into bytecode.

1.  Bytecode:

<bdi>○</bdi>An intermediate, platform-independent representation of the program.

<bdi>○</bdi>Stored in .class files.

1.  Java Virtual Machine (JVM):

<bdi>○</bdi>Executes the bytecode on the host machine.

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensures platform independence by providing the necessary runtime environment.

1.  Just-In-Time Compiler (JIT):

<bdi>○</bdi>Converts bytecode to machine code at runtime for improved performance.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Advantages of Java's Compilation Process

1.  Platform Independence:

<bdi>○</bdi>Bytecode can run on any system with a JVM, ensuring Write Once, Run Anywhere (WORA).

1.  Error Detection:

<bdi>○</bdi>Compilation detects many errors (e.g., syntax and type errors) before runtime.

1.  Optimization:

<bdi>○</bdi>The compiler and JIT apply various optimizations to improve runtime efficiency.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Example: Compilation in Action

#### Source Code (HelloWorld.java):

public class HelloWorld {

public static void main(String[] args) {

System.out.println("Hello, World!");

}

}

#### Compilation:

javac HelloWorld.java

<bdi>●</bdi>Creates HelloWorld.class.

#### Execution:

java HelloWorld

Output:

Hello, World!

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

The Java compilation process ensures that Java programs are platform-independent, secure, and efficient. By compiling code into bytecode and executing it via the JVM, Java provides both flexibility and robustness for software development.

# Basics of Java Programming

## Java Syntax and Structure

Java's syntax and structure define how programs are written and organized. Java's design emphasizes readability, consistency, and simplicity, making it one of the most widely used programming languages. Below is a detailed explanation of Java's structure with an example, and explanations for both the code and output.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Basic Structure of a Java Program

// This is a single-line comment

/*

* This is a multi-line comment

* Comments are ignored by the compiler.

*/

// Class Declaration

public class HelloWorld {

// Main method: the entry point of any Java application

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Print a message to the console

System.out.println("Hello, Java!");

}

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Detailed Breakdown of the Code

1.  Comments:

<bdi>○</bdi>Single-line (//) and multi-line (/* */) comments are ignored by the compiler.

<bdi>○</bdi>Useful for adding notes or explanations to the code.

1.  Class Declaration:

<bdi>○</bdi>public class HelloWorld { }

<bdi>○</bdi>Every Java program must have at least one class.

<bdi>○</bdi>The filename (HelloWorld.java) must match the class name (HelloWorld).

1.  Main Method:

<bdi>○</bdi>public static void main(String[] args)

<bdi>○</bdi>This method is the starting point for program execution.

<bdi>○</bdi>public: Makes the method accessible from outside the class.

<bdi>○</bdi>static: Allows the method to run without creating an instance of the class.

<bdi>○</bdi>void: Indicates the method does not return any value.

<bdi>○</bdi>String[] args: An array to store command-line arguments (optional).

1.  Statements:

<bdi>○</bdi>Code inside the main method is executed line by line.

<bdi>○</bdi>Example: System.out.println("Hello, Java!");

<bdi>■</bdi>Prints the specified string ("Hello, Java!") to the console.

<bdi>■</bdi>System.out refers to the standard output stream.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Execution Process

1.  Write the Code: Save the file as HelloWorld.java.

1.  Compile: Use javac HelloWorld.java to generate HelloWorld.class.

1.  Run: Use java HelloWorld to execute the program.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Explanation of Output

#### Code:

public class HelloWorld {

public static void main(String[] args) {

System.out.println("Hello, Java!");

}

}

#### Output:

Hello, Java!

Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>System.out.println writes "Hello, Java!" to the console, followed by a newline.

<bdi>●</bdi>The program terminates after executing the main method.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### More Detailed Syntax Features

#### 1\. Data Types

Java supports several data types:

| Type | Description | Example |
| int | Integer numbers | int age = 25; |
| double | Decimal numbers | double pi = 3.14; |
| char | Single character | char grade = 'A'; |
| boolean | Logical values (true/false) | boolean isJavaFun = true; |
| String | Sequence of characters | String name = "Java"; |

#### Example:

public class DataTypeExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int age = 25;

double height = 5.9;

char grade = 'A';

boolean isJavaFun = true;

String message = "Learning Java";

System.out.println("Age: " + age);

System.out.println("Height: " + height);

System.out.println("Grade: " + grade);

System.out.println("Is Java fun? " + isJavaFun);

System.out.println("Message: " + message);

}

}

#### Output:

Age: 25

Height: 5.9

Grade: A

Is Java fun? true

Message: Learning Java

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### 2\. Conditional Statements

Control the flow of the program.

Example:

public class ConditionExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int number = 10;

if (number > 0) {

System.out.println("Positive number");

} else {

System.out.println("Non-positive number");

}

}

}

Output:

Positive number

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### 3\. Loops

Used for repetition.

Example (For Loop):

public class LoopExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

System.out.println("Count: " + i);

}

}

}

Output:

Count: 1

Count: 2

Count: 3

Count: 4

Count: 5

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

Java's syntax is intuitive and follows a structured format. It ensures clarity with:

<bdi>●</bdi>Classes as containers for methods and data.

<bdi>●</bdi>The main method as the starting point.

<bdi>●</bdi>Statements and expressions inside methods.

Each section of the program builds towards specific functionality, making Java both simple and powerful for building robust applications.

## Data Types and Variables in Java

Java is a statically typed language, which means every variable must have a declared data type. Data types specify the kind of data a variable can hold, such as integers, decimals, characters, or strings.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Categories of Data Types

1.  Primitive Data Types:

<bdi>○</bdi>Built into Java.

<bdi>○</bdi>Include types like int, double, char, boolean, etc.

1.  Reference Data Types:

<bdi>○</bdi>Represent objects and arrays.

<bdi>○</bdi>Include String, custom classes, and arrays.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Primitive Data Types

| Type | Size | Default Value | Description | Example |
| byte | 1 byte | 0 | Stores integers from -128 to 127. | byte a = 10; |
| short | 2 bytes | 0 | Stores integers from -32,768 to 32,767. | short b = 2000; |
| int | 4 bytes | 0 | Stores integers from -2³¹ to 2³¹-1. | int num = 100; |
| long | 8 bytes | 0L | Stores large integers. | long num = 100000L; |
| float | 4 bytes | 0.0f | Stores fractional numbers, single precision. | float pi = 3.14f; |
| double | 8 bytes | 0.0 | Stores fractional numbers, double precision. | double e = 2.718; |
| char | 2 bytes | '\u0000' | Stores a single character. | char c = 'A'; |
| boolean | 1 bit | false | Stores true/false values. | boolean isJavaFun = true; |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Reference Data Types

<bdi>●</bdi>Strings:

<bdi>○</bdi>A sequence of characters.

<bdi>○</bdi>Example: String name = "Java";

<bdi>●</bdi>Arrays:

<bdi>○</bdi>Collections of values of the same type.

<bdi>○</bdi>Example: int[] numbers = {1, 2, 3};

<bdi>●</bdi>Objects:

<bdi>○</bdi>Instances of classes.

<bdi>○</bdi>Example: Person p = new Person();

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Variables in Java

Variables are named containers for storing data. A variable must:

1.  Have a name (identifier).

1.  Be assigned a data type.

1.  Optionally, be assigned an initial value.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Declaring Variables

#### Syntax:

dataType variableName = value;

#### Example:

int age = 25; // Declares an integer variable named "age" with a value of 25.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Types of Variables

1.  Local Variables:

<bdi>○</bdi>Declared inside a method or block.

<bdi>○</bdi>Only accessible within the method or block.

1.  Instance Variables:

<bdi>○</bdi>Declared inside a class but outside any method.

<bdi>○</bdi>Belong to an instance of the class.

1.  Static Variables:

<bdi>○</bdi>Declared with the static keyword.

<bdi>○</bdi>Belong to the class, not individual objects.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Example Code with Explanation

public class DataTypeExample {

// Instance variable

static String course = "Java Programming";

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Local variables

int age = 20;

double gpa = 3.8;

char grade = 'A';

boolean isEnrolled = true;

// Printing variables

System.out.println("Course: " + course);

System.out.println("Age: " + age);

System.out.println("GPA: " + gpa);

System.out.println("Grade: " + grade);

System.out.println("Enrolled: " + isEnrolled);

}

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Explanation of the Code

1.  Static Variable:

<bdi>○</bdi>static String course = "Java Programming";

<bdi>■</bdi>This variable belongs to the class DataTypeExample.

<bdi>■</bdi>It is accessible without creating an instance of the class.

1.  Local Variables:

<bdi>○</bdi>int age = 20;, double gpa = 3.8;, etc., are local to the main method.

<bdi>○</bdi>They are created when the method is called and destroyed when the method finishes.

1.  System.out.println Statements:

<bdi>○</bdi>Concatenate strings and variables using the + operator.

<bdi>○</bdi>Display the values of the variables in the console.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Output

Course: Java Programming

Age: 20

GPA: 3.8

Grade: A

Enrolled: true

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Explanation of Output

1.  Course: Java Programming:

<bdi>○</bdi>The static variable course holds the value "Java Programming" and is printed first.

1.  Age: 20:

<bdi>○</bdi>The local variable age is assigned the value 20 and displayed.

1.  GPA: 3.8:

<bdi>○</bdi>The gpa variable is a double holding the value 3.8.

1.  Grade: A:

<bdi>○</bdi>The grade variable stores the character 'A'.

1.  Enrolled: true:

<bdi>○</bdi>The boolean variable isEnrolled evaluates to true and is displayed.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Additional Concepts

#### Default Values

<bdi>●</bdi>Local variables do not have default values. They must be initialized before use.

<bdi>●</bdi>Instance variables have default values based on their data type:

<bdi>○</bdi>int: 0

<bdi>○</bdi>boolean: false

<bdi>○</bdi>String (or other objects): null

#### Type Casting

<bdi>●</bdi>Java allows type casting between compatible types.

Example:

int x = 10;

double y = x; // Implicit casting

System.out.println(y); // Output: 10.0

double z = 9.7;

int w = (int) z; // Explicit casting

System.out.println(w); // Output: 9

## Type Casting

Type Casting refers to the process of converting a variable from one data type to another. Java supports automatic and manual type casting, depending on the compatibility of the data types involved.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Types of Type Casting

1.  Implicit Type Casting (Widening Casting):

<bdi>○</bdi>Automatically performed by Java.

<bdi>○</bdi>Happens when a smaller data type is converted to a larger data type (e.g., int to double).

<bdi>○</bdi>No data loss occurs because the target type has a larger size.

1.  Explicit Type Casting (Narrowing Casting):

<bdi>○</bdi>Manually specified by the programmer.

<bdi>○</bdi>Happens when a larger data type is converted to a smaller data type (e.g., double to int).

<bdi>○</bdi>May cause data loss or precision loss.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Widening Casting (Implicit)

#### Code Example:

public class WideningCasting {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int num = 100; // int is a 4-byte data type

double doubleNum = num; // Automatic conversion to 8-byte double

System.out.println("Integer: " + num);

System.out.println("Double: " + doubleNum);

}

}

#### Explanation of Code:

1.  int num = 100;:

<bdi>○</bdi>Declares an integer variable num with a value of 100.

1.  double doubleNum = num;:

<bdi>○</bdi>Implicitly casts the int value to a double type.

<bdi>○</bdi>No precision loss occurs.

#### Output:

Integer: 100

Double: 100.0

Explanation of Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>The integer 100 is converted to 100.0 as a double with no loss of data.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Narrowing Casting (Explicit)

#### Code Example:

public class NarrowingCasting {

public static void main(String[] args) {

double pi = 3.14159; // double is an 8-byte data type

int truncatedPi = (int) pi; // Manual conversion to 4-byte int

System.out.println("Double: " + pi);

System.out.println("Integer: " + truncatedPi);

}

}

#### Explanation of Code:

1.  double pi = 3.14159;:

<bdi>○</bdi>Declares a double variable pi with a value of 3.14159.

1.  int truncatedPi = (int) pi;:

<bdi>○</bdi>Explicitly casts the double value to an int.

<bdi>○</bdi>Fractional part (0.14159) is discarded, leaving only the integer part.

#### Output:

Double: 3.14159

Integer: 3

Explanation of Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>The double value 3.14159 is truncated to the integer 3, losing its fractional part.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Type Casting Between Non-Numeric Data Types

Java allows type casting between numeric and character types because char is internally represented by an integer (Unicode value).

#### Code Example:

public class CharCasting {

public static void main(String[] args) {

char letter = 'A'; // Character 'A'

int ascii = letter; // Implicit conversion to int

char nextLetter = (char) (letter + 1); // Explicit conversion back to char

System.out.println("Character: " + letter);

System.out.println("ASCII Value: " + ascii);

System.out.println("Next Character: " + nextLetter);

}

}

#### Explanation of Code:

1.  char letter = 'A';:

<bdi>○</bdi>Declares a char variable letter with a value 'A'.

1.  int ascii = letter;:

<bdi>○</bdi>Implicitly casts the char to its ASCII integer value (65).

1.  char nextLetter = (char) (letter + 1);:

<bdi>○</bdi>Adds 1 to the ASCII value of letter and casts the result back to char.

#### Output:

Character: A

ASCII Value: 65

Next Character: B

Explanation of Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>'A' is converted to its ASCII value 65.

<bdi>●</bdi>Adding 1 results in 66, which corresponds to the character 'B'.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Casting Incompatible Types

Java does not allow direct casting between incompatible types (e.g., String to int). However, conversions can be achieved using helper methods.

#### Code Example:

public class IncompatibleCasting {

public static void main(String[] args) {

String number = "100";

int parsedNumber = Integer.parseInt(number); // Converts String to int

System.out.println("String: " + number);

System.out.println("Integer: " + parsedNumber);

}

}

#### Explanation of Code:

1.  String number = "100";:

<bdi>○</bdi>Declares a String variable containing "100".

1.  int parsedNumber = Integer.parseInt(number);:

<bdi>○</bdi>Converts the String into an int using the Integer.parseInt method.

#### Output:

String: 100

Integer: 100

Explanation of Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>The string "100" is parsed into the integer 100.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Casting Arrays

Arrays cannot be cast directly, but their elements can be cast individually if compatible.

#### Code Example:

public class ArrayCasting {

public static void main(String[] args) {

double[] numbers = {1.1, 2.2, 3.3};

int[] integers = new int[numbers.length];

for (int i = 0; i < numbers.length; i++) {

integers[i] = (int) numbers[i]; // Explicit casting

}

for (int num : integers) {

System.out.println(num);

}

}

}

#### Output:

1

2

3

Explanation of Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>Each double value in the numbers array is truncated to its integer part and stored in the integers array.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Important Notes

1.  Loss of Precision:

<bdi>○</bdi>Narrowing conversions may result in loss of data (e.g., truncation of decimals).

1.  Incompatible Casts:

<bdi>○</bdi>Casting between unrelated types (e.g., String to int) requires explicit parsing methods.

1.  Safe Conversions:

<bdi>○</bdi>Widening conversions are safe because the larger type can fully represent the smaller type's value.

## Operators and Expressions

Operators are symbols that perform operations on variables and values, while expressions combine variables, constants, and operators to produce a result. Understanding operators and expressions is essential for performing calculations, making decisions, and building logic in Java programs.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Categories of Operators

1.  Arithmetic Operators

1.  Relational (Comparison) Operators

1.  Logical Operators

1.  Bitwise Operators

1.  Assignment Operators

1.  Unary Operators

1.  Ternary (Conditional) Operator

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Arithmetic Operators

Arithmetic operators perform basic mathematical operations.

| Operator | Description | Example | Result |
| + | Addition | 5 + 3 | 8 |
| - | Subtraction | 5 - 3 | 2 |
| * | Multiplication | 5 * 3 | 15 |
| / | Division | 5 / 2 | 2 (integer division) |
| % | Modulus (Remainder) | 5 % 2 | 1 |

#### Code Example:

public class ArithmeticExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int a = 10, b = 3;

System.out.println("Addition: " + (a + b));

System.out.println("Subtraction: " + (a - b));

System.out.println("Multiplication: " + (a * b));

System.out.println("Division: " + (a / b));

System.out.println("Modulus: " + (a % b));

}

}

#### Output:

Addition: 13

Subtraction: 7

Multiplication: 30

Division: 3

Modulus: 1

Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>Each arithmetic operation is performed using the operators and produces results based on the input values.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Relational (Comparison) Operators

Relational operators compare two values and return a boolean result (true or false).

| Operator | Description | Example | Result |
| == | Equal to | 5 == 5 | true |
| != | Not equal to | 5 != 3 | true |
| > | Greater than | 5 > 3 | true |
| < | Less than | 5 < 3 | false |
| >= | Greater than or equal | 5 >= 5 | true |
| <= | Less than or equal | 3 <= 5 | true |

#### Code Example:

public class RelationalExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int x = 10, y = 20;

System.out.println("x == y: " + (x == y));

System.out.println("x != y: " + (x != y));

System.out.println("x > y: " + (x > y));

System.out.println("x < y: " + (x < y));

System.out.println("x >= y: " + (x >= y));

System.out.println("x <= y: " + (x <= y));

}

}

#### Output:

x == y: false

x != y: true

x > y: false

x < y: true

x >= y: false

x <= y: true

Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>Relational operators compare x and y, returning true or false depending on the comparison.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Logical Operators

Logical operators are used to combine multiple boolean expressions.

| Operator | Description | Example | Result |
| && | Logical AND (both true) | true && false | false |
| ` |  | ` | Logical OR (at least one true) |
| ! | Logical NOT (negation) | !true | false |

#### Code Example:

public class LogicalExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

boolean a = true, b = false;

System.out.println("a && b: " + (a && b));

System.out.println("a || b: " + (a || b));

System.out.println("!a: " + (!a));

}

}

#### Output:

a && b: false

a || b: true

!a: false

Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>Logical AND returns true only if both operands are true.

<bdi>●</bdi>Logical OR returns true if at least one operand is true.

<bdi>●</bdi>Logical NOT negates the value of the operand.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Bitwise Operators

Bitwise operators perform operations at the bit level.

| Operator | Description | Example | Result |
| & | Bitwise AND | 5 & 3 | 1 |
| ` | ` | Bitwise OR | `5 |
| ^ | Bitwise XOR | 5 ^ 3 | 6 |
| ~ | Bitwise Complement | ~5 | -6 |
| << | Left shift | 5 << 1 | 10 |
| >> | Right shift | 5 >> 1 | 2 |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Assignment Operators

Assignment operators assign values to variables.

| Operator | Example | Equivalent To |
| = | x = 5 | Assign value |
| += | x += 3 | x = x + 3 |
| -= | x -= 3 | x = x - 3 |
| *= | x *= 3 | x = x * 3 |
| /= | x /= 3 | x = x / 3 |
| %= | x %= 3 | x = x % 3 |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Unary Operators

Unary operators operate on a single operand.

| Operator | Description | Example | Result |
| + | Unary plus (positive) | +5 | 5 |
| - | Unary minus (negative) | -5 | -5 |
| ++ | Increment (add 1) | x++ | x + 1 |
| -- | Decrement (subtract 1) | x-- | x - 1 |
| ! | Logical NOT | !true | false |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 7\. Ternary (Conditional) Operator

The ternary operator is a shorthand for if-else.

Syntax:

condition ? valueIfTrue : valueIfFalse

#### Code Example:

public class TernaryExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int a = 10, b = 20;

int max = (a > b) ? a : b;

System.out.println("Max: " + max);

}

}

#### Output:

Max: 20

Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>If a > b is true, max is assigned a. Otherwise, b is assigned.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Expressions

An expression is a combination of variables, constants, and operators that produce a value.

#### Example:

int result = (10 + 5) * 3; // Expression

Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>(10 + 5) evaluates to 15.

<bdi>●</bdi>15 * 3 evaluates to 45.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Practice Problem

Write a program to demonstrate the use of arithmetic, relational, and logical operators in one expression.

#### Code Example:

public class ExpressionExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int a = 10, b = 20, c = 5;

boolean result = (a + b > c) && (a < b);

System.out.println("Result: " + result);

}

}

#### Output:

Result: true

Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>(a + b > c) evaluates to true (30 > 5).

<bdi>●</bdi>(a < b) evaluates to true.

<bdi>●</bdi>Logical AND (&&) combines both, resulting in true.

## Decision-Making Statements

Decision-making statements in Java are used to evaluate conditions and execute specific blocks of code based on the outcome. These statements enable conditional logic, allowing programs to adapt to different scenarios.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Types of Decision-Making Statements

1.  if Statement

1.  if-else Statement

1.  if-else-if Ladder

1.  switch Statement

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. if Statement

The if statement executes a block of code only if the specified condition evaluates to true.

#### Syntax:

if (condition) {

// Code to execute if condition is true

}

#### Code Example:

public class IfExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int number = 10;

if (number > 5) {

System.out.println("Number is greater than 5");

}

}

}

#### Output:

Number is greater than 5

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The condition number > 5 evaluates to true.

<bdi>●</bdi>As a result, the System.out.println statement inside the if block executes.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. if-else Statement

The if-else statement provides an alternative path if the condition evaluates to false.

#### Syntax:

if (condition) {

// Code to execute if condition is true

} else {

// Code to execute if condition is false

}

#### Code Example:

public class IfElseExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int number = 3;

if (number > 5) {

System.out.println("Number is greater than 5");

} else {

System.out.println("Number is less than or equal to 5");

}

}

}

#### Output:

Number is less than or equal to 5

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The condition number > 5 evaluates to false.

<bdi>●</bdi>The code in the else block executes.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. if-else-if Ladder

The if-else-if ladder is used when multiple conditions need to be checked sequentially. If one condition is true, its corresponding block executes, and the rest are skipped.

#### Syntax:

if (condition1) {

// Code if condition1 is true

} else if (condition2) {

// Code if condition2 is true

} else {

// Code if all conditions are false

}

#### Code Example:

public class IfElseIfExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int marks = 85;

if (marks >= 90) {

System.out.println("Grade: A+");

} else if (marks >= 80) {

System.out.println("Grade: A");

} else if (marks >= 70) {

System.out.println("Grade: B");

} else {

System.out.println("Grade: C");

}

}

}

#### Output:

Grade: A

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The condition marks >= 90 evaluates to false.

<bdi>●</bdi>The condition marks >= 80 evaluates to true, so System.out.println("Grade: A") executes.

<bdi>●</bdi>Remaining conditions are skipped.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. switch Statement

The switch statement evaluates an expression and matches its value against multiple case labels. When a match is found, the code in the corresponding case executes.

#### Syntax:

switch (expression) {

case value1:

// Code for value1

break;

case value2:

// Code for value2

break;

default:

// Default code if no cases match

}

#### Code Example:

public class SwitchExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int day = 3;

switch (day) {

case 1:

System.out.println("Monday");

break;

case 2:

System.out.println("Tuesday");

break;

case 3:

System.out.println("Wednesday");

break;

default:

System.out.println("Invalid day");

}

}

}

#### Output:

Wednesday

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The value of day is 3.

<bdi>●</bdi>The case 3 matches, and System.out.println("Wednesday") executes.

<bdi>●</bdi>The break statement prevents the execution of subsequent cases.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Nested if Statements

An if statement inside another if statement is called a nested if.

#### Code Example:

public class NestedIfExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int age = 25;

boolean isCitizen = true;

if (age >= 18) {

if (isCitizen) {

System.out.println("Eligible to vote");

} else {

System.out.println("Not a citizen");

}

} else {

System.out.println("Not eligible to vote");

}

}

}

#### Output:

Eligible to vote

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The outer condition age >= 18 is true.

<bdi>●</bdi>The inner condition isCitizen is also true, so System.out.println("Eligible to vote") executes.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Common Scenarios

#### Using switch with char

public class SwitchCharExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

char grade = 'A';

switch (grade) {

case 'A':

System.out.println("Excellent");

break;

case 'B':

System.out.println("Good");

break;

default:

System.out.println("Needs Improvement");

}

}

}

#### Output:

Excellent

Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The grade is 'A', so the case 'A' matches, and "Excellent" is printed.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Default Case

The default block in a switch statement is executed if no case matches.

#### Code Example:

public class DefaultCaseExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int choice = 5;

switch (choice) {

case 1:

System.out.println("Option 1");

break;

case 2:

System.out.println("Option 2");

break;

default:

System.out.println("Invalid option");

}

}

}

#### Output:

Invalid option

Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The value 5 does not match any cases, so the default block executes.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Points to Remember

1.  if-else vs. switch:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use if-else for range-based conditions.

<bdi>○</bdi>Use switch for discrete value matching.

1.  break Statement:

<bdi>○</bdi>Prevents the execution of subsequent cases in a switch.

1.  Logical Operators:

<bdi>○</bdi>Combine multiple conditions using && (AND), || (OR), and ! (NOT).

1.  Default Execution:

<bdi>○</bdi>In both if-else and switch, ensure there is a fallback logic for unmatched conditions.

## Loops

Loops are used to execute a block of code repeatedly until a specified condition is met. Java provides several types of loops for handling repetitive tasks efficiently.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Types of Loops in Java

1.  for Loop

1.  Enhanced for Loop (for-each loop)

1.  while Loop

1.  do-while Loop

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. for Loop

A for loop is used when the number of iterations is known beforehand. It consists of three parts:

<bdi>●</bdi>Initialization: Sets the loop control variable.

<bdi>●</bdi>Condition: Evaluates whether the loop should continue.

<bdi>●</bdi>Update: Modifies the control variable after each iteration.

#### Syntax:

for (initialization; condition; update) {

// Code to execute

}

#### Code Example:

public class ForLoopExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

System.out.println("Count: " + i);

}

}

}

#### Output:

Count: 1

Count: 2

Count: 3

Count: 4

Count: 5

#### Explanation:

1.  Initialization: int i = 1 sets i to 1.

1.  Condition: i <= 5 ensures the loop runs while i is less than or equal to 5.

1.  Update: i++ increments i by 1 after each iteration.

1.  The loop executes the System.out.println statement five times, printing the values of i.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Enhanced for Loop (for-each Loop)

The enhanced for loop is used to iterate over arrays or collections.

#### Syntax:

for (type variable : collection) {

// Code to execute

}

#### Code Example:

public class ForEachExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int[] numbers = {10, 20, 30, 40, 50};

for (int num : numbers) {

System.out.println("Number: " + num);

}

}

}

#### Output:

Number: 10

Number: 20

Number: 30

Number: 40

Number: 50

#### Explanation:

1.  The for-each loop iterates through each element of the numbers array.

1.  On each iteration, the current array element is assigned to num and printed.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. while Loop

The while loop is used when the number of iterations is not known in advance and depends on a condition.

#### Syntax:

while (condition) {

// Code to execute

}

#### Code Example:

public class WhileLoopExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int i = 1;

while (i <= 5) {

System.out.println("Count: " + i);

i++;

}

}

}

#### Output:

Count: 1

Count: 2

Count: 3

Count: 4

Count: 5

#### Explanation:

1.  Initialization: int i = 1.

1.  The while loop checks the condition i <= 5.

1.  If the condition is true, the code block executes, and i is incremented by 1.

1.  The loop terminates when i > 5.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. do-while Loop

The do-while loop is similar to the while loop but guarantees that the code block is executed at least once before checking the condition.

#### Syntax:

do {

// Code to execute

} while (condition);

#### Code Example:

public class DoWhileExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int i = 6;

do {

System.out.println("Count: " + i);

i++;

} while (i <= 5);

}

}

#### Output:

Count: 6

#### Explanation:

1.  The do block executes first, printing Count: 6.

1.  After the first iteration, the condition i <= 5 is checked and evaluates to false, terminating the loop.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Nested Loops

Loops can be nested within other loops to handle multidimensional data or repeated tasks within repeated tasks.

#### Code Example:

public class NestedLoopExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

for (int i = 1; i <= 3; i++) {

for (int j = 1; j <= 2; j++) {

System.out.println("Outer: " + i + ", Inner: " + j);

}

}

}

}

#### Output:

Outer: 1, Inner: 1

Outer: 1, Inner: 2

Outer: 2, Inner: 1

Outer: 2, Inner: 2

Outer: 3, Inner: 1

Outer: 3, Inner: 2

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The outer loop runs from i = 1 to i = 3.

<bdi>●</bdi>For each iteration of the outer loop, the inner loop runs from j = 1 to j = 2.

<bdi>●</bdi>Each combination of i and j is printed.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Infinite Loops

A loop with no terminating condition results in an infinite loop.

#### Code Example:

public class InfiniteLoopExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

while (true) {

System.out.println("This is an infinite loop!");

break; // To prevent endless execution

}

}

}

#### Output:

This is an infinite loop!

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The condition true ensures the loop runs indefinitely.

<bdi>●</bdi>The break statement exits the loop after the first iteration.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 7\. Breaking and Continuing in Loops

#### break Statement:

Exits the loop immediately.

Code Example:

public class BreakExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

if (i == 3) break;

System.out.println("Count: " + i);

}

}

}

Output:

Count: 1

Count: 2

Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The loop terminates when i == 3 due to the break statement.

#### continue Statement:

Skips the current iteration and proceeds to the next.

Code Example:

public class ContinueExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

if (i == 3) continue;

System.out.println("Count: " + i);

}

}

}

Output:

Count: 1

Count: 2

Count: 4

Count: 5

Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The iteration where i == 3 is skipped, and the loop continues with the next iteration.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Choosing the Right Loop

<bdi>●</bdi>Use for loops for known iteration counts.

<bdi>●</bdi>Use while loops when the number of iterations depends on a condition.

<bdi>●</bdi>Use do-while loops if the code must execute at least once.

<bdi>●</bdi>Use nested loops for multidimensional tasks or patterns.

## Arrays

An array is a data structure that allows you to store multiple values of the same type in a single variable. Arrays are fixed in size and are stored in contiguous memory locations.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Arrays

1.  Fixed Size: Once an array is created, its size cannot be changed.

1.  Index-Based Access: Array elements are accessed using an index, starting from 0.

1.  Homogeneous Data: Arrays store elements of the same type.

1.  Multidimensional Arrays: Java supports arrays with more than one dimension, such as 2D or 3D arrays.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Types of Arrays

1.  Single-Dimensional Arrays

1.  Multi-Dimensional Arrays

1.  Jagged Arrays

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Single-Dimensional Arrays

#### Declaring and Initializing Arrays

Declaration: dataType[] arrayName;

Instantiation: arrayName = new dataType[size];

Initialization: arrayName[index] = value;

#### Code Example:

public class SingleDimensionalArray {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Declare and initialize an array

int[] numbers = {10, 20, 30, 40, 50};

// Access and print array elements

for (int i = 0; i < numbers.length; i++) {

System.out.println("Element at index " + i + ": " + numbers[i]);

}

}

}

#### Output:

Element at index 0: 10

Element at index 1: 20

Element at index 2: 30

Element at index 3: 40

Element at index 4: 50

#### Explanation:

1.  The array numbers is initialized with {10, 20, 30, 40, 50}.

1.  The for loop iterates through the array using numbers.length to determine the size.

1.  Each element is accessed using its index and printed.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Multi-Dimensional Arrays

Multi-dimensional arrays are arrays of arrays. The most commonly used type is the 2D array.

#### Declaration and Initialization:

Declaration: dataType[][] arrayName;

Instantiation: arrayName = new dataType[rows][columns];

Initialization: arrayName[row][column] = value;

#### Code Example:

public class MultiDimensionalArray {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Declare and initialize a 2D array

int[][] matrix = {

{1, 2, 3},

{4, 5, 6},

{7, 8, 9}

};

// Access and print elements

for (int i = 0; i < matrix.length; i++) {

for (int j = 0; j < matrix[i].length; j++) {

System.out.print(matrix[i][j] + " ");

}

System.out.println(); // New line for each row

}

}

}

#### Output:

1 2 3

4 5 6

7 8 9

#### Explanation:

1.  The matrix array is a 2D array initialized with three rows and three columns.

1.  The outer loop iterates through the rows, and the inner loop iterates through the columns.

1.  Each element is accessed using its [row][column] index and printed.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Jagged Arrays

A jagged array is an array of arrays where the subarrays can have different sizes.

#### Code Example:

public class JaggedArrayExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Declare a jagged array

int[][] jaggedArray = {

{1, 2},

{3, 4, 5},

{6}

};

// Access and print elements

for (int i = 0; i < jaggedArray.length; i++) {

for (int j = 0; j < jaggedArray[i].length; j++) {

System.out.print(jaggedArray[i][j] + " ");

}

System.out.println(); // New line for each subarray

}

}

}

#### Output:

1 2

3 4 5

6

#### Explanation:

1.  The jaggedArray contains subarrays of varying lengths.

1.  The nested loops access each subarray and print its elements.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Operations on Arrays

#### 1\. Finding the Length of an Array

int length = arrayName.length;

#### Code Example:

public class ArrayLengthExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int[] numbers = {10, 20, 30};

System.out.println("Array Length: " + numbers.length);

}

}

#### Output:

Array Length: 3

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The length property returns the size of the array.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### 2\. Copying an Array

System.arraycopy(source, sourcePos, dest, destPos, length);

#### Code Example:

public class ArrayCopyExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int[] original = {1, 2, 3};

int[] copy = new int[3];

System.arraycopy(original, 0, copy, 0, original.length);

for (int num : copy) {

System.out.println(num);

}

}

}

#### Output:

1

2

3

#### Explanation:

1.  The System.arraycopy method copies elements from original to copy.

1.  The loop prints the copied elements.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### 3\. Enhanced for Loop

The enhanced for loop (for-each) simplifies array iteration.

#### Code Example:

public class EnhancedForLoop {

public static void main(String[] args) {

String[] fruits = {"Apple", "Banana", "Cherry"};

for (String fruit : fruits) {

System.out.println(fruit);

}

}

}

#### Output:

Apple

Banana

Cherry

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The for-each loop iterates through each element in the fruits array and prints it.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Common Array Errors

1.  ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException:

<bdi>○</bdi>Occurs when accessing an index outside the array bounds.

Example: int[] arr = {1, 2, 3};

System.out.println(arr[5]); // Error

1.  NullPointerException:

<bdi>○</bdi>Occurs when accessing an uninitialized or null array.

Example: int[] arr = null;

System.out.println(arr[0]); // Error

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

Arrays are a fundamental part of Java programming. Key points include:

<bdi>●</bdi>Single-Dimensional Arrays: Store linear data.

<bdi>●</bdi>Multi-Dimensional Arrays: Handle tabular or grid-like data.

<bdi>●</bdi>Jagged Arrays: Allow uneven subarray sizes.

<bdi>●</bdi>Arrays can be iterated using loops (for, while, for-each).

<bdi>●</bdi>Proper indexing and bounds checks are essential to avoid runtime errors.

## Input and Output

Java provides a variety of ways to handle input and output (I/O). These mechanisms allow you to read data from the user, files, or other sources and write data to the console, files, or other destinations.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Basic Console Output

Java uses the System.out object for output to the console.

#### Example:

public class ConsoleOutput {

public static void main(String[] args) {

System.out.println("Hello, World!"); // Prints with a newline

System.out.print("This is a Java Program."); // Prints without a newline

System.out.printf("Value of Pi: %.2f", 3.14159); // Formatted output

}

}

#### Output:

Hello, World!

This is a Java Program.Value of Pi: 3.14

Explanation:

1.  System.out.println prints text and moves to the next line.

1.  System.out.print prints text without moving to the next line.

1.  System.out.printf provides formatted output using placeholders.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Basic Console Input

For reading input from the user, Java offers multiple approaches, including Scanner and BufferedReader.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Using the Scanner Class

The Scanner class is part of the java.util package and is used for reading user input from the console.

#### Steps to Use Scanner:

Import the Scanner class: import java.util.Scanner;

Create a Scanner object: Scanner sc = new Scanner(System.in);

#### Code Example:

import java.util.Scanner;

public class ScannerExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Scanner sc = new Scanner(System.in);

System.out.print("Enter your name: ");

String name = sc.nextLine(); // Reads a line of input

System.out.print("Enter your age: ");

int age = sc.nextInt(); // Reads an integer

System.out.print("Enter your height: ");

double height = sc.nextDouble(); // Reads a double

System.out.println("Name: " + name);

System.out.println("Age: " + age);

System.out.println("Height: " + height);

sc.close(); // Close the scanner to free resources

}

}

#### Input:

John

25

5.9

#### Output:

Name: John

Age: 25

Height: 5.9

Explanation:

1.  nextLine(): Reads an entire line of input, including spaces.

1.  nextInt(): Reads an integer value.

1.  nextDouble(): Reads a decimal value.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Using the BufferedReader Class

The BufferedReader class is part of the java.io package and reads input more efficiently, especially for larger data. It is often combined with the InputStreamReader class.

#### Steps to Use BufferedReader:

Import the BufferedReader and InputStreamReader classes: import java.io.BufferedReader;

import java.io.InputStreamReader;

Create a BufferedReader object: BufferedReader br = new BufferedReader(new InputStreamReader(System.in));

#### Code Example:

import java.io.BufferedReader;

import java.io.InputStreamReader;

import java.io.IOException;

public class BufferedReaderExample {

public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {

BufferedReader br = new BufferedReader(new InputStreamReader(System.in));

System.out.print("Enter your name: ");

String name = br.readLine(); // Reads a line of input

System.out.print("Enter your age: ");

int age = Integer.parseInt(br.readLine()); // Reads and converts to int

System.out.print("Enter your height: ");

double height = Double.parseDouble(br.readLine()); // Reads and converts to double

System.out.println("Name: " + name);

System.out.println("Age: " + age);

System.out.println("Height: " + height);

}

}

#### Input:

Alice

30

5.6

#### Output:

Name: Alice

Age: 30

Height: 5.6

Explanation:

1.  readLine(): Reads a line of input as a string.

1.  Integer.parseInt(): Converts the string input to an integer.

1.  Double.parseDouble(): Converts the string input to a double.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Comparison: Scanner vs BufferedReader

| Feature | Scanner | BufferedReader |
| Ease of Use | Easy to use with various methods | Requires parsing for non-string inputs |
| Performance | Slower for large inputs | Faster for large inputs |
| Input Type | Supports multiple input types | Reads only strings via readLine() |
| Thread-Safety | Not thread-safe | Thread-safe |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. File I/O (Reading and Writing Files)

Java also supports file I/O using classes like File, FileReader, and FileWriter.

#### Reading a File:

import java.io.BufferedReader;

import java.io.FileReader;

import java.io.IOException;

public class FileReadExample {

public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {

BufferedReader br = new BufferedReader(new FileReader("example.txt"));

String line;

while ((line = br.readLine()) != null) {

System.out.println(line);

}

br.close();

}

}

#### Writing to a File:

import java.io.FileWriter;

import java.io.IOException;

public class FileWriteExample {

public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {

FileWriter writer = new FileWriter("output.txt");

writer.write("This is a test file.");

writer.write("\nJava File I/O is simple!");

writer.close();

System.out.println("File written successfully.");

}

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

1.  System.out:

<bdi>○</bdi>Used for printing to the console with methods like print, println, and printf.

1.  Scanner:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ideal for simple, small-scale user input.

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides methods like nextLine, nextInt, nextDouble.

1.  BufferedReader:

<bdi>○</bdi>Better for performance-critical applications with large inputs.

<bdi>○</bdi>Requires manual parsing for data types.

1.  File I/O:

<bdi>○</bdi>Useful for reading and writing data to/from files.

# Object-Oriented Programming (OOP)

### Principles of Object-Oriented Programming (OOP)

1.  Encapsulation Encapsulation is the mechanism of restricting direct access to some of an object's components and ensuring that the internal representation of the object is hidden from the outside. This is achieved using access modifiers (private, protected, public) and providing controlled access through methods like getters and setters.

<bdi>○</bdi>Benefits:

<bdi>■</bdi>Protects object integrity by preventing unauthorized or harmful changes.

<bdi>■</bdi>Makes code easier to maintain and understand.

Example in Java: public class Person {

private String name;

private int age;

// Getter for name

public String getName() {

return name;

}

// Setter for name

public void setName(String name) {

this.name = name;

}

// Getter for age

public int getAge() {

return age;

}

// Setter for age

public void setAge(int age) {

if (age > 0) {

this.age = age;

}

}

}

1.  Inheritance Inheritance allows a class (child) to acquire properties and behaviors (methods) from another class (parent). This promotes code reuse and establishes a hierarchical relationship between classes.

<bdi>○</bdi>Key concepts:

<bdi>■</bdi>The extends keyword is used in Java.

<bdi>■</bdi>The child class can override methods from the parent class.

Example in Java: public class Animal {

public void eat() {

System.out.println("This animal eats food.");

}

}

public class Dog extends Animal {

@Override

public void eat() {

System.out.println("This dog eats bones.");

}

}

public class Main {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Animal animal = new Animal();

animal.eat();

Dog dog = new Dog();

dog.eat();

}

}

1.  Polymorphism Polymorphism means "many forms" and allows methods to perform differently based on the context or object. It can be achieved through:

<bdi>○</bdi>Method Overloading: Same method name with different parameters.

<bdi>○</bdi>Method Overriding: A subclass provides a specific implementation of a method already defined in its superclass.

Example in Java: public class Shape {

public void draw() {

System.out.println("Drawing a shape.");

}

}

public class Circle extends Shape {

@Override

public void draw() {

System.out.println("Drawing a circle.");

}

}

public class Square extends Shape {

@Override

public void draw() {

System.out.println("Drawing a square.");

}

}

public class Main {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Shape shape;

shape = new Circle();

shape.draw();

shape = new Square();

shape.draw();

}

}

1.  Abstraction Abstraction focuses on hiding implementation details while exposing only the essential features of an object. It can be achieved using:

<bdi>○</bdi>Abstract classes: Classes that cannot be instantiated and may contain abstract (unimplemented) methods.

<bdi>○</bdi>Interfaces: Completely abstract classes where all methods must be implemented by the inheriting class.

Example in Java: // Abstract Class Example

abstract class Vehicle {

abstract void start();

public void stop() {

System.out.println("Vehicle stopped.");

}

}

class Car extends Vehicle {

@Override

void start() {

System.out.println("Car starts with a key.");

}

}

public class Main {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Vehicle car = new Car();

car.start();

car.stop();

}

}

## Classes and Objects

Java is an object-oriented programming language, which means it is based on the concepts of classes and objects. These are the building blocks of Java programs.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. What is a Class?

A class is a blueprint for creating objects. It defines the properties (attributes) and behaviors (methods) that an object can have.

#### Syntax:

class ClassName {

// Fields (attributes)

// Methods (behaviors)

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. What is an Object?

An object is an instance of a class. It represents a real-world entity and has:

<bdi>●</bdi>State: Represented by fields (attributes).

<bdi>●</bdi>Behavior: Represented by methods (functions).

#### Creating an Object:

ClassName objectName = new ClassName();

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Example of a Class and Object

#### Code Example:

public class Person {

// Fields (attributes)

String name;

int age;

// Method (behavior)

void introduce() {

System.out.println("Hi, my name is " + name + " and I am " + age + " years old.");

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Creating an object of the Person class

Person person1 = new Person();

// Assigning values to fields

person1.name = "Alice";

person1.age = 25;

// Calling a method on the object

person1.introduce();

}

}

#### Output:

Hi, my name is Alice and I am 25 years old.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Explanation

1.  Class Definition:

<bdi>○</bdi>The Person class defines two fields: name and age.

<bdi>○</bdi>It has one method, introduce, which prints the person's details.

1.  Object Creation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Person person1 = new Person(); creates an object of the Person class.

1.  Field Assignment:

<bdi>○</bdi>person1.name = "Alice"; assigns the value "Alice" to the name field of the person1 object.

<bdi>○</bdi>person1.age = 25; assigns the value 25 to the age field.

1.  Method Invocation:

<bdi>○</bdi>person1.introduce(); calls the introduce method, which prints the object’s details.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Access Modifiers

Access modifiers control the visibility of class members (fields and methods).

| Modifier | Access |
| public | Accessible everywhere. |
| private | Accessible only within the class. |
| protected | Accessible within the package and subclasses. |
| (default) | Accessible within the package. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Constructors

A constructor is a special method used to initialize objects. It has the same name as the class and no return type.

#### Example:

public class Person {

String name;

int age;

// Constructor

Person(String personName, int personAge) {

name = personName;

age = personAge;

}

void introduce() {

System.out.println("Hi, my name is " + name + " and I am " + age + " years old.");

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Using the constructor to initialize the object

Person person1 = new Person("Bob", 30);

person1.introduce();

}

}

#### Output:

Hi, my name is Bob and I am 30 years old.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Explanation

1.  The constructor Person(String personName, int personAge) initializes the fields name and age when an object is created.

1.  new Person("Bob", 30) automatically calls the constructor, passing "Bob" and 30 as arguments.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Methods

Methods define the behaviors of a class. They can:

<bdi>●</bdi>Take arguments.

<bdi>●</bdi>Return values.

#### Code Example:

public class Calculator {

int add(int a, int b) {

return a + b;

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

Calculator calc = new Calculator();

int sum = calc.add(5, 7);

System.out.println("Sum: " + sum);

}

}

#### Output:

Sum: 12

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. this Keyword

The this keyword refers to the current instance of the class.

#### Code Example:

public class Person {

String name;

Person(String name) {

this.name = name; // Refers to the current object's field

}

void introduce() {

System.out.println("Hi, my name is " + this.name);

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

Person person1 = new Person("Charlie");

person1.introduce();

}

}

#### Output:

Hi, my name is Charlie

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 7\. Static Members

<bdi>●</bdi>Static Fields and Methods belong to the class rather than a specific object.

<bdi>●</bdi>Accessed using the class name.

#### Code Example:

public class MathUtils {

static int square(int x) {

return x * x;

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

System.out.println("Square of 4: " + MathUtils.square(4));

}

}

#### Output:

Square of 4: 16

Explanation:
