<bdi>●</bdi>The square method is static, so it is called using the class name MathUtils.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 8\. Encapsulation

Encapsulation restricts direct access to fields by using private access modifiers and providing getter and setter methods.

#### Code Example:

public class BankAccount {

private double balance;

// Getter

public double getBalance() {

return balance;

}

// Setter

public void deposit(double amount) {

if (amount > 0) {

balance += amount;

}

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

BankAccount account = new BankAccount();

account.deposit(500.0);

System.out.println("Balance: " + account.getBalance());

}

}

#### Output:

Balance: 500.0

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 9\. Object-Oriented Concepts in Classes

1.  Inheritance:

<bdi>○</bdi>A class can inherit fields and methods from another class using the extends keyword.

1.  Polymorphism:

<bdi>○</bdi>A class can override methods from its superclass.

1.  Abstraction:

<bdi>○</bdi>Abstract classes and interfaces define the blueprint for subclasses.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Classes define the blueprint for objects.

<bdi>●</bdi>Objects represent instances of a class.

<bdi>●</bdi>Methods define behaviors, while fields store state.

<bdi>●</bdi>Constructors initialize objects.

<bdi>●</bdi>Access modifiers (public, private, protected) control visibility.

<bdi>●</bdi>Encapsulation secures fields with getters and setters.

### Instance Variables and Methods in Java

Instance variables and instance methods are fundamental concepts in Java's object-oriented programming model. They represent the state and behavior of individual objects created from a class.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Instance Variables

Instance variables are non-static variables defined within a class but outside any method, constructor, or block. Each object of the class has its own copy of these variables.

#### Key Features of Instance Variables

<bdi>●</bdi>Declared at the class level (outside methods).

<bdi>●</bdi>Each object gets its own copy.

<bdi>●</bdi>Values are independent across objects.

<bdi>●</bdi>Can have different access modifiers (private, public, etc.).

<bdi>●</bdi>Default values:

<bdi>○</bdi>Numeric types: 0

<bdi>○</bdi>boolean: false

<bdi>○</bdi>Object references: null

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Instance Variables

public class Car {

// Instance variables

String brand;

int year;

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Creating objects of the Car class

Car car1 = new Car();

Car car2 = new Car();

// Assigning values to instance variables

car1.brand = "Toyota";

car1.year = 2020;

car2.brand = "Honda";

car2.year = 2018;

// Printing values of instance variables

System.out.println("Car 1: " + car1.brand + ", " + car1.year);

System.out.println("Car 2: " + car2.brand + ", " + car2.year);

}

}

#### Output:

Car 1: Toyota, 2020

Car 2: Honda, 2018

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation of the Code

1.  Instance Variables:

<bdi>○</bdi>brand and year are instance variables.

<bdi>○</bdi>Each Car object (car1 and car2) has its own copy of these variables.

1.  Object-Specific Values:

<bdi>○</bdi>car1.brand is assigned "Toyota" and car1.year is assigned 2020.

<bdi>○</bdi>car2.brand is assigned "Honda" and car2.year is assigned 2018.

1.  Independent State:

<bdi>○</bdi>Changing the instance variables of one object does not affect the other object.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Instance Methods

Instance methods are methods that operate on instance variables. They belong to the object (not the class) and require an object to be called.

#### Key Features of Instance Methods

<bdi>●</bdi>Can access instance variables directly.

<bdi>●</bdi>Use the this keyword to refer to the current object (optional but useful).

<bdi>●</bdi>Require an object to invoke them.

<bdi>●</bdi>Defined without the static keyword.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Instance Methods

public class Student {

// Instance variables

String name;

int grade;

// Instance method

void displayInfo() {

System.out.println("Name: " + name);

System.out.println("Grade: " + grade);

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Creating an object of the Student class

Student student1 = new Student();

student1.name = "Alice";

student1.grade = 90;

// Calling an instance method

student1.displayInfo();

}

}

#### Output:

Name: Alice

Grade: 90

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation of the Code

1.  Instance Method:

<bdi>○</bdi>displayInfo() is an instance method that prints the values of the instance variables name and grade.

1.  Object-Specific Behavior:

<bdi>○</bdi>The method operates on the student1 object and accesses its variables.

1.  Direct Access:

<bdi>○</bdi>Instance methods can directly access instance variables (name and grade).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Using Instance Variables and Methods Together

#### Code Example:

public class Employee {

// Instance variables

String name;

double salary;

// Instance method

double annualSalary() {

return salary * 12;

}

// Instance method to display details

void displayDetails() {

System.out.println("Name: " + name);

System.out.println("Annual Salary: " + annualSalary());

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Creating an object of the Employee class

Employee emp = new Employee();

emp.name = "John";

emp.salary = 5000;

// Calling instance methods

emp.displayDetails();

}

}

#### Output:

Name: John

Annual Salary: 60000.0

#### Explanation of the Code

1.  Instance Variables:

<bdi>○</bdi>name and salary are instance variables representing the state of an employee.

1.  Instance Method with Computation:

<bdi>○</bdi>annualSalary() calculates the annual salary using the instance variable salary.

1.  Instance Method with Dependency:

<bdi>○</bdi>displayDetails() calls another instance method (annualSalary()) and prints the employee details.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. this Keyword

The this keyword is used to refer to the current object within an instance method or constructor. It helps differentiate between instance variables and method parameters when they have the same name.

#### Code Example: this Keyword

public class Product {

// Instance variables

String name;

double price;

// Constructor using `this`

Product(String name, double price) {

this.name = name; // Assigns parameter `name` to instance variable `name`

this.price = price; // Assigns parameter `price` to instance variable `price`

}

// Instance method

void display() {

System.out.println("Product Name: " + this.name);

System.out.println("Product Price: " + this.price);

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

Product prod = new Product("Laptop", 1200.50);

prod.display();

}

}

#### Output:

Product Name: Laptop

Product Price: 1200.5

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Common Uses of Instance Variables and Methods

<bdi>●</bdi>Represent Object-Specific Data:

<bdi>○</bdi>Example: name, age, and balance for a Person object.

<bdi>●</bdi>Encapsulation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use private instance variables and provide public getter/setter methods for controlled access.

<bdi>●</bdi>Object-Specific Operations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Example: Calculating a specific object’s salary, grade, or interest.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Comparison of Instance and Static Members

| Aspect | Instance Variables/Methods | Static Variables/Methods |
| Belongs To | Specific objects | The class itself |
| Requires Object | Yes | No |
| Access | Can access both instance and static members | Can access only static members directly |
| Memory Usage | Separate memory for each object | Shared memory across all objects |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Instance Variables:

<bdi>○</bdi>Store object-specific data.

<bdi>○</bdi>Each object has its own copy.

<bdi>●</bdi>Instance Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>Operate on instance variables.

<bdi>○</bdi>Require an object to invoke them.

<bdi>●</bdi>this Keyword:

<bdi>○</bdi>Refers to the current object, useful for distinguishing instance variables from parameters.

## Constructors

A constructor is a special method in Java used to initialize an object when it is created. It has the same name as the class and is automatically called when a new object is instantiated.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Constructors

1.  Same Name as the Class: The constructor must have the same name as the class.

1.  No Return Type: Constructors do not have a return type, not even void.

1.  Called Automatically: The constructor is invoked automatically when an object is created.

1.  Overloading: Constructors can be overloaded (multiple constructors with different parameter lists).

1.  Default Constructor: If no constructor is defined, Java provides a no-argument default constructor.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Types of Constructors

1.  Default Constructor

1.  Parameterized Constructor

1.  No-Argument Constructor

1.  Copy Constructor (Manual implementation)

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Default Constructor

If no constructor is defined in the class, Java provides a default no-argument constructor.

#### Code Example:

public class DefaultConstructorExample {

int number;

String name;

public static void main(String[] args) {

DefaultConstructorExample obj = new DefaultConstructorExample();

System.out.println("Number: " + obj.number); // Default value is 0

System.out.println("Name: " + obj.name); // Default value is null

}

}

#### Output:

Number: 0

Name: null

#### Explanation:

1.  Java provides a default constructor that initializes number to 0 and name to null.

1.  The default constructor is called automatically when the object is created.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Parameterized Constructor

A constructor that takes arguments to initialize instance variables.

#### Code Example:

public class ParameterizedConstructorExample {

int number;

String name;

// Parameterized constructor

public ParameterizedConstructorExample(int num, String str) {

number = num;

name = str;

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

ParameterizedConstructorExample obj = new ParameterizedConstructorExample(10, "Alice");

System.out.println("Number: " + obj.number);

System.out.println("Name: " + obj.name);

}

}

#### Output:

Number: 10

Name: Alice

#### Explanation:

1.  The parameterized constructor initializes number and name with the values passed during object creation.

1.  new ParameterizedConstructorExample(10, "Alice") invokes the constructor with arguments 10 and "Alice".

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. No-Argument Constructor

A no-argument constructor is explicitly defined without any parameters.

#### Code Example:

public class NoArgumentConstructorExample {

int number;

String name;

// No-argument constructor

public NoArgumentConstructorExample() {

number = 0;

name = "Default Name";

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

NoArgumentConstructorExample obj = new NoArgumentConstructorExample();

System.out.println("Number: " + obj.number);

System.out.println("Name: " + obj.name);

}

}

#### Output:

Number: 0

Name: Default Name

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The no-argument constructor initializes number to 0 and name to "Default Name".

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Constructor Overloading

Multiple constructors can exist in a class, each with different parameter lists.

#### Code Example:

public class ConstructorOverloadingExample {

int number;

String name;

// No-argument constructor

public ConstructorOverloadingExample() {

number = -1;

name = "Unknown";

}

// Parameterized constructor

public ConstructorOverloadingExample(int num, String str) {

number = num;

name = str;

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

ConstructorOverloadingExample obj1 = new ConstructorOverloadingExample();

ConstructorOverloadingExample obj2 = new ConstructorOverloadingExample(42, "Bob");

System.out.println("Object 1: Number = " + obj1.number + ", Name = " + obj1.name);

System.out.println("Object 2: Number = " + obj2.number + ", Name = " + obj2.name);

}

}

#### Output:

Object 1: Number = -1, Name = Unknown

Object 2: Number = 42, Name = Bob

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>Two constructors are defined:

<bdi>○</bdi>A no-argument constructor.

<bdi>○</bdi>A parameterized constructor.

<bdi>●</bdi>The constructor invoked depends on the arguments passed during object creation.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Using this in Constructors

The this keyword can be used to:

1.  Differentiate between instance variables and parameters.

1.  Call another constructor in the same class.

#### Code Example:

public class ThisKeywordExample {

int number;

String name;

// Constructor with this

public ThisKeywordExample(int number, String name) {

this.number = number;

this.name = name;

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

ThisKeywordExample obj = new ThisKeywordExample(15, "Charlie");

System.out.println("Number: " + obj.number);

System.out.println("Name: " + obj.name);

}

}

#### Output:

Number: 15

Name: Charlie

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Copy Constructor

A copy constructor creates a new object as a copy of an existing object.

#### Code Example:

public class CopyConstructorExample {

int number;

String name;

// Parameterized constructor

public CopyConstructorExample(int num, String str) {

number = num;

name = str;

}

// Copy constructor

public CopyConstructorExample(CopyConstructorExample obj) {

number = obj.number;

name = obj.name;

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

CopyConstructorExample obj1 = new CopyConstructorExample(10, "Alice");

CopyConstructorExample obj2 = new CopyConstructorExample(obj1);

System.out.println("Object 1: Number = " + obj1.number + ", Name = " + obj1.name);

System.out.println("Object 2: Number = " + obj2.number + ", Name = " + obj2.name);

}

}

#### Output:

Object 1: Number = 10, Name = Alice

Object 2: Number = 10, Name = Alice

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The copy constructor takes another object of the same class as an argument and duplicates its values.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Comparison of Constructor Types

| Type | Purpose |
| Default Constructor | Automatically provided by Java if none is defined. |
| No-Argument Constructor | Explicitly initializes default values. |
| Parameterized Constructor | Allows setting custom values during object creation. |
| Copy Constructor | Creates a duplicate object with the same state. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Points to Remember

1.  Constructors are not inherited but can be called using super in subclasses.

1.  A class can have multiple constructors (constructor overloading).

1.  The this keyword is used to avoid naming conflicts or call other constructors.

1.  Use constructors to ensure objects are always created in a valid state.

Constructors are fundamental in Java as they allow you to control how objects are initialized and ensure consistency in object creation.

## Static and Instance Members

In Java, class members (variables and methods) can either be static or instance. These classifications determine whether they belong to the class itself or to individual objects created from the class.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Differences: Static vs Instance Members

| Aspect | Static Members | Instance Members |
| Belonging | Belong to the class (shared among all objects). | Belong to individual objects (unique to each object). |
| Access | Accessed using the class name or object reference. | Accessed using object reference only. |
| Memory Usage | Stored once in memory (class-level). | Separate memory allocated for each object. |
| Keyword | Declared with the static keyword. | No special keyword. |
| Behavior | Independent of object instances. | Dependent on object instances. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Static Members

Static members are associated with the class itself, not with any specific instance of the class.

#### Key Features of Static Members

1.  Declared using the static keyword.

1.  Shared across all objects of the class.

1.  Can be accessed directly using the class name.

1.  Cannot directly access instance members (without an object reference).

1.  Often used for utility functions or constants.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Static Variables

Static variables are shared among all objects of a class. They are commonly used for constants or counters.

#### Code Example:

public class StaticVariableExample {

static int counter = 0; // Static variable

public StaticVariableExample() {

counter++;

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

StaticVariableExample obj1 = new StaticVariableExample();

StaticVariableExample obj2 = new StaticVariableExample();

System.out.println("Counter: " + StaticVariableExample.counter);

}

}

#### Output:

Counter: 2

#### Explanation:

1.  The counter variable is static, so it is shared among all instances.

1.  Each time a new object is created, the constructor increments counter.

1.  The value of counter is accessed using the class name.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Static Methods

Static methods can be called without creating an instance of the class.

#### Code Example:

public class StaticMethodExample {

static int square(int x) {

return x * x;

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

int result = StaticMethodExample.square(5);

System.out.println("Square of 5: " + result);

}

}

#### Output:

Square of 5: 25

#### Explanation:

1.  The square method is static, so it is called using the class name.

1.  It performs the computation independently of any instance.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Instance Members

Instance members are associated with a specific object. Each object has its own copy of instance variables and can independently invoke instance methods.

#### Instance Variables

Instance variables store data unique to each object.

#### Code Example:

public class InstanceVariableExample {

int id; // Instance variable

String name;

public InstanceVariableExample(int id, String name) {

this.id = id;

this.name = name;

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

InstanceVariableExample obj1 = new InstanceVariableExample(1, "Alice");

InstanceVariableExample obj2 = new InstanceVariableExample(2, "Bob");

System.out.println("Object 1: ID = " + obj1.id + ", Name = " + obj1.name);

System.out.println("Object 2: ID = " + obj2.id + ", Name = " + obj2.name);

}

}

#### Output:

Object 1: ID = 1, Name = Alice

Object 2: ID = 2, Name = Bob

#### Explanation:

1.  Each object has its own id and name instance variables.

1.  Changes to one object do not affect the other.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Instance Methods

Instance methods operate on instance variables and require an object to be called.

#### Code Example:

public class InstanceMethodExample {

String name;

public InstanceMethodExample(String name) {

this.name = name;

}

void display() {

System.out.println("Name: " + name);

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

InstanceMethodExample obj = new InstanceMethodExample("Charlie");

obj.display();

}

}

#### Output:

Name: Charlie

#### Explanation:

1.  The display method is an instance method, so it must be called on an object (obj).

1.  It accesses the name instance variable specific to the object.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Accessing Static and Instance Members

#### Code Example:

public class StaticInstanceExample {

static String staticVar = "Static";

String instanceVar = "Instance";

static void staticMethod() {

System.out.println("Inside static method");

}

void instanceMethod() {

System.out.println("Inside instance method");

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Accessing static members

System.out.println("Static Variable: " + StaticInstanceExample.staticVar);

StaticInstanceExample.staticMethod();

// Accessing instance members

StaticInstanceExample obj = new StaticInstanceExample();

System.out.println("Instance Variable: " + obj.instanceVar);

obj.instanceMethod();

}

}

#### Output:

Static Variable: Static

Inside static method

Instance Variable: Instance

Inside instance method

#### Explanation:

1.  Static Members:

<bdi>○</bdi>Accessed using the class name (StaticInstanceExample.staticVar and StaticInstanceExample.staticMethod()).

<bdi>○</bdi>Independent of object creation.

1.  Instance Members:

<bdi>○</bdi>Accessed using an object (obj.instanceVar and obj.instanceMethod()).

<bdi>○</bdi>Depend on the specific object.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Mixing Static and Instance Members

Static methods cannot directly access instance members because they do not operate on a specific object.

#### Code Example:

public class MixingStaticInstance {

String instanceVar = "Instance";

static void staticMethod() {

// Cannot access instanceVar directly

// System.out.println(instanceVar); // Error

// Create an object to access instanceVar

MixingStaticInstance obj = new MixingStaticInstance();

System.out.println("Accessed Instance Variable: " + obj.instanceVar);

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

staticMethod();

}

}

#### Output:

Accessed Instance Variable: Instance

#### Explanation:

1.  Static methods cannot directly access instance members because they are not tied to any object.

1.  Creating an object allows access to instance variables and methods.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Use Cases

| Type | Use Case |
| Static Variables | Shared constants or counters (e.g., Math.PI, number of objects created). |
| Static Methods | Utility methods (e.g., Math.sqrt(), Arrays.sort()). |
| Instance Variables | Storing object-specific data (e.g., name, age, balance). |
| Instance Methods | Behaviors specific to an object (e.g., deposit(), withdraw()). |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Static Members:

<bdi>○</bdi>Shared by all objects, belong to the class.

<bdi>○</bdi>Ideal for common data and utility methods.

<bdi>●</bdi>Instance Members:

<bdi>○</bdi>Unique to each object, belong to individual instances.

<bdi>○</bdi>Used for object-specific state and behavior.

## Method Overloading and Overriding

Java supports two important concepts related to methods in Object-Oriented Programming: Method Overloading and Method Overriding. Both allow a program to define multiple methods with similar functionalities but serve different purposes.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Method Overloading

Method Overloading occurs when multiple methods in the same class have the same name but different parameter lists (type, number, or order of parameters). It provides compile-time polymorphism.

#### Key Features of Method Overloading

1.  Methods must have the same name but different parameter lists.

1.  Overloaded methods can have different return types.

1.  It allows the same method name to handle different types or numbers of arguments.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Method Overloading

public class MethodOverloadingExample {

// Method with one parameter

void display(int number) {

System.out.println("Number: " + number);

}

// Overloaded method with two parameters

void display(int number, String message) {

System.out.println("Number: " + number + ", Message: " + message);

}

// Overloaded method with different parameter type

void display(String message) {

System.out.println("Message: " + message);

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

MethodOverloadingExample obj = new MethodOverloadingExample();

obj.display(10); // Calls the first method

obj.display(20, "Hello"); // Calls the second method

obj.display("Overloading Example"); // Calls the third method

}

}

#### Output:

Number: 10

Number: 20, Message: Hello

Message: Overloading Example

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation of the Code

1.  First Method Call:

<bdi>○</bdi>obj.display(10) matches the method with one int parameter: void display(int number).

1.  Second Method Call:

<bdi>○</bdi>obj.display(20, "Hello") matches the method with two parameters: void display(int number, String message).

1.  Third Method Call:

<bdi>○</bdi>obj.display("Overloading Example") matches the method with one String parameter: void display(String message).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Rules for Method Overloading

1.  Different Parameter List:

<bdi>○</bdi>Methods must differ by type, number, or order of parameters.

Example: void print(int a);

void print(double b);

void print(int a, int b);

1.  Cannot Overload by Return Type Alone:

<bdi>○</bdi>Changing only the return type does not allow overloading.

Example: void print(int a);

int print(int a); // Error: Method already defined

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Method Overriding

Method Overriding occurs when a subclass provides a specific implementation of a method that is already defined in its superclass. It provides runtime polymorphism.

#### Key Features of Method Overriding

1.  The method in the subclass must have:

<bdi>○</bdi>The same name as the method in the superclass.

<bdi>○</bdi>The same parameter list.

1.  The overriding method replaces the superclass's method for the subclass.

1.  The return type must be the same (or covariant).

1.  The @Override annotation can be used for clarity.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Method Overriding

class Animal {

void sound() {

System.out.println("Animals make sound");

}

}

class Dog extends Animal {

@Override

void sound() {

System.out.println("Dogs bark");

}

}

public class MethodOverridingExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Animal animal = new Animal();

animal.sound(); // Calls the method in Animal class

Dog dog = new Dog();

dog.sound(); // Calls the overridden method in Dog class

Animal ref = new Dog();

ref.sound(); // Calls the overridden method due to runtime polymorphism

}

}

#### Output:

Animals make sound

Dogs bark

Dogs bark

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation of the Code

1.  Overridden Method:

<bdi>○</bdi>The Dog class overrides the sound method from the Animal class.

1.  Polymorphism:

<bdi>○</bdi>When the ref reference of type Animal points to an object of type Dog, the overridden method in Dog is called at runtime.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Rules for Method Overriding

1.  Same Method Signature:

<bdi>○</bdi>The method must have the same name, parameters, and return type as the method in the superclass.

1.  Access Modifiers:

<bdi>○</bdi>The overriding method cannot reduce the visibility of the method in the superclass.

Example: public void sound(); // Superclass

protected void sound(); // Error in Subclass

1.  Use of @Override Annotation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensures that the method is correctly overriding a superclass method.

Example: @Override

void sound();

1.  Final Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>Methods declared as final in the superclass cannot be overridden.

1.  Static Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>Static methods are not overridden; they are hidden.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Difference Between Method Overloading and Overriding

| Aspect | Method Overloading | Method Overriding |
| Definition | Same method name with different parameter lists. | Same method signature in superclass and subclass. |
| Polymorphism Type | Compile-time polymorphism. | Runtime polymorphism. |
| Return Type | Can differ. | Must be the same or covariant. |
| Access Modifier | No restriction. | Cannot reduce visibility. |
| Scope | Within the same class. | Across superclass and subclass. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Advanced Scenarios

#### Overloading with Varargs

public class VarargsExample {

void display(int... numbers) {

System.out.println("Number of arguments: " + numbers.length);

}

void display(String message) {

System.out.println("Message: " + message);

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

VarargsExample obj = new VarargsExample();

obj.display(1, 2, 3); // Calls varargs method

obj.display("Hello"); // Calls string method

}

}

#### Output:

Number of arguments: 3

Message: Hello

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Overriding Static Methods (Method Hiding)

Static methods are not overridden; they are hidden when redefined in a subclass.

class Parent {

static void greet() {

System.out.println("Hello from Parent");

}

}

class Child extends Parent {

static void greet() {

System.out.println("Hello from Child");

}

}

public class MethodHidingExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Parent.greet(); // Calls Parent's static method

Child.greet(); // Calls Child's static method

}

}

#### Output:

Hello from Parent

Hello from Child

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Method Overloading:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides flexibility by allowing methods with the same name to perform similar tasks on different input types.

<bdi>●</bdi>Method Overriding:

<bdi>○</bdi>Allows a subclass to provide a specific implementation of a method from its superclass.

## Access Modifiers

Access Modifiers in Java control the visibility and accessibility of classes, methods, and variables. They determine whether other classes or objects can access a particular member or not.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Types of Access Modifiers

Java provides four levels of access modifiers:

| Modifier | Class | Package | Subclass | World (Other Packages) |
| public | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| protected | Yes | Yes | Yes | No (unless subclassed) |
| Default | Yes | Yes | No | No |
| private | Yes | No | No | No |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. public Access Modifier

The public modifier makes a member accessible from anywhere.

#### Code Example:

public class PublicExample {

public String message = "This is a public message";

public void display() {

System.out.println(message);

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

PublicExample obj = new PublicExample();

obj.display();

}

}

#### Output:

This is a public message

#### Explanation:

1.  The message variable and the display method are both public, making them accessible from anywhere.

1.  The main method accesses and prints the public message.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. private Access Modifier

The private modifier restricts access to the same class only. Members declared as private are not accessible outside their class.

#### Code Example:

public class PrivateExample {

private String message = "This is a private message";

private void display() {

System.out.println(message);

}

public void show() {

display(); // Allowed within the class

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

PrivateExample obj = new PrivateExample();

obj.show(); // Indirectly accesses the private method

}

}

#### Output:

This is a private message

#### Explanation:

1.  The message variable and the display method are private and cannot be accessed directly outside the class.

1.  The show method provides indirect access to the private display method.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Default Access (No Modifier)

When no access modifier is specified, the member has package-level visibility. This means it is accessible only within the same package.

#### Code Example:

File: DefaultExample.java

class DefaultExample {

String message = "This is a default message";

void display() {

System.out.println(message);

}

}

File: MainClass.java (same package):

public class MainClass {

public static void main(String[] args) {

DefaultExample obj = new DefaultExample();

obj.display(); // Accessible because they are in the same package

}

}

#### Output:

This is a default message

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The message variable and the display method are accessible within the same package.

<bdi>●</bdi>They are not accessible if the MainClass is in a different package.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. protected Access Modifier

The protected modifier allows access within the same package and subclasses in other packages.

#### Code Example:

File: ParentClass.java

package example1;

public class ParentClass {

protected String message = "This is a protected message";

protected void display() {

System.out.println(message);

}

}

File: ChildClass.java

package example2;

import example1.ParentClass;

public class ChildClass extends ParentClass {

public static void main(String[] args) {

ChildClass obj = new ChildClass();

obj.display(); // Accessible because ChildClass is a subclass

}

}

#### Output:

This is a protected message

#### Explanation:

1.  The message variable and display method are protected, so they are accessible in the ChildClass because it inherits from ParentClass.

1.  Protected members are not directly accessible by unrelated classes in other packages.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Access Levels Summary

| Modifier | Same Class | Same Package | Subclass (Other Package) | World (Other Packages) |
| public | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| protected | Yes | Yes | Yes | No |
| Default | Yes | Yes | No | No |
| private | Yes | No | No | No |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Practical Example with All Access Modifiers

#### File: AccessModifiersExample.java

package example1;

public class AccessModifiersExample {

public String publicVar = "Public";

protected String protectedVar = "Protected";

String defaultVar = "Default";

private String privateVar = "Private";

public void publicMethod() {

System.out.println("Public Method");

}

protected void protectedMethod() {

System.out.println("Protected Method");

}

void defaultMethod() {

System.out.println("Default Method");

}

private void privateMethod() {

System.out.println("Private Method");

}

public void accessAll() {

System.out.println(publicVar);

System.out.println(protectedVar);

System.out.println(defaultVar);

System.out.println(privateVar);

publicMethod();

protectedMethod();

defaultMethod();

privateMethod();

}

}

#### File: AccessTest.java

package example1;

public class AccessTest {

public static void main(String[] args) {

AccessModifiersExample obj = new AccessModifiersExample();

// Accessing variables

System.out.println(obj.publicVar); // Accessible

System.out.println(obj.protectedVar); // Accessible (same package)

System.out.println(obj.defaultVar); // Accessible (same package)

// System.out.println(obj.privateVar); // Error: private access

// Accessing methods

obj.publicMethod(); // Accessible

obj.protectedMethod(); // Accessible (same package)

obj.defaultMethod(); // Accessible (same package)

// obj.privateMethod(); // Error: private access

}

}

#### Output:

Public

Protected

Default

Public Method

Protected Method

Default Method

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Points to Remember

1.  public:

<bdi>○</bdi>Accessible everywhere.

1.  private:

<bdi>○</bdi>Accessible only within the class.

1.  Default:

<bdi>○</bdi>Accessible within the same package.

1.  protected:

<bdi>○</bdi>Accessible within the same package and subclasses in other packages.

## Abstract Classes and Methods

Abstract classes and abstract methods in Java are used to achieve abstraction, which hides implementation details and focuses on essential functionalities. Abstract classes serve as a blueprint for other classes and cannot be instantiated directly.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Abstract Classes

1.  Abstract Class:

<bdi>○</bdi>Declared using the abstract keyword.

<bdi>○</bdi>Can have both abstract (unimplemented) and non-abstract (implemented) methods.

<bdi>○</bdi>Cannot be instantiated directly.

<bdi>○</bdi>Used to define common behavior for subclasses.

1.  Abstract Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>Declared without a body (no implementation).

<bdi>○</bdi>Must be implemented by any concrete (non-abstract) subclass.

1.  Partial Implementation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Abstract classes can have instance variables, constructors, and concrete methods alongside abstract methods.

1.  Inheritance:

<bdi>○</bdi>A subclass must provide implementations for all abstract methods or be declared as abstract itself.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Syntax

#### Abstract Class:

abstract class ClassName {

// Abstract method (no implementation)

abstract void abstractMethod();

// Concrete method (with implementation)

void concreteMethod() {

System.out.println("This is a concrete method.");

}

}

#### Concrete Subclass:

class SubClass extends ClassName {

@Override

void abstractMethod() {

System.out.println("Abstract method implemented in SubClass.");

}

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Example: Abstract Classes and Methods

#### Code Example:

abstract class Animal {

// Abstract method

abstract void makeSound();

// Concrete method

void sleep() {

System.out.println("Sleeping...");

}

}

class Dog extends Animal {

@Override

void makeSound() {

System.out.println("Bark");

}

}

class Cat extends Animal {

@Override

void makeSound() {

System.out.println("Meow");

}

}

public class AbstractClassExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Animal dog = new Dog();

dog.makeSound(); // Calls the overridden method in Dog class

dog.sleep(); // Calls the concrete method from Animal class

Animal cat = new Cat();

cat.makeSound(); // Calls the overridden method in Cat class

cat.sleep(); // Calls the concrete method from Animal class

}

}

#### Output:

Bark

Sleeping...

Meow

Sleeping...

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation of the Code

1.  Abstract Class Animal:

<bdi>○</bdi>Defines an abstract method makeSound() and a concrete method sleep().

1.  Subclasses Dog and Cat:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provide implementations for the abstract method makeSound().

1.  Polymorphism:

<bdi>○</bdi>The dog and cat objects are of type Animal, but their method calls are resolved at runtime based on their actual class.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Abstract Class with Constructor and Fields

Abstract classes can have constructors and fields, which are inherited by subclasses.

#### Code Example:

abstract class Vehicle {

String brand;

// Constructor

Vehicle(String brand) {

this.brand = brand;

}

// Abstract method

abstract void drive();

// Concrete method

void displayBrand() {

System.out.println("Brand: " + brand);

}

}

class Car extends Vehicle {

Car(String brand) {

super(brand); // Call the abstract class constructor

}

@Override

void drive() {

System.out.println("Driving a car...");

}

}

public class AbstractWithConstructorExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Vehicle myCar = new Car("Toyota");

myCar.displayBrand(); // Access inherited concrete method

myCar.drive(); // Access overridden method

}

}

#### Output:

Brand: Toyota

Driving a car...

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation of the Code

1.  Abstract Class with Constructor:

<bdi>○</bdi>The Vehicle class has a constructor to initialize the brand field.

1.  Subclass Constructor:

<bdi>○</bdi>The Car class uses super() to call the Vehicle constructor.

1.  Concrete and Overridden Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>displayBrand() is inherited and called without changes.

<bdi>○</bdi>drive() is implemented in the subclass.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Abstract Classes with Multiple Subclasses

Abstract classes are useful for defining a common interface for multiple related classes.

#### Code Example:

abstract class Shape {

abstract double calculateArea();

}

class Circle extends Shape {

double radius;

Circle(double radius) {

this.radius = radius;

}

@Override

double calculateArea() {

return Math.PI * radius * radius;

}

}

class Rectangle extends Shape {

double length, width;

Rectangle(double length, double width) {

this.length = length;

this.width = width;

}

@Override

double calculateArea() {

return length * width;

}

}

public class AbstractClassShapeExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Shape circle = new Circle(5.0);

Shape rectangle = new Rectangle(4.0, 6.0);

System.out.println("Circle Area: " + circle.calculateArea());

System.out.println("Rectangle Area: " + rectangle.calculateArea());

}

}

#### Output:

Circle Area: 78.53981633974483

Rectangle Area: 24.0

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation of the Code

1.  Abstract Class Shape:

<bdi>○</bdi>Defines the abstract method calculateArea().

1.  Subclasses:

<bdi>○</bdi>Circle and Rectangle provide specific implementations for calculateArea().

1.  Polymorphism:

<bdi>○</bdi>Both circle and rectangle are of type Shape, but their calculateArea() calls resolve to their respective implementations.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Abstract Classes vs Interfaces

| Aspect | Abstract Class | Interface |
| Methods | Can have both abstract and concrete methods. | All methods are abstract (before Java 8). |
| Fields | Can have instance variables and constants. | Only constants (implicitly public static final). |
| Constructors | Can have constructors. | Cannot have constructors. |
| Inheritance | A class can extend only one abstract class. | A class can implement multiple interfaces. |
| Access Modifiers | Methods can have any access modifier. | Methods are implicitly public. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Practical Scenarios for Abstract Classes

1.  Common Behavior:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use abstract classes to define common fields and methods for related classes.

<bdi>○</bdi>Example: Animal for Dog and Cat.

1.  Partial Implementation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Combine abstract methods with concrete methods to partially implement behavior.

<bdi>○</bdi>Example: Vehicle for Car and Bike.

1.  Restrict Instantiation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Abstract classes prevent direct instantiation, enforcing the use of subclasses.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Abstract Classes:

<bdi>○</bdi>Serve as blueprints for related classes.

<bdi>○</bdi>Can have a mix of abstract and concrete methods.

<bdi>○</bdi>Cannot be instantiated directly.

<bdi>●</bdi>Abstract Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>Must be implemented by concrete subclasses.

<bdi>●</bdi>Practical Uses:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enforce a common interface for related classes.

<bdi>○</bdi>Share code across multiple subclasses.

## Interfaces

An interface in Java is a blueprint for a class that specifies a set of methods that a class must implement. Interfaces achieve 100% abstraction and are often used to define common behavior across unrelated classes.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Interfaces

1.  Declaration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Declared using the interface keyword.

<bdi>○</bdi>Contains method signatures (abstract methods) and constants.

<bdi>○</bdi>Cannot have constructors or instance variables.

1.  Implementation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Classes implement an interface using the implements keyword.

<bdi>○</bdi>A class must provide implementations for all the methods declared in the interface.

1.  Multiple Inheritance:

<bdi>○</bdi>A class can implement multiple interfaces, allowing for multiple inheritance.

1.  Default and Static Methods (introduced in Java 8):

<bdi>○</bdi>Interfaces can have concrete methods with the default or static keyword.

1.  Functional Interfaces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Interfaces with a single abstract method are functional interfaces (e.g., Runnable).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Syntax

#### Declaring an Interface

interface InterfaceName {

// Abstract methods

void method1();

void method2();

}

#### Implementing an Interface

class ClassName implements InterfaceName {

@Override

public void method1() {

// Implementation

}

@Override

public void method2() {

// Implementation

}

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Basic Example of an Interface

#### Code Example:

interface Animal {

void makeSound(); // Abstract method

void eat();

}

class Dog implements Animal {

@Override

public void makeSound() {

System.out.println("Bark");

}

@Override

public void eat() {

System.out.println("Dog eats food.");

}

}

class Cat implements Animal {

@Override

public void makeSound() {

System.out.println("Meow");

}

@Override

public void eat() {

System.out.println("Cat eats food.");

}

}

public class InterfaceExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Animal dog = new Dog();

dog.makeSound();

dog.eat();

Animal cat = new Cat();

cat.makeSound();

cat.eat();

}

}

#### Output:

Bark

Dog eats food.

Meow

Cat eats food.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation

1.  Interface Definition:

<bdi>○</bdi>The Animal interface declares two abstract methods: makeSound() and eat().

1.  Implementation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Both Dog and Cat implement the Animal interface by providing concrete implementations for its methods.

1.  Polymorphism:

<bdi>○</bdi>The dog and cat objects are of type Animal, but their method calls are resolved at runtime based on the actual class (Dog or Cat).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Interface with Constants

Interfaces can declare constants, which are implicitly public static final.

#### Code Example:

interface Constants {

int MAX_SPEED = 120; // Implicitly public static final

}

class Vehicle implements Constants {

void displayMaxSpeed() {

System.out.println("Max speed is: " + MAX_SPEED);

}

}

public class InterfaceConstantsExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Vehicle car = new Vehicle();

car.displayMaxSpeed();

}

}

#### Output:

Max speed is: 120

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation

<bdi>●</bdi>The MAX_SPEED constant in the Constants interface is shared across all implementing classes.

<bdi>●</bdi>It is automatically public static final.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Default and Static Methods in Interfaces (Java 8)

#### Code Example:

interface Payment {

void pay();

// Default method

default void printReceipt() {

System.out.println("Printing receipt...");

}

// Static method

static void welcomeMessage() {

System.out.println("Welcome to the payment system!");

}

}

class CreditCardPayment implements Payment {

@Override

public void pay() {

System.out.println("Paying with credit card.");

}

}

public class DefaultStaticExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Payment.welcomeMessage(); // Call static method directly

Payment payment = new CreditCardPayment();

payment.pay();

payment.printReceipt(); // Call default method

}

}

#### Output:

Welcome to the payment system!

Paying with credit card.

Printing receipt...

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation

1.  Default Method:

<bdi>○</bdi>printReceipt() provides a default implementation in the Payment interface.

<bdi>○</bdi>Classes can use it as is or override it.

1.  Static Method:

<bdi>○</bdi>welcomeMessage() is a static method and is called using the interface name.

1.  Implementation:

<bdi>○</bdi>CreditCardPayment implements the Payment interface and provides an implementation for the pay() method.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Multiple Interfaces and Multiple Inheritance

A class can implement multiple interfaces, allowing it to inherit behavior from multiple sources.

#### Code Example:

interface Flyable {

void fly();

}

interface Swimable {

void swim();

}

class Bird implements Flyable, Swimable {

@Override

public void fly() {

System.out.println("Bird is flying.");

}

@Override

public void swim() {

System.out.println("Bird is swimming.");

}

}

public class MultipleInterfacesExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Bird bird = new Bird();

bird.fly();

bird.swim();

}

}

#### Output:

Bird is flying.

Bird is swimming.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation

<bdi>●</bdi>The Bird class implements both Flyable and Swimable interfaces.

<bdi>●</bdi>It provides implementations for all abstract methods in the two interfaces.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Functional Interfaces (Java 8)

A functional interface is an interface with exactly one abstract method. These interfaces are used with lambda expressions.

#### Code Example:

@FunctionalInterface

interface Greeting {

void sayHello(String name);

}

public class FunctionalInterfaceExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Using a lambda expression to implement the interface

Greeting greeting = name -> System.out.println("Hello, " + name);

greeting.sayHello("Alice");

}

}

#### Output:

Hello, Alice

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation

1.  Functional Interface:

<bdi>○</bdi>Greeting has a single abstract method sayHello(), making it a functional interface.

<bdi>○</bdi>Marked with the @FunctionalInterface annotation for clarity.

1.  Lambda Expression:

<bdi>○</bdi>The lambda expression name -> System.out.println("Hello, " + name) provides an implementation for sayHello().

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Comparison Between Interfaces and Abstract Classes

| Aspect | Interface | Abstract Class |
| Methods | All methods are abstract (until Java 8). | Can have both abstract and concrete methods. |
| Fields | Only constants (public static final). | Can have instance variables and constants. |
| Constructors | Cannot have constructors. | Can have constructors. |
| Multiple Inheritance | A class can implement multiple interfaces. | A class can extend only one abstract class. |
| Default/Static Methods | Supported (Java 8+). | Not applicable. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Points

1.  Multiple Inheritance:

<bdi>○</bdi>Interfaces allow a class to inherit behavior from multiple sources.

<bdi>○</bdi>Abstract classes do not support multiple inheritance.

1.  Default Implementation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use default methods for optional or common behavior in interfaces (Java 8+).

1.  Functional Interfaces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Simplify functional programming in Java, especially with lambda expressions.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Practical Use Cases

<bdi>●</bdi>Interfaces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Define a common contract for unrelated classes.

<bdi>○</bdi>Example: Runnable, Comparable.

<bdi>●</bdi>Abstract Classes:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provide a partial implementation for related classes.

<bdi>○</bdi>Example: Shape, Vehicle.

## Inner Classes and Anonymous Classes

Inner classes and anonymous classes are advanced features of Java that enable more modular and organized code. They allow defining classes within other classes, giving a closer relationship between the two and facilitating encapsulation.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Inner Classes

An inner class is a class defined within another class. Inner classes have access to all the members of their enclosing class, including private members.

#### Types of Inner Classes

1.  Non-Static Nested Class (Inner Class)

1.  Static Nested Class

1.  Local Inner Class

1.  Anonymous Inner Class

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1.1 Non-Static Inner Class

A non-static inner class (simply called an inner class) is tied to an instance of its enclosing class. It can access all the instance members of the enclosing class.

#### Code Example:

public class Outer {

private String message = "Hello from Outer Class";

// Inner class

class Inner {

void display() {

System.out.println(message); // Accessing private member of Outer class

}

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

Outer outer = new Outer(); // Create instance of Outer

Outer.Inner inner = outer.new Inner(); // Create instance of Inner

inner.display();

}

}

#### Output:

Hello from Outer Class

#### Explanation:

1.  The Inner class is an instance-level nested class and requires an instance of the Outer class to be created.

1.  The display() method in Inner accesses the private member message of the Outer class.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1.2 Static Nested Class

A static nested class is associated with the class itself rather than an instance of the class. It can only access the static members of the enclosing class.

#### Code Example:

public class Outer {

private static String staticMessage = "Hello from Static Nested Class";

// Static nested class

static class StaticNested {

void display() {

System.out.println(staticMessage);

}

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

Outer.StaticNested nested = new Outer.StaticNested(); // Direct creation

nested.display();

}

}

#### Output:

Hello from Static Nested Class

#### Explanation:

1.  The StaticNested class is a static nested class, so it does not require an instance of Outer to be created.

1.  It can access the static member staticMessage of the Outer class.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1.3 Local Inner Class

A local inner class is defined inside a method, constructor, or block. It is local to that scope and cannot be accessed outside it.

#### Code Example:

public class Outer {

void printMessage() {

final String localMessage = "Hello from Local Inner Class";

// Local inner class

class LocalInner {

void display() {

System.out.println(localMessage);

}

}

LocalInner localInner = new LocalInner();

localInner.display();

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

Outer outer = new Outer();

outer.printMessage();

}

}

#### Output:

Hello from Local Inner Class

#### Explanation:

1.  The LocalInner class is defined within the printMessage method.

1.  It has access to the localMessage variable because it is declared final or effectively final.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1.4 Anonymous Inner Class

An anonymous inner class is an inner class without a name. It is used to create an object of a class that implements an interface or extends a class, all in one step.

#### Code Example:

interface Greeting {

void sayHello();

}

public class AnonymousClassExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Anonymous inner class implementing an interface

Greeting greeting = new Greeting() {

@Override

public void sayHello() {

System.out.println("Hello from Anonymous Inner Class");

}

};

greeting.sayHello();

}

}

#### Output:

Hello from Anonymous Inner Class

#### Explanation:

1.  The anonymous inner class implements the Greeting interface without giving the class a name.

1.  It provides an inline implementation for the sayHello method.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Practical Use Cases

#### Use Case: Sorting with Anonymous Inner Class

import java.util.Arrays;

import java.util.Comparator;

public class SortingExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

String[] fruits = {"Banana", "Apple", "Cherry"};

// Anonymous inner class for custom sorting

Arrays.sort(fruits, new Comparator<String>() {

@Override

public int compare(String o1, String o2) {

return o2.compareTo(o1); // Sort in descending order

}

});

System.out.println(Arrays.toString(fruits));

}

}

#### Output:

[Cherry, Banana, Apple]

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Comparison of Inner Class Types

| Type | Scope | Access to Outer Class | Use Case |
| Non-Static Inner | Instance of the enclosing class. | Full access to outer instance. | Modeling close relationships between classes. |
| Static Nested | Enclosing class (no outer instance). | Only static members. | Nested utilities or helper classes. |
| Local Inner | Local to a method or block. | Full access to local variables. | Encapsulation within a method. |
| Anonymous Inner | Inline implementation (no name). | Full access to outer instance. | One-time use for interfaces or abstract classes. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

1.  Inner Classes:

<bdi>○</bdi>Classes defined within other classes or methods.

<bdi>○</bdi>Includes non-static inner, static nested, and local inner classes.

<bdi>○</bdi>Useful for logically grouping classes and accessing enclosing class members.

1.  Anonymous Classes:

<bdi>○</bdi>Simplifies inline implementation for interfaces or abstract classes.

<bdi>○</bdi>Best suited for quick, one-off use cases like listeners or sorting.

# Core Java Concepts

## Packages in Java

A package is a namespace that organizes classes and interfaces into groups. It helps to avoid naming conflicts, maintain code reusability, and manage access control. There are two types of packages in Java:

1.  Built-in Packages: Provided by the Java Standard Library.

1.  User-defined Packages: Created by developers for organizing their own classes.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Built-in Packages

Java provides several built-in packages for commonly used functionality, such as utility classes, data structures, I/O operations, networking, etc.

#### Common Built-in Packages

| Package | Purpose |
| java.lang | Core classes like String, Math, System, Object. |
| java.util | Utility classes like ArrayList, HashMap, Collections, Scanner. |
| java.io | Classes for file and stream handling (File, BufferedReader, etc.). |
| java.net | Classes for networking (Socket, URL). |
| java.sql | Classes for database connectivity (Connection, ResultSet). |
| java.time | Classes for date and time (LocalDate, LocalTime, DateTimeFormatter). |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Example: Using Built-in Packages

#### Code Example:

import java.util.ArrayList;

public class BuiltInPackageExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Using ArrayList from java.util package

ArrayList<String> fruits = new ArrayList<>();

fruits.add("Apple");

fruits.add("Banana");

fruits.add("Cherry");

for (String fruit : fruits) {

System.out.println(fruit);

}

}

}

#### Output

Apple

Banana

Cherry

#### Explanation:

1.  The ArrayList class from the java.util package is imported.

1.  The fruits list stores and displays a collection of strings.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. User-defined Packages

User-defined packages are created by developers to organize their code. A package typically represents a folder or directory structure.

#### Steps to Create and Use a User-defined Package

1.  Define a Package:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use the package keyword at the top of the file.

<bdi>○</bdi>Save the file in a folder with the same name as the package.

1.  Compile:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use javac -d . ClassName.java to compile and create the package structure.

1.  Import and Use:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use the import statement to access the package in another class.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Example: User-defined Package

File: mypackage/Calculator.java

package mypackage;

public class Calculator {

public int add(int a, int b) {

return a + b;

}

public int subtract(int a, int b) {

return a - b;

}

}

File: MainClass.java

import mypackage.Calculator;

public class MainClass {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Calculator calc = new Calculator();

System.out.println("Addition: " + calc.add(10, 5));

System.out.println("Subtraction: " + calc.subtract(10, 5));

}

}

#### Compilation and Execution:

Create a folder structure matching the package name: mypackage/

Calculator.java

Compile the Calculator.java file: javac -d . mypackage/Calculator.java

Compile and run MainClass.java: javac MainClass.java

java MainClass

#### Output:

Addition: 15

Subtraction: 5

#### Explanation:

1.  The Calculator class is part of the mypackage package.

1.  It is imported and used in MainClass to perform addition and subtraction.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Package Access Control

Access modifiers control the visibility of classes and members across packages:

| Modifier | Same Class | Same Package | Subclasses (Other Packages) | Other Packages |
| public | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| protected | Yes | Yes | Yes | No |
| Default (no modifier) | Yes | Yes | No | No |
| private | Yes | No | No | No |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Nested Packages

Packages can have a nested structure to represent hierarchical organization.

#### Example: Nested Package

File: company/finance/SalaryCalculator.java

package company.finance;

public class SalaryCalculator {

public double calculateSalary(double base, double bonus) {

return base + bonus;

}

}

File: MainApp.java

import company.finance.SalaryCalculator;

public class MainApp {

public static void main(String[] args) {

SalaryCalculator calc = new SalaryCalculator();

System.out.println("Total Salary: " + calc.calculateSalary(50000, 7000));

}

}

#### Compilation and Execution:

Create the folder structure: company/

finance/

SalaryCalculator.java

Compile the SalaryCalculator.java file: javac -d . company/finance/SalaryCalculator.java

Compile and run MainApp.java: javac MainApp.java

java MainApp

#### Output:

Total Salary: 57000.0

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Importing Entire Packages

You can import all classes from a package using the * wildcard.

#### Code Example:

import java.util.*;

public class ImportAllExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

ArrayList<String> items = new ArrayList<>();

items.add("Pen");

items.add("Notebook");

HashMap<String, Integer> stock = new HashMap<>();

stock.put("Pen", 50);

stock.put("Notebook", 20);

System.out.println(items);

System.out.println(stock);

}

}

#### Output:

[Pen, Notebook]

{Pen=50, Notebook=20}

#### Explanation:

1.  import java.util.* imports all classes in the java.util package.

1.  Both ArrayList and HashMap are used without individually importing them.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Avoiding Name Conflicts with Fully Qualified Names

If two packages have classes with the same name, use the fully qualified name to avoid ambiguity.

#### Code Example:

package package1;

public class Greeting {

public void sayHello() {

System.out.println("Hello from Package 1");

}

}

package package2;

public class Greeting {

public void sayHello() {

System.out.println("Hello from Package 2");

}

}

public class MainApp {

public static void main(String[] args) {

package1.Greeting greet1 = new package1.Greeting();

package2.Greeting greet2 = new package2.Greeting();

greet1.sayHello();

greet2.sayHello();

}

}

#### Output:

Hello from Package 1

Hello from Package 2

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Advantages of Using Packages

1.  Encapsulation: Groups related classes together.

1.  Modularity: Makes the codebase easier to manage.

1.  Reusability: Promotes the reuse of classes across projects.

1.  Name Conflicts: Avoids class name conflicts using namespaces.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Built-in Packages:

<bdi>○</bdi>Include utility classes for common tasks.

<bdi>○</bdi>Examples: java.util, java.io.

<bdi>●</bdi>User-defined Packages:

<bdi>○</bdi>Created by developers for custom organization.

<bdi>○</bdi>Use the package keyword to define and import to use.

<bdi>●</bdi>Access Control:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use modifiers like public, protected, and default to manage visibility.

<bdi>●</bdi>Best Practices:

<bdi>○</bdi>Organize related classes into meaningful packages.

<bdi>○</bdi>Use nested packages for hierarchical projects.

## Exception Handling

Exception Handling in Java is a mechanism to handle runtime errors, ensuring normal program flow. Exceptions are events that disrupt the normal execution of a program. Java provides robust features to handle these exceptions and recover gracefully.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Key Concepts in Exception Handling

#### What is an Exception?

<bdi>●</bdi>An exception is an unwanted or unexpected event that occurs during the execution of a program.

<bdi>●</bdi>Example: Division by zero, invalid array index access.

#### What is Exception Handling?

<bdi>●</bdi>A process of responding to exceptions by catching and resolving them.

<bdi>●</bdi>Prevents abnormal termination of the program.

#### Hierarchy of Exceptions

<bdi>●</bdi>The root class of all exceptions is Throwable.

<bdi>●</bdi>Throwable has two subclasses:

1.  Exception: Recoverable issues (e.g., IOException, SQLException).

1.  Error: Irrecoverable issues (e.g., OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Exception Handling Keywords

| Keyword | Purpose |
| try | Defines a block of code to be tested for exceptions. |
| catch | Defines a block of code to handle the exception. |
| finally | Defines a block of code that executes whether an exception is handled or not. |
| throw | Used to explicitly throw an exception. |
| throws | Declares exceptions that a method can throw, allowing the caller to handle them. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Basic Structure

try {

// Code that may throw an exception

} catch (ExceptionType1 e1) {

// Handle ExceptionType1

} catch (ExceptionType2 e2) {

// Handle ExceptionType2

} finally {

// Code that will always execute (optional)

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Types of Exceptions

#### Checked Exceptions

<bdi>●</bdi>Checked at compile-time.

<bdi>●</bdi>Example: IOException, SQLException.

#### Unchecked Exceptions

<bdi>●</bdi>Occur at runtime (not checked at compile-time).

<bdi>●</bdi>Example: ArithmeticException, NullPointerException.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Code Examples

#### Example 1: Handling ArithmeticException

public class ArithmeticExceptionExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

try {

int result = 10 / 0; // This will throw ArithmeticException

System.out.println("Result: " + result);

} catch (ArithmeticException e) {

System.out.println("Error: Division by zero is not allowed.");

} finally {

System.out.println("Execution completed.");

}

}

}

#### Output:

Error: Division by zero is not allowed.

Execution completed.

#### Explanation:

1.  The try block attempts to divide by zero, throwing ArithmeticException.

1.  The catch block handles the exception and prints an error message.

1.  The finally block executes regardless of whether an exception occurred.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Example 2: Handling Multiple Exceptions

public class MultipleExceptionsExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

try {

int[] numbers = {1, 2, 3};

System.out.println(numbers[5]); // This will throw ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException

} catch (ArithmeticException e) {

System.out.println("Arithmetic Exception caught.");

} catch (ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException e) {

System.out.println("Array Index Out of Bounds Exception caught.");

} finally {

System.out.println("Execution completed.");

}

}

}

#### Output:

Array Index Out of Bounds Exception caught.

Execution completed.

#### Explanation:

1.  The try block attempts to access an invalid index, throwing ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException.

1.  The second catch block handles this specific exception.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Example 3: Using throw

public class ThrowExample {

public static void validateAge(int age) {

if (age < 18) {

throw new IllegalArgumentException("Age must be 18 or above.");

}

System.out.println("Valid age.");

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

try {

validateAge(15); // This will throw IllegalArgumentException

} catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {

System.out.println("Caught Exception: " + e.getMessage());

}

}

}

#### Output:

Caught Exception: Age must be 18 or above.

#### Explanation:

1.  The validateAge method explicitly throws IllegalArgumentException if the age is less than 18.

1.  The catch block handles the exception and prints the message.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Example 4: Using throws

import java.io.BufferedReader;

import java.io.FileReader;

import java.io.IOException;

public class ThrowsExample {

public static void readFile() throws IOException {

BufferedReader reader = new BufferedReader(new FileReader("nonexistent.txt"));

System.out.println(reader.readLine());

reader.close();

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

try {

readFile(); // This will throw IOException

} catch (IOException e) {

System.out.println("Caught IOException: " + e.getMessage());

}

}

}

#### Output:

Caught IOException: nonexistent.txt (No such file or directory)

#### Explanation:

1.  The readFile method declares throws IOException because it may throw an exception while accessing the file.

1.  The caller (main) handles the exception using a try-catch block.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Custom Exceptions

Custom exceptions allow developers to define application-specific error handling.

#### Code Example:

class InvalidAgeException extends Exception {

public InvalidAgeException(String message) {

super(message);

}

}

public class CustomExceptionExample {

public static void checkAge(int age) throws InvalidAgeException {

if (age < 18) {

throw new InvalidAgeException("Age must be 18 or above.");

}

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

try {

checkAge(16); // Throws custom exception

} catch (InvalidAgeException e) {

System.out.println("Caught Exception: " + e.getMessage());

}

}

}

#### Output:

Caught Exception: Age must be 18 or above.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 7\. Best Practices in Exception Handling

1.  Use Specific Exceptions:

<bdi>○</bdi>Catch specific exceptions instead of using Exception as a catch-all.

1.  Avoid Silent Failures:

<bdi>○</bdi>Do not leave the catch block empty.

1.  Use finally for Cleanup:

<bdi>○</bdi>Always release resources (e.g., close files or database connections) in the finally block.

1.  Throw Custom Exceptions:

<bdi>○</bdi>Create meaningful exception classes for application-specific issues.

1.  Log Exceptions:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use logging frameworks to capture stack traces for debugging.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Comparison: Checked vs Unchecked Exceptions

| Aspect | Checked Exceptions | Unchecked Exceptions |
| Definition | Checked at compile-time. | Occur at runtime (not checked). |
| Examples | IOException, SQLException. | ArithmeticException, NullPointerException. |
| Declaration | Must be declared with throws. | No need for declaration. |
| Recoverability | Typically recoverable. | Often indicates a programming error. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Keywords:

<bdi>○</bdi>try, catch, finally, throw, throws.

<bdi>●</bdi>Exception Types:

<bdi>○</bdi>Checked (compile-time) and Unchecked (runtime).

<bdi>●</bdi>Custom Exceptions:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use for application-specific scenarios.

<bdi>●</bdi>Best Practices:

<bdi>○</bdi>Catch specific exceptions, use finally for cleanup, and avoid silent failures.

## Java Collections Framework

The Java Collections Framework is a unified architecture for storing and manipulating groups of objects. It provides several interfaces, classes, and algorithms to manage collections of objects efficiently. Below is an overview:

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Core Concepts

<bdi>●</bdi>Collection: A group of objects (elements), such as lists, sets, or queues.

<bdi>●</bdi>Framework Components:

<bdi>○</bdi>Interfaces: Define the abstract data types (e.g., List, Set, Map).

<bdi>○</bdi>Implementations: Concrete classes implementing the interfaces (e.g., ArrayList, HashSet, HashMap).

<bdi>○</bdi>Algorithms: Utility methods (e.g., sort, shuffle) provided by the Collections class.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Main Interfaces

#### a. Collection Interface

The root interface for most collection types, with key subinterfaces:

<bdi>●</bdi>List: An ordered collection (e.g., ArrayList, LinkedList).

<bdi>●</bdi>Set: A collection that doesn't allow duplicate elements (e.g., HashSet, TreeSet).

<bdi>●</bdi>Queue: A collection for holding elements prior to processing, typically in FIFO order (e.g., PriorityQueue).

<bdi>●</bdi>Deque: A double-ended queue allowing insertion/removal at both ends (e.g., ArrayDeque).

#### b. Map Interface

Not a subtype of Collection. Represents key-value pairs:

<bdi>●</bdi>Implementations: HashMap, TreeMap, LinkedHashMap.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Key Classes

#### a. List Implementations

1.  ArrayList:

<bdi>○</bdi>Backed by a dynamic array.

<bdi>○</bdi>Fast for random access.

<bdi>○</bdi>Slower for insertions/deletions in the middle.

1.  LinkedList:

<bdi>○</bdi>Backed by a doubly-linked list.

<bdi>○</bdi>Fast for insertions/deletions in the middle.

<bdi>○</bdi>Slower for random access.

1.  Vector:

<bdi>○</bdi>Synchronized version of ArrayList.

1.  Stack:

<bdi>○</bdi>A subclass of Vector implementing LIFO behavior.

#### b. Set Implementations

1.  HashSet:

<bdi>○</bdi>Backed by a hash table.

<bdi>○</bdi>No guarantees about the order of elements.

1.  LinkedHashSet:

<bdi>○</bdi>Maintains insertion order.

<bdi>○</bdi>Backed by a hash table and a linked list.

1.  TreeSet:

<bdi>○</bdi>Backed by a red-black tree.

<bdi>○</bdi>Maintains elements in sorted order.

#### c. Map Implementations

1.  HashMap:

<bdi>○</bdi>Backed by a hash table.

<bdi>○</bdi>Does not guarantee order.

1.  LinkedHashMap:

<bdi>○</bdi>Maintains insertion order.

1.  TreeMap:

<bdi>○</bdi>Backed by a red-black tree.

<bdi>○</bdi>Maintains keys in sorted order.

1.  Hashtable:

<bdi>○</bdi>Legacy synchronized implementation.

#### d. Queue/Deque Implementations

1.  PriorityQueue:

<bdi>○</bdi>Orders elements based on natural ordering or a comparator.

1.  ArrayDeque:

<bdi>○</bdi>Resizable array-based implementation for Deque.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Utility Classes

1.  Collections: Contains static methods for:

<bdi>○</bdi>Sorting (sort)

<bdi>○</bdi>Searching (binarySearch)

<bdi>○</bdi>Synchronizing collections (synchronizedList)

<bdi>○</bdi>Making collections immutable (unmodifiableList).

1.  Arrays: Contains static methods for working with arrays as collections.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Framework Architecture

<bdi>●</bdi>Interfaces: Define the ADTs (Collection, List, Set, Map, etc.).

<bdi>●</bdi>Abstract Classes: Provide skeletal implementations (AbstractList, AbstractSet).

<bdi>●</bdi>Concrete Classes: Provide actual implementations (ArrayList, HashMap).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Advantages

1.  Standardization: Unified way to handle collections.

1.  Reusability: Pre-built data structures save development time.

1.  Interoperability: Algorithms work seamlessly with any collection implementation.

1.  Flexibility: Easy switching between implementations (e.g., ArrayList to LinkedList).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 7\. Common Operations

#### Example: ArrayList

import java.util.ArrayList;

import java.util.Collections;

public class CollectionsExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

ArrayList<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>();

list.add(5);

list.add(2);

list.add(8);

// Sorting

Collections.sort(list);

System.out.println("Sorted List: " + list);

// Searching

int index = Collections.binarySearch(list, 8);

System.out.println("Index of 8: " + index);

}

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 8\. Best Practices

1.  Prefer List or Set over raw array for flexibility.

1.  Use HashMap for fast lookups but avoid relying on its order.

1.  Always synchronize explicitly when accessing collections in multithreaded environments.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 9\. Summary Table

| Interface | Implementation | Characteristics |
| List | ArrayList | Dynamic array, fast random access |
|  | LinkedList | Doubly-linked list, fast insertions/deletions |
| Set | HashSet | No duplicates, unordered |
|  | TreeSet | Sorted order, no duplicates |
| Queue | PriorityQueue | Priority-based processing |
| Map | HashMap | Key-value pairs, unordered |
|  | TreeMap | Key-value pairs, sorted by key |

## ArrayList

Here’s a detailed explanation of the ArrayList in Java, accompanied by example code with thorough explanations for both the code and its output.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### What is an ArrayList?

<bdi>●</bdi>Definition: ArrayList is a part of the Java Collections Framework and implements the List interface.

<bdi>●</bdi>Key Characteristics:

1.  It is a resizable array.

1.  Allows duplicate elements.

1.  Maintains insertion order.

1.  Provides random access to elements using an index.

1.  Not synchronized (but can be synchronized manually using Collections.synchronizedList).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Methods in ArrayList

<bdi>●</bdi>add(E e): Adds an element to the list.

<bdi>●</bdi>add(int index, E e): Adds an element at the specified index.

<bdi>●</bdi>remove(Object o) or remove(int index): Removes an element by value or index.

<bdi>●</bdi>get(int index): Retrieves an element by index.

<bdi>●</bdi>set(int index, E e): Replaces an element at the specified index.

<bdi>●</bdi>contains(Object o): Checks if the list contains a specific element.

<bdi>●</bdi>size(): Returns the number of elements in the list.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Example Code

import java.util.ArrayList;

import java.util.Collections;

public class ArrayListExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Step 1: Create an ArrayList

ArrayList<String> fruits = new ArrayList<>();

// Step 2: Add elements to the ArrayList

fruits.add("Apple");

fruits.add("Banana");

fruits.add("Cherry");

fruits.add("Apple"); // Duplicate

// Step 3: Access elements

System.out.println("Element at index 1: " + fruits.get(1));

// Step 4: Modify an element

fruits.set(1, "Blueberry");

System.out.println("After modifying index 1: " + fruits);

// Step 5: Remove elements

fruits.remove("Apple"); // Removes the first occurrence

System.out.println("After removing 'Apple': " + fruits);

// Step 6: Check for an element

boolean containsCherry = fruits.contains("Cherry");

System.out.println("Does the list contain 'Cherry'? " + containsCherry);

// Step 7: Sort the ArrayList

Collections.sort(fruits);

System.out.println("Sorted ArrayList: " + fruits);

// Step 8: Get the size of the ArrayList

System.out.println("Size of the ArrayList: " + fruits.size());

}

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Explanation of Code

Creating an ArrayList: ArrayList<String> fruits = new ArrayList<>();

An ArrayList of String type is created, allowing storage of string elements.

Adding Elements: fruits.add("Apple");

fruits.add("Banana");

fruits.add("Cherry");

fruits.add("Apple");

Adds elements to the ArrayList.

The duplicate "Apple" is allowed because ArrayList supports duplicates.

Accessing an Element: System.out.println("Element at index 1: " + fruits.get(1));

Retrieves the element at index 1 using the get() method.

Indexing starts from 0.

Modifying an Element: fruits.set(1, "Blueberry");

Replaces the element at index 1 with "Blueberry" using the set() method.

Removing an Element: fruits.remove("Apple");
