Removes the first occurrence of "Apple" from the list using the remove(Object o) method.

Checking for an Element: boolean containsCherry = fruits.contains("Cherry");

Checks if "Cherry" exists in the list using the contains() method.

Sorting the ArrayList: Collections.sort(fruits);

Sorts the elements in the list in natural order using Collections.sort().

Getting the Size: System.out.println("Size of the ArrayList: " + fruits.size());

Returns the number of elements in the list using size().

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Explanation of Output

#### Output:

Element at index 1: Banana

After modifying index 1: [Apple, Blueberry, Cherry, Apple]

After removing 'Apple': [Blueberry, Cherry, Apple]

Does the list contain 'Cherry'? true

Sorted ArrayList: [Apple, Blueberry, Cherry]

Size of the ArrayList: 3

1.  Element at Index 1:

<bdi>○</bdi>The second element is "Banana", retrieved using get(1).

1.  After Modifying Index 1:

"Banana" is replaced with "Blueberry". The list becomes: [Apple, Blueberry, Cherry, Apple]

1.  After Removing "Apple":

The first occurrence of "Apple" is removed. The list becomes: [Blueberry, Cherry, Apple]

1.  Checking for "Cherry":

<bdi>○</bdi>"Cherry" is in the list, so contains("Cherry") returns true.

1.  Sorted ArrayList:

The list is sorted alphabetically. The final order is: [Apple, Blueberry, Cherry]

1.  Size of the ArrayList:

<bdi>○</bdi>After sorting, the list contains three elements, so size() returns 3.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Advantages of ArrayList

1.  Dynamic Resizing: Automatically grows as elements are added.

1.  Ease of Use: Provides built-in methods for common operations.

1.  Random Access: Fast element retrieval using indexes.

### Disadvantages

1.  Not Thread-Safe: Use Collections.synchronizedList() for thread safety.

1.  Slow Insertions/Deletions: Adding/removing elements in the middle can be costly due to shifting.

## LinkedList

A LinkedList is a linear data structure where elements (nodes) are linked using pointers. Java provides a built-in LinkedList class in the java.util package that implements the List, Deque, and Queue interfaces.

Unlike an ArrayList, which uses a dynamic array for storage, a LinkedList uses a doubly linked list structure. This difference makes LinkedList more efficient for certain operations, such as insertions and deletions.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of LinkedList

1.  Dynamic Size: Unlike arrays, the size of a LinkedList is not fixed.

1.  Efficient Insertions/Deletions:

<bdi>○</bdi>Insertions and deletions are faster than in ArrayList, especially at the beginning or middle.

1.  Doubly Linked: Each node contains pointers to the previous and next node.

1.  Implements Multiple Interfaces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Implements the List interface for ordered collections.

<bdi>○</bdi>Implements the Deque and Queue interfaces for queue-like operations.

1.  Allows Null Elements: Similar to other List implementations, it supports null as an element.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Hierarchy

LinkedList is part of the Java Collections Framework:

java.lang.Object

↳ java.util.AbstractCollection

↳ java.util.AbstractList

↳ java.util.LinkedList

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Basic Operations on LinkedList

#### Code Example:

import java.util.LinkedList;

public class LinkedListExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Create a LinkedList

LinkedList<String> names = new LinkedList<>();

// Add elements

names.add("Alice");

names.add("Bob");

names.add("Charlie");

System.out.println("Initial LinkedList: " + names);

// Add element at the beginning

names.addFirst("Zara");

System.out.println("After addFirst: " + names);

// Add element at the end

names.addLast("Daisy");

System.out.println("After addLast: " + names);

// Remove an element

names.remove("Bob");

System.out.println("After removing 'Bob': " + names);

// Access elements

System.out.println("First Element: " + names.getFirst());

System.out.println("Last Element: " + names.getLast());

}

}

#### Output:

Initial LinkedList: [Alice, Bob, Charlie]

After addFirst: [Zara, Alice, Bob, Charlie]

After addLast: [Zara, Alice, Bob, Charlie, Daisy]

After removing 'Bob': [Zara, Alice, Charlie, Daisy]

First Element: Zara

Last Element: Daisy

#### Explanation:

1.  Add Elements:

<bdi>○</bdi>add(), addFirst(), and addLast() are used to insert elements at different positions.

1.  Remove Elements:

<bdi>○</bdi>remove("Bob") removes the specified element from the list.

1.  Access Elements:

<bdi>○</bdi>getFirst() and getLast() retrieve the first and last elements of the list.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Iterating Through a LinkedList

#### Code Example:

import java.util.LinkedList;

import java.util.Iterator;

public class LinkedListIteration {

public static void main(String[] args) {

LinkedList<Integer> numbers = new LinkedList<>();

numbers.add(10);

numbers.add(20);

numbers.add(30);

numbers.add(40);

// Using a for-each loop

System.out.println("Using for-each loop:");

for (int number : numbers) {

System.out.println(number);

}

// Using an Iterator

System.out.println("\nUsing Iterator:");

Iterator<Integer> iterator = numbers.iterator();

while (iterator.hasNext()) {

System.out.println(iterator.next());

}

}

}

#### Output:

Using for-each loop:

10

20

30

40

Using Iterator:

10

20

30

40

#### Explanation:

1.  For-each Loop:

<bdi>○</bdi>Simplifies iteration for collections implementing the Iterable interface.

1.  Iterator:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides a more traditional way to traverse elements, useful for concurrent modifications.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Using LinkedList as a Queue

The LinkedList class implements the Deque and Queue interfaces, making it suitable for queue-like operations.

#### Code Example:

import java.util.LinkedList;

public class LinkedListAsQueue {

public static void main(String[] args) {

LinkedList<String> queue = new LinkedList<>();

// Add elements to the queue

queue.offer("Task1");

queue.offer("Task2");

queue.offer("Task3");

System.out.println("Queue: " + queue);

// Peek at the front element

System.out.println("Front Element: " + queue.peek());

// Process and remove elements from the queue

while (!queue.isEmpty()) {

System.out.println("Processing: " + queue.poll());

}

System.out.println("Queue after processing: " + queue);

}

}

#### Output:

Queue: [Task1, Task2, Task3]

Front Element: Task1

Processing: Task1

Processing: Task2

Processing: Task3

Queue after processing: []

#### Explanation:

1.  offer():

<bdi>○</bdi>Adds elements to the end of the queue.

1.  peek():

<bdi>○</bdi>Retrieves the front element without removing it.

1.  poll():

<bdi>○</bdi>Retrieves and removes the front element.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Using LinkedList as a Deque

A Deque (Double-Ended Queue) allows elements to be added or removed from both ends.

#### Code Example:

import java.util.LinkedList;

public class LinkedListAsDeque {

public static void main(String[] args) {

LinkedList<String> deque = new LinkedList<>();

// Add elements to both ends

deque.addFirst("Front");

deque.addLast("Back");

System.out.println("Deque: " + deque);

// Remove elements from both ends

System.out.println("Removed from front: " + deque.removeFirst());

System.out.println("Removed from back: " + deque.removeLast());

System.out.println("Deque after removals: " + deque);

}

}

#### Output:

Deque: [Front, Back]

Removed from front: Front

Removed from back: Back

Deque after removals: []

#### Explanation:

1.  addFirst() and addLast():

<bdi>○</bdi>Add elements to the front and back of the deque.

1.  removeFirst() and removeLast():

<bdi>○</bdi>Remove elements from the front and back.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Comparison: LinkedList vs ArrayList

| Aspect | LinkedList | ArrayList |
| Data Structure | Doubly linked list. | Dynamic array. |
| Insertion/Deletion | Faster for frequent insertions/deletions, especially at the beginning or middle. | Slower due to shifting of elements. |
| Access Time | Slower for random access (O(n) complexity). | Faster for random access (O(1) complexity). |
| Memory Usage | Higher due to extra pointers. | Lower as it uses contiguous memory. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Best Practices and Use Cases

1.  When to Use LinkedList:

<bdi>○</bdi>Frequent insertions and deletions at the beginning or middle of the list.

<bdi>○</bdi>Use as a queue or deque.

1.  When to Avoid LinkedList:

<bdi>○</bdi>Random access is required frequently.

<bdi>○</bdi>Memory overhead is a concern.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Key Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>add(), addFirst(), addLast(), remove(), getFirst(), getLast().

<bdi>○</bdi>offer(), poll(), peek() for queue operations.

<bdi>○</bdi>addFirst() and removeLast() for deque operations.

<bdi>●</bdi>Efficiency:

<bdi>○</bdi>Faster for insertions/deletions.

<bdi>○</bdi>Slower for random access compared to ArrayList.

## TreeSet

A TreeSet is a part of Java's java.util package and is a member of the Java Collections Framework. It implements the NavigableSet interface, which extends SortedSet. A TreeSet stores elements in a sorted order (natural or custom) and ensures no duplicates.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of TreeSet

1.  Sorted Order:

<bdi>○</bdi>Maintains elements in their natural order or a custom order defined by a Comparator.

1.  Unique Elements:

<bdi>○</bdi>Does not allow duplicate elements.

1.  Balanced Tree:

<bdi>○</bdi>Internally uses a Red-Black Tree for storing elements, providing efficient performance.

1.  Performance:

<bdi>○</bdi>Operations like add, remove, and search take O(log n) time.

1.  NavigableSet Features:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides methods to navigate through the set (higher, lower, ceiling, floor).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### TreeSet Class Hierarchy

java.lang.Object

↳ java.util.AbstractCollection

↳ java.util.AbstractSet

↳ java.util.TreeSet

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Constructors

| Constructor | Description |
| TreeSet() | Creates an empty TreeSet with natural ordering. |
| TreeSet(Comparator<? super E> comp) | Creates an empty TreeSet with a custom comparator. |
| TreeSet(Collection<? extends E> col) | Creates a TreeSet containing elements from the specified collection. |
| TreeSet(SortedSet<E> set) | Creates a TreeSet containing elements from the specified sorted set. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Basic Operations with TreeSet

#### Code Example:

import java.util.TreeSet;

public class TreeSetExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

TreeSet<Integer> numbers = new TreeSet<>();

// Add elements

numbers.add(50);

numbers.add(20);

numbers.add(10);

numbers.add(40);

numbers.add(30);

System.out.println("TreeSet: " + numbers);

// Remove an element

numbers.remove(20);

System.out.println("After removing 20: " + numbers);

// Check for an element

System.out.println("Contains 30? " + numbers.contains(30));

// Get the first and last elements

System.out.println("First Element: " + numbers.first());

System.out.println("Last Element: " + numbers.last());

}

}

#### Output:

TreeSet: [10, 20, 30, 40, 50]

After removing 20: [10, 30, 40, 50]

Contains 30? true

First Element: 10

Last Element: 50

#### Explanation:

1.  Sorted Order:

<bdi>○</bdi>The elements are stored in ascending order (natural ordering).

1.  Remove and Contains:

<bdi>○</bdi>remove(20) removes the specified element.

<bdi>○</bdi>contains(30) checks for the presence of an element.

1.  First and Last Elements:

<bdi>○</bdi>first() and last() retrieve the smallest and largest elements, respectively.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. TreeSet with Custom Comparator

You can use a custom comparator to define the order of elements.

#### Code Example:

import java.util.TreeSet;

import java.util.Comparator;

public class CustomComparatorExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Custom comparator for descending order

TreeSet<Integer> numbers = new TreeSet<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());

numbers.add(10);

numbers.add(20);

numbers.add(30);

numbers.add(40);

System.out.println("TreeSet in Descending Order: " + numbers);

}

}

#### Output:

TreeSet in Descending Order: [40, 30, 20, 10]

#### Explanation:

1.  Custom Comparator:

<bdi>○</bdi>Comparator.reverseOrder() sorts elements in descending order.

1.  TreeSet Construction:

<bdi>○</bdi>The comparator is passed to the TreeSet constructor to define the order.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Navigating a TreeSet

The TreeSet class provides methods to navigate elements:

| Method | Description |
| higher(e) | Returns the smallest element strictly greater than e. |
| lower(e) | Returns the largest element strictly less than e. |
| ceiling(e) | Returns the smallest element greater than or equal to e. |
| floor(e) | Returns the largest element less than or equal to e. |

#### Code Example:

import java.util.TreeSet;

public class NavigableTreeSetExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

TreeSet<Integer> numbers = new TreeSet<>();

numbers.add(10);

numbers.add(20);

numbers.add(30);

numbers.add(40);

System.out.println("TreeSet: " + numbers);

System.out.println("Higher than 25: " + numbers.higher(25));

System.out.println("Lower than 25: " + numbers.lower(25));

System.out.println("Ceiling of 25: " + numbers.ceiling(25));

System.out.println("Floor of 25: " + numbers.floor(25));

}

}

#### Output:

TreeSet: [10, 20, 30, 40]

Higher than 25: 30

Lower than 25: 20

Ceiling of 25: 30

Floor of 25: 20

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Iterating Through a TreeSet

#### Code Example:

import java.util.TreeSet;

public class TreeSetIteration {

public static void main(String[] args) {

TreeSet<String> fruits = new TreeSet<>();

fruits.add("Apple");

fruits.add("Banana");

fruits.add("Cherry");

// Using a for-each loop

System.out.println("Iterating with for-each loop:");

for (String fruit : fruits) {

System.out.println(fruit);

}

// Using an iterator

System.out.println("\nIterating with iterator:");

var iterator = fruits.iterator();

while (iterator.hasNext()) {

System.out.println(iterator.next());

}

}

}

#### Output:

Iterating with for-each loop:

Apple

Banana

Cherry

Iterating with iterator:

Apple

Banana

Cherry

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. TreeSet vs HashSet

| Aspect | TreeSet | HashSet |
| Ordering | Maintains sorted order. | Does not guarantee order. |
| Performance | Slower (O(log n) for add/remove). | Faster (O(1) for add/remove). |
| Use Case | When sorted data is required. | When fast operations are needed. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. TreeSet with Objects

You can use custom objects with TreeSet by implementing Comparable or using a Comparator.

#### Code Example:

import java.util.TreeSet;

class Person implements Comparable<Person> {

String name;

int age;

Person(String name, int age) {

this.name = name;

this.age = age;

}

@Override

public int compareTo(Person other) {

return Integer.compare(this.age, other.age);

}

@Override

public String toString() {

return name + " (" + age + ")";

}

}

public class TreeSetWithObjects {

public static void main(String[] args) {

TreeSet<Person> people = new TreeSet<>();

people.add(new Person("Alice", 30));

people.add(new Person("Bob", 25));

people.add(new Person("Charlie", 35));

System.out.println("People TreeSet: " + people);

}

}

#### Output:

People TreeSet: [Bob (25), Alice (30), Charlie (35)]

#### Explanation:

1.  Custom Comparable:

<bdi>○</bdi>The Person class implements Comparable to define sorting by age.

1.  TreeSet with Objects:

<bdi>○</bdi>The TreeSet automatically sorts Person objects based on the compareTo method.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices and Use Cases

1.  When to Use TreeSet:

<bdi>○</bdi>When sorted data is required.

<bdi>○</bdi>For range queries (subSet, headSet, tailSet).

1.  When to Avoid TreeSet:

<bdi>○</bdi>If fast operations are required (use HashSet instead).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Key Features:

<bdi>○</bdi>Maintains sorted, unique elements.

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides efficient navigation and range-based operations.

<bdi>●</bdi>Performance:

<bdi>○</bdi>Insertions, deletions, and lookups are O(log n).

<bdi>●</bdi>Common Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>add(), remove(), first(), last(), higher(), lower().

## HashMap

HashMap is a part of the java.util package in Java. It implements the Map interface, which allows you to store key-value pairs. HashMap is one of the most commonly used data structures because it provides fast access, insertion, and deletion using keys.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of HashMap

1.  Key-Value Pairs:

<bdi>○</bdi>Each key is mapped to a specific value.

<bdi>○</bdi>Keys must be unique, but values can be duplicated.

1.  Fast Performance:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides O(1) time complexity for basic operations like get() and put(), assuming a good hash function.

1.  Allows Nulls:

<bdi>○</bdi>Permits null values and a single null key.

1.  Not Synchronized:

<bdi>○</bdi>Not thread-safe by default, making it faster but requiring synchronization in multi-threaded environments.

1.  Unordered:

<bdi>○</bdi>Does not maintain insertion order; elements are stored based on hash codes.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### HashMap Class Hierarchy

java.lang.Object

↳ java.util.AbstractMap

↳ java.util.HashMap

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Constructors

| Constructor | Description |
| HashMap() | Creates an empty HashMap with a default capacity (16) and load factor (0.75). |
| HashMap(int initialCapacity) | Creates a HashMap with the specified initial capacity. |
| HashMap(int initialCapacity, float loadFactor) | Creates a HashMap with the specified initial capacity and load factor. |
| HashMap(Map<? extends K, ? extends V> m) | Creates a HashMap initialized with the entries of the specified map. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Basic Operations

#### Code Example:

import java.util.HashMap;

public class HashMapExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Create a HashMap

HashMap<Integer, String> map = new HashMap<>();

// Add key-value pairs

map.put(1, "Alice");

map.put(2, "Bob");

map.put(3, "Charlie");

System.out.println("Initial HashMap: " + map);

// Get a value

System.out.println("Value for key 2: " + map.get(2));

// Remove a key-value pair

map.remove(1);

System.out.println("After removing key 1: " + map);

// Check if a key exists

System.out.println("Contains key 3? " + map.containsKey(3));

// Check if a value exists

System.out.println("Contains value 'Bob'? " + map.containsValue("Bob"));

// Size of the HashMap

System.out.println("Size of HashMap: " + map.size());

}

}

#### Output:

Initial HashMap: {1=Alice, 2=Bob, 3=Charlie}

Value for key 2: Bob

After removing key 1: {2=Bob, 3=Charlie}

Contains key 3? true

Contains value 'Bob'? true

Size of HashMap: 2

#### Explanation:

1.  Add Key-Value Pairs:

<bdi>○</bdi>put(1, "Alice") adds a mapping for key 1 to the value "Alice".

1.  Access Values:

<bdi>○</bdi>get(2) retrieves the value associated with key 2.

1.  Remove Entries:

<bdi>○</bdi>remove(1) deletes the key-value pair for key 1.

1.  Check Existence:

<bdi>○</bdi>containsKey(3) checks if key 3 exists.

<bdi>○</bdi>containsValue("Bob") checks if the value "Bob" exists.

1.  Size:

<bdi>○</bdi>size() returns the number of key-value pairs.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Iterating Through a HashMap

#### Code Example:

import java.util.HashMap;

import java.util.Map;

public class HashMapIteration {

public static void main(String[] args) {

HashMap<String, Integer> scores = new HashMap<>();

scores.put("Alice", 85);

scores.put("Bob", 92);

scores.put("Charlie", 78);

// Using a for-each loop on entrySet()

System.out.println("Using entrySet:");

for (Map.Entry<String, Integer> entry : scores.entrySet()) {

System.out.println(entry.getKey() + ": " + entry.getValue());

}

// Using keySet() and values()

System.out.println("\nUsing keySet and values:");

for (String key : scores.keySet()) {

System.out.println(key + ": " + scores.get(key));

}

}

}

#### Output:

Using entrySet:

Alice: 85

Bob: 92

Charlie: 78

Using keySet and values:

Alice: 85

Bob: 92

Charlie: 78

#### Explanation:

1.  entrySet():

<bdi>○</bdi>Iterates over the set of key-value pairs.

1.  keySet() and values():

<bdi>○</bdi>Iterates over keys and retrieves values using get().

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Handling Null Keys and Values

#### Code Example:

import java.util.HashMap;

public class HashMapNullValues {

public static void main(String[] args) {

HashMap<String, String> map = new HashMap<>();

// Adding null key and null values

map.put(null, "NullKey");

map.put("Key1", null);

map.put("Key2", "Value2");

System.out.println("HashMap with nulls: " + map);

// Access null key

System.out.println("Value for null key: " + map.get(null));

}

}

#### Output:

HashMap with nulls: {null=NullKey, Key1=null, Key2=Value2}

Value for null key: NullKey

#### Explanation:

1.  Null Key:

<bdi>○</bdi>A single null key is allowed.

1.  Null Values:

<bdi>○</bdi>Multiple null values are permitted.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Replacing Values

#### Code Example:

import java.util.HashMap;

public class HashMapReplace {

public static void main(String[] args) {

HashMap<String, String> map = new HashMap<>();

map.put("Key1", "Value1");

map.put("Key2", "Value2");

System.out.println("Before replace: " + map);

// Replace value for a key

map.replace("Key1", "UpdatedValue1");

System.out.println("After replace: " + map);

}

}

#### Output:

Before replace: {Key1=Value1, Key2=Value2}

After replace: {Key1=UpdatedValue1, Key2=Value2}

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The replace() method updates the value for a specific key.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. HashMap vs TreeMap vs LinkedHashMap

| Aspect | HashMap | TreeMap | LinkedHashMap |
| Ordering | No ordering. | Sorted order (natural or custom). | Insertion order maintained. |
| Performance | Best for fast access (O(1)). | Slower due to sorting (O(log n)). | Similar to HashMap with ordering (O(1)). |
| Null Handling | Allows one null key and multiple null values. | Does not allow null keys. | Allows one null key and multiple null values. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. HashMap with Custom Objects

#### Code Example:

import java.util.HashMap;

class Person {

String name;

int age;

Person(String name, int age) {

this.name = name;

this.age = age;

}

@Override

public String toString() {

return name + " (" + age + ")";

}

}

public class HashMapCustomObjects {

public static void main(String[] args) {

HashMap<Integer, Person> people = new HashMap<>();

people.put(1, new Person("Alice", 30));

people.put(2, new Person("Bob", 25));

System.out.println("HashMap with custom objects:");

for (var entry : people.entrySet()) {

System.out.println(entry.getKey() + ": " + entry.getValue());

}

}

}

#### Output:

HashMap with custom objects:

1: Alice (30)

2: Bob (25)

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The Person class is used as a value in the HashMap.

<bdi>●</bdi>The toString() method defines how the object is printed.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices

1.  Hashing Function:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensure keys have proper hashCode() and equals() implementations for consistent behavior.

1.  Avoid Null Keys in Multi-threaded Environments:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use ConcurrentHashMap to avoid null-related issues.

1.  Use Initial Capacity Wisely:

<bdi>○</bdi>Avoid frequent resizing by setting an appropriate initial capacity.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Key Features:

<bdi>○</bdi>Stores unique keys with associated values.

<bdi>○</bdi>Allows null key and values.

<bdi>○</bdi>Unordered storage with fast operations.

<bdi>●</bdi>Common Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>put(), get(), remove(), replace(), containsKey(), entrySet().

<bdi>●</bdi>Use Cases:

<bdi>○</bdi>Fast lookups.

<bdi>○</bdi>Caching.

<bdi>○</bdi>Key-value data storage.

## TreeMap

A TreeMap in Java is a part of the java.util package and implements the Map interface. It is a collection that stores key-value pairs in a sorted order, either based on the natural ordering of the keys or a custom Comparator.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of TreeMap

1.  Sorted Order:

<bdi>○</bdi>Maintains the keys in ascending order by default (natural ordering).

<bdi>○</bdi>A custom sorting order can be defined using a Comparator.

1.  NavigableMap:

<bdi>○</bdi>TreeMap implements the NavigableMap interface, providing navigation methods like ceilingKey, floorKey, higherKey, and lowerKey.

1.  Efficient Operations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Backed by a Red-Black Tree, providing O(log n) time complexity for put, get, remove, and navigation operations.

1.  Unique Keys:

<bdi>○</bdi>Keys must be unique, and null is not allowed as a key.

1.  Thread Safety:

<bdi>○</bdi>Not thread-safe; use Collections.synchronizedMap or ConcurrentSkipListMap for concurrent scenarios.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Hierarchy

java.lang.Object

↳ java.util.AbstractMap

↳ java.util.TreeMap

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Constructors

| Constructor | Description |
| TreeMap() | Creates an empty TreeMap with natural ordering. |
| TreeMap(Comparator<? super K> comp) | Creates an empty TreeMap with the specified comparator for ordering. |
| TreeMap(Map<? extends K, ? extends V> m) | Creates a TreeMap containing the same mappings as the specified map. |
| TreeMap(SortedMap<K, ? extends V> m) | Creates a TreeMap with the mappings and ordering of the given SortedMap. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Basic Operations with TreeMap

#### Code Example:

import java.util.TreeMap;

public class TreeMapExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Create a TreeMap

TreeMap<Integer, String> map = new TreeMap<>();

// Add key-value pairs

map.put(3, "Three");

map.put(1, "One");

map.put(4, "Four");

map.put(2, "Two");

System.out.println("Initial TreeMap: " + map);

// Access value for a key

System.out.println("Value for key 2: " + map.get(2));

// Remove a key-value pair

map.remove(3);

System.out.println("After removing key 3: " + map);

// Check for a key and a value

System.out.println("Contains key 4? " + map.containsKey(4));

System.out.println("Contains value 'Five'? " + map.containsValue("Five"));

// Size of the TreeMap

System.out.println("Size of TreeMap: " + map.size());

}

}

#### Output:

Initial TreeMap: {1=One, 2=Two, 3=Three, 4=Four}

Value for key 2: Two

After removing key 3: {1=One, 2=Two, 4=Four}

Contains key 4? true

Contains value 'Five'? false

Size of TreeMap: 3

#### Explanation:

1.  Sorted Order:

<bdi>○</bdi>The keys are stored in ascending order (natural ordering).

1.  Add and Access:

<bdi>○</bdi>put(3, "Three") adds a key-value pair.

<bdi>○</bdi>get(2) retrieves the value for key 2.

1.  Remove and Check:

<bdi>○</bdi>remove(3) deletes the key-value pair for key 3.

<bdi>○</bdi>containsKey(4) and containsValue("Five") check for the presence of a key and value, respectively.

1.  Size:

<bdi>○</bdi>size() returns the number of key-value pairs.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Custom Ordering with Comparator

#### Code Example:

import java.util.TreeMap;

import java.util.Comparator;

public class CustomOrderingExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// TreeMap with a custom comparator (descending order)

TreeMap<Integer, String> map = new TreeMap<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());

map.put(1, "One");

map.put(3, "Three");

map.put(2, "Two");

System.out.println("TreeMap in Descending Order: " + map);

}

}

#### Output:

TreeMap in Descending Order: {3=Three, 2=Two, 1=One}

#### Explanation:

1.  Custom Comparator:

<bdi>○</bdi>The Comparator.reverseOrder() sorts the keys in descending order.

1.  TreeMap Construction:

<bdi>○</bdi>The comparator is passed to the TreeMap constructor, affecting how the keys are stored.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Navigating a TreeMap

TreeMap provides methods for retrieving entries based on keys.

| Method | Description |
| higherKey(k) | Returns the smallest key strictly greater than k. |
| lowerKey(k) | Returns the largest key strictly less than k. |
| ceilingKey(k) | Returns the smallest key greater than or equal to k. |
| floorKey(k) | Returns the largest key less than or equal to k. |

#### Code Example:

import java.util.TreeMap;

public class TreeMapNavigation {

public static void main(String[] args) {

TreeMap<Integer, String> map = new TreeMap<>();

map.put(10, "Ten");

map.put(20, "Twenty");

map.put(30, "Thirty");

map.put(40, "Forty");

System.out.println("TreeMap: " + map);

System.out.println("Higher Key than 25: " + map.higherKey(25));

System.out.println("Lower Key than 25: " + map.lowerKey(25));

System.out.println("Ceiling Key of 25: " + map.ceilingKey(25));

System.out.println("Floor Key of 25: " + map.floorKey(25));

}

}

#### Output:

TreeMap: {10=Ten, 20=Twenty, 30=Thirty, 40=Forty}

Higher Key than 25: 30

Lower Key than 25: 20

Ceiling Key of 25: 30

Floor Key of 25: 20

#### Explanation:

1.  Navigation Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>higherKey(25) finds the smallest key strictly greater than 25.

<bdi>○</bdi>lowerKey(25) finds the largest key strictly less than 25.

<bdi>○</bdi>ceilingKey(25) and floorKey(25) find keys greater/less than or equal to 25.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Iterating Through a TreeMap

#### Code Example:

import java.util.TreeMap;

import java.util.Map;

public class TreeMapIteration {

public static void main(String[] args) {

TreeMap<String, Integer> map = new TreeMap<>();

map.put("Alice", 30);

map.put("Bob", 25);

map.put("Charlie", 35);

// Using for-each loop on entrySet()

System.out.println("Using entrySet:");

for (Map.Entry<String, Integer> entry : map.entrySet()) {

System.out.println(entry.getKey() + ": " + entry.getValue());

}

// Using keySet()

System.out.println("\nUsing keySet:");

for (String key : map.keySet()) {

System.out.println(key + ": " + map.get(key));

}

}

}

#### Output:

Using entrySet:

Alice: 30

Bob: 25

Charlie: 35

Using keySet:

Alice: 30

Bob: 25

Charlie: 35

#### Explanation:

1.  Entry Set:

<bdi>○</bdi>Iterates over key-value pairs.

1.  Key Set:

<bdi>○</bdi>Iterates over keys and retrieves values using get().

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. TreeMap with Custom Objects

#### Code Example:

import java.util.TreeMap;

class Person implements Comparable<Person> {

String name;

int age;

Person(String name, int age) {

this.name = name;

this.age = age;

}

@Override

public int compareTo(Person other) {

return Integer.compare(this.age, other.age);

}

@Override

public String toString() {

return name + " (" + age + ")";

}

}

public class TreeMapCustomObjects {

public static void main(String[] args) {

TreeMap<Person, String> map = new TreeMap<>();

map.put(new Person("Alice", 30), "Engineer");

map.put(new Person("Bob", 25), "Doctor");

map.put(new Person("Charlie", 35), "Teacher");

System.out.println("TreeMap with custom objects:");

for (var entry : map.entrySet()) {

System.out.println(entry.getKey() + ": " + entry.getValue());

}

}

}

#### Output:

TreeMap with custom objects:

Bob (25): Doctor

Alice (30): Engineer

Charlie (35): Teacher

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>The Person class implements Comparable to define sorting by age.

<bdi>●</bdi>The TreeMap sorts Person objects based on their age.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### TreeMap vs HashMap

| Aspect | TreeMap | HashMap |
| Ordering | Sorted order (natural or custom). | No ordering. |
| Performance | Slower due to sorting (O(log n)). | Faster for basic operations (O(1)). |
| Null Handling | Does not allow null keys. | Allows one null key. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices

1.  Use TreeMap when sorted keys are required.

1.  Avoid using TreeMap for large datasets where sorting is unnecessary (use HashMap instead).

1.  For thread-safe operations, consider ConcurrentSkipListMap.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Key Features:

<bdi>○</bdi>Stores key-value pairs in a sorted order.

<bdi>○</bdi>Allows navigation methods for range queries.

<bdi>●</bdi>Performance:

<bdi>○</bdi>Efficient for sorted data, but slower than HashMap.

<bdi>●</bdi>Common Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>put(), get(), remove(), higherKey(), lowerKey().

## Iterators

An Iterator is an object in Java that allows you to traverse elements in a collection, one element at a time. It provides methods to iterate through the collection without exposing its internal structure.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Iterators

1.  Sequential Access:

<bdi>○</bdi>Allows sequential traversal of elements in a collection.

1.  Universal:

<bdi>○</bdi>Works with any class that implements the java.util.Collection interface.

1.  Modifications:

<bdi>○</bdi>Some iterators allow modifications (like remove()), while others do not (e.g., forEach).

1.  Fail-Fast Behavior:

<bdi>○</bdi>Iterators throw a ConcurrentModificationException if the collection is modified structurally during iteration (unless using a Concurrent collection).

1.  Advanced Iterators:

<bdi>○</bdi>Specialized iterators include ListIterator and Spliterator.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Iterator Interface

The java.util.Iterator interface has three primary methods:

| Method | Description |
| boolean hasNext() | Returns true if the iteration has more elements. |
| E next() | Returns the next element in the iteration. |
| void remove() | Removes the last element returned by next() (optional operation). |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Basic Iterator Example

#### Code Example:

import java.util.ArrayList;

import java.util.Iterator;

public class IteratorExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

ArrayList<String> list = new ArrayList<>();

list.add("Alice");

list.add("Bob");

list.add("Charlie");

// Get an iterator

Iterator<String> iterator = list.iterator();

// Traverse the list

System.out.println("Iterating through the list:");

while (iterator.hasNext()) {

String name = iterator.next();

System.out.println(name);

}

}

}

#### Output:

Iterating through the list:

Alice

Bob

Charlie

#### Explanation:

1.  Create Iterator:

<bdi>○</bdi>iterator() is called on the ArrayList to obtain an Iterator object.

1.  Traversal:

<bdi>○</bdi>hasNext() checks if there are more elements.

<bdi>○</bdi>next() retrieves the next element in the list.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Using remove() in Iterator

#### Code Example:

import java.util.ArrayList;

import java.util.Iterator;

public class IteratorRemoveExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

ArrayList<String> list = new ArrayList<>();

list.add("Alice");

list.add("Bob");

list.add("Charlie");

// Get an iterator

Iterator<String> iterator = list.iterator();

// Remove "Bob" from the list

while (iterator.hasNext()) {

String name = iterator.next();

if (name.equals("Bob")) {

iterator.remove();

}

}

System.out.println("List after removal: " + list);

}

}

#### Output:

List after removal: [Alice, Charlie]

#### Explanation:

1.  remove():

<bdi>○</bdi>Removes the last element returned by the iterator.

1.  Iterator's Responsibility:

<bdi>○</bdi>Structural modifications to the collection should only be made through the iterator during traversal.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Using ListIterator

A ListIterator is an extension of Iterator that allows:

<bdi>●</bdi>Traversal in both directions.

<bdi>●</bdi>Adding, replacing, or removing elements.

| Method | Description |
| boolean hasPrevious() | Returns true if there are elements when traversing in reverse. |
| E previous() | Returns the previous element in the list. |
| void add(E e) | Inserts the specified element into the list. |
| void set(E e) | Replaces the last element returned by next() or previous() with the specified element. |

#### Code Example:

import java.util.ArrayList;

import java.util.ListIterator;

public class ListIteratorExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

ArrayList<String> list = new ArrayList<>();

list.add("Alice");

list.add("Bob");

list.add("Charlie");

// Get a ListIterator

ListIterator<String> iterator = list.listIterator();

// Traverse forward

System.out.println("Forward iteration:");

while (iterator.hasNext()) {

System.out.println(iterator.next());

}

// Traverse backward

System.out.println("\nBackward iteration:");

while (iterator.hasPrevious()) {

System.out.println(iterator.previous());

}

// Modify elements

iterator = list.listIterator();

while (iterator.hasNext()) {

String name = iterator.next();

if (name.equals("Bob")) {

iterator.set("Robert");

}

}

System.out.println("\nList after modification: " + list);

}

}

#### Output:

Forward iteration:

Alice

Bob

Charlie

Backward iteration:

Charlie

Bob

Alice

List after modification: [Alice, Robert, Charlie]

#### Explanation:

1.  Forward and Backward Traversal:

<bdi>○</bdi>hasNext() and next() are used for forward traversal.

<bdi>○</bdi>hasPrevious() and previous() are used for backward traversal.

1.  Modify Elements:

<bdi>○</bdi>set("Robert") replaces "Bob" with "Robert".

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Using Spliterator

A Spliterator is a specialized iterator introduced in Java 8\. It supports parallelism and functional-style operations.

| Method | Description |
| boolean tryAdvance(Consumer<? super T> action) | Performs the given action on the next element. |
| Spliterator<T> trySplit() | Splits the iterator into two, enabling parallel processing. |
| long estimateSize() | Estimates the number of remaining elements. |
| int characteristics() | Returns the characteristics of the Spliterator (e.g., ORDERED, DISTINCT). |

#### Code Example:

import java.util.ArrayList;

import java.util.Spliterator;

public class SpliteratorExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

ArrayList<String> list = new ArrayList<>();

list.add("Alice");

list.add("Bob");

list.add("Charlie");

// Get a Spliterator

Spliterator<String> spliterator = list.spliterator();

// Use tryAdvance

System.out.println("Using tryAdvance:");

spliterator.tryAdvance(System.out::println);

// Use forEachRemaining

System.out.println("\nUsing forEachRemaining:");

spliterator.forEachRemaining(System.out::println);

}

}

#### Output:

Using tryAdvance:

Alice

Using forEachRemaining:

Bob

Charlie

#### Explanation:

1.  tryAdvance:

<bdi>○</bdi>Processes the next element and moves the iterator forward.

1.  forEachRemaining:

<bdi>○</bdi>Processes all remaining elements in the Spliterator.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Fail-Fast Iterators

Iterators in Java are fail-fast, meaning they throw a ConcurrentModificationException if the collection is structurally modified after the iterator is created (except through the iterator).

#### Code Example:

import java.util.ArrayList;

import java.util.Iterator;

public class FailFastExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

ArrayList<String> list = new ArrayList<>();

list.add("Alice");

list.add("Bob");

Iterator<String> iterator = list.iterator();

while (iterator.hasNext()) {

System.out.println(iterator.next());

list.add("Charlie"); // Structural modification

}

}

}

#### Output:

Exception in thread "main" java.util.ConcurrentModificationException

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>Modifying the collection directly while iterating triggers a ConcurrentModificationException.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Best Practices with Iterators

1.  Avoid Structural Modifications:

<bdi>○</bdi>Modify the collection only through the iterator during iteration.

1.  Use Enhanced For-Loop When Possible:

<bdi>○</bdi>Simpler and more concise for non-modifying operations.

1.  Use Concurrent Collections:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use CopyOnWriteArrayList or ConcurrentHashMap for safe iterations in multi-threaded environments.

1.  Use Spliterator for Parallel Processing:

<bdi>○</bdi>Efficient for functional-style programming and large datasets.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Iterators:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enable sequential traversal of collection elements.

<bdi>○</bdi>Provide methods like hasNext, next, and remove.

<bdi>●</bdi>ListIterator:

<bdi>○</bdi>Allows bidirectional traversal and modifications.

<bdi>●</bdi>Spliterator:

<bdi>○</bdi>Supports parallelism and functional-style operations.

<bdi>●</bdi>Fail-Fast Behavior:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensures safety during structural modifications.

## Streams and Parallel Streams

Streams in Java are introduced in Java 8 as a part of the java.util.stream package. They allow developers to perform functional-style operations on collections and other data sources. A Parallel Stream is a specialized version of a stream that enables concurrent processing, taking advantage of multiple threads.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Streams

1.  Functional Programming:

<bdi>○</bdi>Streams use lambda expressions to process data declaratively.

1.  Lazy Evaluation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Intermediate operations are evaluated only when a terminal operation is invoked.

1.  Non-Destructive:

<bdi>○</bdi>Streams do not modify the original data source.

1.  Pipelining:

<bdi>○</bdi>Multiple operations can be chained to form a pipeline.

1.  Stateless Operations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Operations do not rely on mutable shared state, making them thread-safe.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Stream vs Parallel Stream

| Aspect | Stream | Parallel Stream |
| Execution | Sequential (single-threaded). | Concurrent (multi-threaded). |
| Performance | Suitable for small datasets. | Suitable for large datasets. |
| Order Preservation | Preserves order of elements. | May not preserve order of elements. |
| Overhead | Minimal thread management overhead. | Additional overhead due to thread splitting and merging. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Stream API Operations

#### 1\. Intermediate Operations

| Method | Description |
| filter() | Filters elements based on a predicate. |
| map() | Transforms elements using a function. |
| distinct() | Removes duplicate elements. |
| sorted() | Sorts elements in natural or custom order. |
| limit() | Truncates the stream to a given size. |

#### 2\. Terminal Operations

| Method | Description |
| forEach() | Performs an action on each element. |
| collect() | Transforms the elements into a collection or another format. |
| reduce() | Reduces elements to a single value. |
| count() | Returns the number of elements. |
| anyMatch() | Returns true if any element matches a condition. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Basic Stream Example

#### Code Example:

import java.util.Arrays;

import java.util.List;

public class StreamExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

List<Integer> numbers = Arrays.asList(1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6);

// Filter even numbers and double them

List<Integer> result = numbers.stream()

.filter(n -> n % 2 == 0)

.map(n -> n * 2)

.toList();

System.out.println("Processed List: " + result);

}

}

#### Output:

Processed List: [4, 8, 12]

#### Explanation:

1.  Filter:

<bdi>○</bdi>filter(n -> n % 2 == 0) keeps only even numbers.

1.  Transform:

<bdi>○</bdi>map(n -> n * 2) doubles each number.

1.  Collect:

<bdi>○</bdi>Converts the stream back into a List.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Parallel Stream Example

#### Code Example:

import java.util.stream.IntStream;

public class ParallelStreamExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Sum numbers from 1 to 10 using a parallel stream

int sum = IntStream.rangeClosed(1, 10)

.parallel()

.sum();

System.out.println("Sum: " + sum);

}

}

#### Output:

Sum: 55

#### Explanation:

1.  Range Creation:

<bdi>○</bdi>IntStream.rangeClosed(1, 10) creates a stream of numbers from 1 to 10.

1.  Parallel Execution:

<bdi>○</bdi>.parallel() enables multi-threaded processing.

1.  Reduction:

<bdi>○</bdi>sum() combines all elements into a single result.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Stream Operations

#### Example: Filtering, Mapping, and Collecting

#### Code Example:

import java.util.Arrays;

import java.util.List;

import java.util.stream.Collectors;

public class StreamOperations {

public static void main(String[] args) {

List<String> names = Arrays.asList("Alice", "Bob", "Charlie", "David");

// Filter names starting with 'A', convert to uppercase, and collect into a list

List<String> result = names.stream()

.filter(name -> name.startsWith("A"))

.map(String::toUpperCase)

.collect(Collectors.toList());

System.out.println("Processed Names: " + result);

}

}

#### Output:

Processed Names: [ALICE]

#### Explanation:

1.  Filter:

<bdi>○</bdi>Keeps only names starting with 'A'.

1.  Map:

<bdi>○</bdi>Converts names to uppercase.

1.  Collect:

<bdi>○</bdi>Gathers the results into a List.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Example: Sorting and Distinct

#### Code Example:

import java.util.Arrays;

import java.util.List;

public class StreamDistinctSorted {

public static void main(String[] args) {

List<Integer> numbers = Arrays.asList(4, 2, 7, 4, 2, 9, 5);

// Remove duplicates and sort

List<Integer> result = numbers.stream()

.distinct()

.sorted()

.toList();

System.out.println("Sorted and Distinct Numbers: " + result);

}

}

#### Output:

Sorted and Distinct Numbers: [2, 4, 5, 7, 9]

#### Explanation:

1.  Distinct:

<bdi>○</bdi>Removes duplicate elements.

1.  Sorted:

<bdi>○</bdi>Arranges elements in ascending order.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Comparing Performance: Stream vs Parallel Stream

#### Code Example:

import java.util.stream.IntStream;

public class StreamPerformance {

public static void main(String[] args) {

long startTime, endTime;

// Sequential Stream

startTime = System.currentTimeMillis();

IntStream.range(1, 1_000_000).sum();

endTime = System.currentTimeMillis();

System.out.println("Sequential Stream Time: " + (endTime - startTime) + " ms");

// Parallel Stream

startTime = System.currentTimeMillis();

IntStream.range(1, 1_000_000).parallel().sum();

endTime = System.currentTimeMillis();

System.out.println("Parallel Stream Time: " + (endTime - startTime) + " ms");

}

}

#### Output (Example, depends on hardware):

Sequential Stream Time: 20 ms

Parallel Stream Time: 5 ms

#### Explanation:

1.  Sequential Stream:

<bdi>○</bdi>Processes elements in a single thread.

1.  Parallel Stream:

<bdi>○</bdi>Splits the workload across multiple threads, improving performance for large datasets.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Using reduce for Custom Aggregation

#### Code Example:

import java.util.Arrays;

import java.util.List;

public class StreamReduceExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

List<Integer> numbers = Arrays.asList(1, 2, 3, 4, 5);

// Calculate product of all numbers

int product = numbers.stream()

.reduce(1, (a, b) -> a * b);

System.out.println("Product of Numbers: " + product);

}

}

#### Output:

Product of Numbers: 120

#### Explanation:

1.  Identity:

<bdi>○</bdi>1 is the starting value for multiplication.

1.  Accumulator:

<bdi>○</bdi>(a, b) -> a * b multiplies each element with the accumulated result.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Points for Parallel Streams

1.  Use Cases:

<bdi>○</bdi>Parallel streams are suitable for large datasets or computationally expensive operations.

1.  Avoid Overhead:

<bdi>○</bdi>For small datasets, parallel streams can introduce unnecessary overhead.

1.  Thread Safety:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensure operations are stateless to avoid concurrency issues.

1.  Fork/Join Framework:

<bdi>○</bdi>Parallel streams internally use the Fork/Join framework for splitting tasks.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices

1.  Use Streams for clear and declarative data processing.

1.  Use Parallel Streams judiciously, considering the size of the data and overhead.

1.  Avoid modifying shared data structures in parallel streams.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Streams:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enable functional-style processing of collections.

<bdi>○</bdi>Suitable for sequential, non-threaded operations.

<bdi>●</bdi>Parallel Streams:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use multiple threads for concurrent processing.

<bdi>○</bdi>Suitable for large datasets or compute-intensive tasks.

## Generics

Generics in Java are a feature that allows classes, interfaces, and methods to operate on types specified by the programmer at compile-time. They provide type safety, reusability, and readability for collections and other data structures.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Generics

1.  Type Safety:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensures that only compatible types are added to a collection or method, avoiding ClassCastException.

1.  Code Reusability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Allows the same code to work with different types.

1.  Elimination of Type Casting:

<bdi>○</bdi>Avoids explicit casting when retrieving elements from collections.

1.  Compile-Time Checking:

<bdi>○</bdi>Errors are caught at compile time instead of runtime.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Generic Classes

A generic class allows you to define a class that can operate on different types.

#### Code Example:

class Box<T> {

private T item;

public void setItem(T item) {

this.item = item;

}

public T getItem() {

return item;

}

}

public class GenericClassExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Box for Strings

Box<String> stringBox = new Box<>();

stringBox.setItem("Hello");

System.out.println("String Box: " + stringBox.getItem());

// Box for Integers

Box<Integer> integerBox = new Box<>();

integerBox.setItem(123);

System.out.println("Integer Box: " + integerBox.getItem());

}

}

#### Output:

String Box: Hello

Integer Box: 123

#### Explanation:

1.  Generic Parameter <T>:

<bdi>○</bdi>T is a type parameter that will be replaced by the specified type (String, Integer, etc.).

1.  Type Safety:

<bdi>○</bdi>You cannot assign a non-String value to stringBox or a non-Integer value to integerBox.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Generic Methods

A generic method allows defining methods with type parameters, enabling them to work with different data types.

#### Code Example:

class GenericMethodExample {

public static <T> void printArray(T[] array) {

for (T element : array) {

System.out.print(element + " ");

}

System.out.println();

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

Integer[] intArray = {1, 2, 3};

String[] strArray = {"Hello", "World"};

printArray(intArray);

printArray(strArray);

}

}

#### Output:

1 2 3

Hello World

#### Explanation:

1.  Generic Method Syntax:

<bdi>○</bdi><T> specifies the generic type parameter for the method.

1.  Reusability:

<bdi>○</bdi>The printArray method works with both Integer[] and String[].

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Bounded Type Parameters

You can restrict the types that can be used as arguments for a generic type using bounded type parameters.

#### Code Example:

class BoundedType<T extends Number> {

private T number;

public void setNumber(T number) {

this.number = number;

}

public double square() {

return number.doubleValue() * number.doubleValue();

}

}

public class BoundedTypeExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

BoundedType<Integer> intObj = new BoundedType<>();

intObj.setNumber(5);

System.out.println("Square of 5: " + intObj.square());

BoundedType<Double> doubleObj = new BoundedType<>();

doubleObj.setNumber(3.14);

System.out.println("Square of 3.14: " + doubleObj.square());

}

}

#### Output:

Square of 5: 25.0

Square of 3.14: 9.8596

#### Explanation:

1.  Bounded Parameter <T extends Number>:

<bdi>○</bdi>Restricts T to subclasses of Number (e.g., Integer, Double).

1.  Method Call:

<bdi>○</bdi>number.doubleValue() converts the number to a double for calculations.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Wildcards in Generics

Wildcards are used when you do not know the exact type of the generic class or method.

#### Wildcard Types

| Wildcard | Description |
| <?> | Unbounded wildcard: accepts any type. |
| <? extends Type> | Upper bounded wildcard: accepts Type or its subclasses. |
| <? super Type> | Lower bounded wildcard: accepts Type or its superclasses. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Example: Unbounded Wildcard

import java.util.List;

import java.util.ArrayList;

public class UnboundedWildcardExample {

public static void printList(List<?> list) {

for (Object obj : list) {

System.out.print(obj + " ");

}

System.out.println();

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

List<Integer> intList = List.of(1, 2, 3);

List<String> strList = List.of("A", "B", "C");

printList(intList);

printList(strList);

}

}

#### Output:

1 2 3

A B C

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Example: Upper Bounded Wildcard

import java.util.List;

public class UpperBoundedWildcardExample {

public static double sumList(List<? extends Number> list) {

double sum = 0.0;

for (Number num : list) {

sum += num.doubleValue();

}

return sum;

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

List<Integer> intList = List.of(1, 2, 3);

List<Double> doubleList = List.of(1.1, 2.2, 3.3);

System.out.println("Sum of intList: " + sumList(intList));

System.out.println("Sum of doubleList: " + sumList(doubleList));

}

}

#### Output:

Sum of intList: 6.0

Sum of doubleList: 6.6

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Example: Lower Bounded Wildcard

import java.util.List;

import java.util.ArrayList;

public class LowerBoundedWildcardExample {

public static void addNumbers(List<? super Integer> list) {

list.add(1);

list.add(2);

list.add(3);

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

List<Number> numList = new ArrayList<>();

addNumbers(numList);

System.out.println("List after adding numbers: " + numList);

}

}

#### Output:

List after adding numbers: [1, 2, 3]

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Generics with Collections

Generics are commonly used with Java Collections, such as List, Set, and Map.

#### Code Example:

import java.util.ArrayList;

public class GenericCollectionsExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// List of Strings

ArrayList<String> names = new ArrayList<>();

names.add("Alice");

names.add("Bob");

names.add("Charlie");

for (String name : names) {

System.out.println(name);

}

}

}

#### Output:

Alice

Bob

Charlie

#### Explanation:

1.  Generic Collection:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensures type safety, allowing only String values in the list.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Advantages of Generics

1.  Type Safety:

<bdi>○</bdi>Detects type-related errors at compile time.

1.  Code Reusability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Generic classes and methods work with any type.

1.  Elimination of Type Casting:

<bdi>○</bdi>Reduces boilerplate code.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

1.  Generics Syntax:

<bdi>○</bdi><T> for classes and methods.

<bdi>○</bdi><T extends Class> for bounded parameters.

1.  Wildcards:

<bdi>○</bdi><?>, <? extends T>, <? super T> for flexible type handling.

1.  Use Cases:

<bdi>○</bdi>Collections, utility methods, custom classes.

## Enumerations

An enumeration (enum) in Java is a special type used to define a set of named constants. Enums improve code readability and safety by grouping related constants together. They are introduced in Java 5 and are part of the java.lang package.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Enumerations

1.  Type Safety:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enum constants are type-safe, meaning you cannot assign unrelated values to them.

1.  Implicit Class:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enums are implicitly subclasses of java.lang.Enum and can have fields, methods, and constructors.

1.  Set of Constants:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enums define a fixed set of constants (e.g., DAYS, COLORS, DIRECTIONS).

1.  Readable Code:

<bdi>○</bdi>Replacing hard-coded constants with enums makes the code more maintainable and expressive.

1.  Built-in Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enums provide methods like values(), ordinal(), and name() for convenient operations.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Basic Enum Declaration and Usage

#### Code Example:

enum Day {

MONDAY, TUESDAY, WEDNESDAY, THURSDAY, FRIDAY, SATURDAY, SUNDAY

}

public class EnumExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Access enum constants

Day today = Day.FRIDAY;

System.out.println("Today is: " + today);

// Iterate through all enum values

System.out.println("Days of the week:");

for (Day day : Day.values()) {

System.out.println(day);

}

}

}

#### Output:

Today is: FRIDAY

Days of the week:

MONDAY

TUESDAY

WEDNESDAY

THURSDAY

FRIDAY

SATURDAY

SUNDAY

#### Explanation:

1.  Enum Declaration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Day is an enum with predefined constants.

1.  Accessing Enum Constants:

<bdi>○</bdi>Day.FRIDAY accesses the FRIDAY constant.

1.  Iteration:

<bdi>○</bdi>values() returns an array of all enum constants, which can be iterated using a loop.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Enum with Fields, Methods, and Constructor

Enums can have fields, methods, and constructors to add behavior.

#### Code Example:

enum Season {

WINTER("Cold"), SPRING("Pleasant"), SUMMER("Hot"), FALL("Cool");

private final String description;

// Constructor

Season(String description) {

this.description = description;

}

// Getter method

public String getDescription() {

return description;

}

}

public class EnumWithFields {

public static void main(String[] args) {

for (Season season : Season.values()) {

System.out.println(season + ": " + season.getDescription());

}

}

}

#### Output:

WINTER: Cold

SPRING: Pleasant

SUMMER: Hot

FALL: Cool

#### Explanation:

1.  Fields and Constructor:

<bdi>○</bdi>Each enum constant has an associated description, set through the constructor.

1.  Getter Method:

<bdi>○</bdi>getDescription() retrieves the description for each constant.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Using Enum in Switch Statements

Enums integrate seamlessly with switch statements, making conditional logic more readable.

#### Code Example:

enum TrafficLight {

RED, YELLOW, GREEN

}

public class EnumSwitchExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

TrafficLight light = TrafficLight.RED;

switch (light) {

case RED:

System.out.println("Stop");

break;

case YELLOW:

System.out.println("Get Ready");

break;

case GREEN:

System.out.println("Go");

break;

}

}

}

#### Output:

Stop

#### Explanation:

1.  Enum in Switch:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enum constants are directly used in the case statements.

1.  Readable Logic:

<bdi>○</bdi>Replacing string-based conditions with enums ensures type safety and avoids typos.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Enum with Abstract Methods

Enums can define abstract methods that each constant must implement.

#### Code Example:

enum Operation {

ADD {

@Override

double apply(double x, double y) {

return x + y;

}

},

SUBTRACT {

@Override

double apply(double x, double y) {

return x - y;

}

},

MULTIPLY {

@Override

double apply(double x, double y) {

return x * y;

}

},

DIVIDE {

@Override

double apply(double x, double y) {

return x / y;

}

};

// Abstract method

abstract double apply(double x, double y);

}

public class EnumWithAbstractMethod {

public static void main(String[] args) {

double x = 10.0, y = 5.0;

for (Operation op : Operation.values()) {

System.out.println(op + ": " + op.apply(x, y));

}

}

}

#### Output:

ADD: 15.0

SUBTRACT: 5.0

MULTIPLY: 50.0

DIVIDE: 2.0

#### Explanation:

1.  Abstract Method:

<bdi>○</bdi>Each enum constant provides its specific implementation for the apply method.

1.  Behavioral Enums:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enums are not just data but can encapsulate behavior as well.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Built-in Enum Methods

| Method | Description |
| values() | Returns an array of all enum constants. |
| ordinal() | Returns the index of the constant (0-based). |
| name() | Returns the name of the constant as a string. |
| valueOf(String) | Converts a string to the corresponding enum constant (case-sensitive). |

#### Code Example:

enum Planet {

MERCURY, VENUS, EARTH, MARS, JUPITER, SATURN, URANUS, NEPTUNE

}

public class BuiltInEnumMethods {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Using values()

for (Planet planet : Planet.values()) {

System.out.println(planet + " is at position " + planet.ordinal());

}

// Using valueOf()

Planet myPlanet = Planet.valueOf("EARTH");

System.out.println("\nMy Planet: " + myPlanet);

}

}

#### Output:

MERCURY is at position 0

VENUS is at position 1

EARTH is at position 2

MARS is at position 3

JUPITER is at position 4

SATURN is at position 5

URANUS is at position 6

NEPTUNE is at position 7

My Planet: EARTH

#### Explanation:

1.  values():

<bdi>○</bdi>Returns all constants in the enum.

1.  ordinal():

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides the index of each constant.

1.  valueOf(String):

<bdi>○</bdi>Converts a string into the corresponding enum constant.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Enum with Interfaces

Enums can implement interfaces to define additional behavior.

#### Code Example:

interface Printable {

void printMessage();

}

enum Role implements Printable {

ADMIN {

@Override

public void printMessage() {

System.out.println("Admin: Has all privileges.");

}

},

USER {

@Override

public void printMessage() {

System.out.println("User: Has limited privileges.");

}

},

GUEST {

@Override

public void printMessage() {

System.out.println("Guest: Has minimal privileges.");

}

};

}

public class EnumWithInterface {

public static void main(String[] args) {

for (Role role : Role.values()) {

role.printMessage();

}

}

}

#### Output:

Admin: Has all privileges.

User: Has limited privileges.

Guest: Has minimal privileges.

#### Explanation:

1.  Interface Implementation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Each enum constant provides a specific implementation for the printMessage method.

1.  Reusability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Common behavior (Printable) is shared across enum constants.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Advantages of Enums

1.  Type Safety:

<bdi>○</bdi>Prevents invalid values, ensuring only predefined constants are used.

1.  Readability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides meaningful names for constants, improving code clarity.

1.  Behavioral Extensions:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enums can encapsulate related behaviors using fields, methods, and constructors.

1.  Integration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Seamless use in switch statements and collections.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Enums:

<bdi>○</bdi>Define a fixed set of constants.

<bdi>○</bdi>Can have fields, methods, constructors, and even abstract methods.

<bdi>●</bdi>Built-in Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>values(), ordinal(), name(), valueOf(String).

<bdi>●</bdi>Advanced Features:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enums with interfaces, constructors, and custom methods.

## Wrapper Classes

Wrapper Classes in Java are used to convert primitive data types into objects and vice versa. They "wrap" primitive types in a class, allowing them to be used as objects. These classes are part of the java.lang package and are particularly useful for working with collections, serialization, and other object-based operations.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### List of Wrapper Classes

| Primitive Type | Wrapper Class |
| byte | Byte |
| short | Short |
| int | Integer |
| long | Long |
| float | Float |
| double | Double |
| char | Character |
| boolean | Boolean |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Wrapper Classes

1.  Object Representation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Primitive types can be treated as objects.

1.  Type Conversion:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides utility methods for converting between types.

1.  Boxing and Unboxing:

<bdi>○</bdi>Autoboxing: Automatic conversion of a primitive type to its wrapper class.

<bdi>○</bdi>Unboxing: Automatic conversion of a wrapper class to its primitive type.

1.  Constants and Utility Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>Wrapper classes provide constants (e.g., Integer.MAX_VALUE) and utility methods (e.g., parseInt).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Autoboxing and Unboxing

#### Code Example:

public class AutoboxingUnboxingExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Autoboxing: Primitive to Wrapper

Integer intObj = 10; // int to Integer

Double doubleObj = 5.5; // double to Double

System.out.println("Autoboxing:");

System.out.println("Integer object: " + intObj);

System.out.println("Double object: " + doubleObj);

// Unboxing: Wrapper to Primitive

int intValue = intObj; // Integer to int

double doubleValue = doubleObj; // Double to double

System.out.println("\nUnboxing:");

System.out.println("Primitive int: " + intValue);

System.out.println("Primitive double: " + doubleValue);

}

}

#### Output:

Autoboxing:

Integer object: 10

Double object: 5.5

Unboxing:

Primitive int: 10

Primitive double: 5.5

#### Explanation:

1.  Autoboxing:

<bdi>○</bdi>Automatically converts primitives (int, double) into wrapper objects (Integer, Double).

1.  Unboxing:

<bdi>○</bdi>Automatically converts wrapper objects back into primitives.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Utility Methods in Wrapper Classes

Wrapper classes provide utility methods for type conversion, parsing, and more.

#### Code Example:

public class UtilityMethodsExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Parsing strings to primitives

int intValue = Integer.parseInt("123");

double doubleValue = Double.parseDouble("45.67");

System.out.println("Parsed int: " + intValue);

System.out.println("Parsed double: " + doubleValue);

// Converting primitives to strings

String strFromInt = Integer.toString(123);

String strFromDouble = Double.toString(45.67);

System.out.println("String from int: " + strFromInt);

System.out.println("String from double: " + strFromDouble);

// Using constants

System.out.println("Integer Max Value: " + Integer.MAX_VALUE);

System.out.println("Double Min Value: " + Double.MIN_VALUE);

}

}

#### Output:

Parsed int: 123

Parsed double: 45.67

String from int: 123

String from double: 45.67

Integer Max Value: 2147483647

Double Min Value: 4.9E-324

#### Explanation:

1.  parseInt and parseDouble:

<bdi>○</bdi>Convert strings to corresponding primitives.

1.  toString:

<bdi>○</bdi>Converts primitives to strings.

1.  Constants:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provide metadata like maximum and minimum values.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Using Wrapper Classes with Collections

Collections in Java require objects, so wrapper classes are used when working with primitive types.

#### Code Example:

import java.util.ArrayList;

public class WrapperInCollections {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Using Integer wrapper with ArrayList

ArrayList<Integer> numbers = new ArrayList<>();

numbers.add(10); // Autoboxing

numbers.add(20);

numbers.add(30);

System.out.println("Numbers in ArrayList:");

for (int number : numbers) { // Unboxing

System.out.println(number);

}

}

}

#### Output:

Numbers in ArrayList:

10

20

30

#### Explanation:

1.  Autoboxing:

<bdi>○</bdi>Primitives are automatically wrapped into Integer objects when added to the ArrayList.

1.  Unboxing:

<bdi>○</bdi>Elements are automatically unwrapped into int during iteration.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Comparison of Wrapper Objects

Wrapper classes provide the equals method for value-based comparisons, while == compares object references.

#### Code Example:

public class WrapperComparison {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Integer num1 = 100;

Integer num2 = 100;

Integer num3 = 200;

// Comparing wrapper objects

System.out.println("num1 == num2: " + (num1 == num2)); // True due to caching

System.out.println("num1.equals(num2): " + num1.equals(num2)); // True

System.out.println("num1 == num3: " + (num1 == num3)); // False

System.out.println("num1.equals(num3): " + num1.equals(num3)); // False

}

}

#### Output:

num1 == num2: true

num1.equals(num2): true

num1 == num3: false

num1.equals(num3): false

#### Explanation:

1.  Caching for Small Integers:

<bdi>○</bdi>Wrapper classes cache small values (e.g., -128 to 127 for Integer), so == may return true for these values.

1.  equals Method:

<bdi>○</bdi>Compares the actual values inside the wrapper objects.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Wrapper Class Constants

Each wrapper class provides useful constants like MAX_VALUE and MIN_VALUE.

#### Code Example:

public class WrapperConstants {

public static void main(String[] args) {

System.out.println("Byte Max: " + Byte.MAX_VALUE);

System.out.println("Short Max: " + Short.MAX_VALUE);

System.out.println("Integer Max: " + Integer.MAX_VALUE);

System.out.println("Long Max: " + Long.MAX_VALUE);

System.out.println("Float Max: " + Float.MAX_VALUE);

System.out.println("Double Max: " + Double.MAX_VALUE);

}

}

#### Output:

Byte Max: 127

Short Max: 32767

Integer Max: 2147483647

Long Max: 9223372036854775807

Float Max: 3.4028235E38

Double Max: 1.7976931348623157E308

#### Explanation:

<bdi>●</bdi>Constants like MAX_VALUE represent the largest value each primitive type can hold.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Wrapping and Unwrapping Manually

Manual boxing and unboxing are possible, but autoboxing and unboxing are preferred.

#### Code Example:

public class ManualBoxingUnboxing {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Manual boxing

Integer intObj = Integer.valueOf(42);

// Manual unboxing

int intValue = intObj.intValue();

System.out.println("Boxed Integer: " + intObj);

System.out.println("Unboxed int: " + intValue);

}

}

#### Output:

Boxed Integer: 42

Unboxed int: 42

#### Explanation:

1.  valueOf:

<bdi>○</bdi>Wraps the primitive into an object.

1.  intValue:

<bdi>○</bdi>Extracts the primitive value from the wrapper.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Advantages of Wrapper Classes

1.  Integration with Collections:

<bdi>○</bdi>Allows primitives to be stored in collections.

1.  Type Conversion:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides utility methods for parsing and converting data types.

1.  Object-Oriented Features:

<bdi>○</bdi>Adds behavior (methods, constants) to primitive types.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

1.  Autoboxing/Unboxing:

<bdi>○</bdi>Automatic conversion between primitives and wrapper objects.

1.  Constants and Utility Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>Helpful for parsing, conversion, and value range operations.

1.  Comparison:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use equals for value-based comparison.

1.  Use Cases:

<bdi>○</bdi>Essential for collections, generic types, and object-based operations.

# Advanced Java Topics

# Multithreading and Concurrency

## Thread Lifecycle in Java

A thread in Java goes through multiple states during its lifetime, collectively referred to as the Thread Lifecycle. The Java Thread class, part of the java.lang package, models these states. Understanding the lifecycle is crucial for writing efficient and bug-free multithreaded programs.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Thread States

The thread lifecycle consists of the following states:

1.  New:

<bdi>○</bdi>A thread is in the NEW state when it is created using the Thread class but has not yet started.

1.  Runnable:

<bdi>○</bdi>A thread is in the RUNNABLE state after it is started and ready to run. The thread scheduler determines when it actually runs.

1.  Running:

<bdi>○</bdi>A thread transitions to the RUNNING state when the CPU starts executing it.

1.  Blocked/Waiting/Timed Waiting:

<bdi>○</bdi>A thread moves to these states when it is waiting for a resource or another thread to complete its task:

<bdi>■</bdi>Blocked: Waiting to acquire a lock.

<bdi>■</bdi>Waiting: Waiting indefinitely for another thread's signal.

<bdi>■</bdi>Timed Waiting: Waiting for a signal or resource for a specified time.

1.  Terminated:

<bdi>○</bdi>A thread is in the TERMINATED state after it completes its task or is explicitly stopped.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Thread Lifecycle Diagram

NEW --> RUNNABLE --> RUNNING --> WAITING/BLOCKED/TIMED_WAITING --> TERMINATED

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Thread Lifecycle Methods

| Method | Description |
| start() | Starts a thread, moving it to the RUNNABLE state. |
| run() | Defines the task to be executed by the thread. |
| sleep(milliseconds) | Puts the thread in a TIMED_WAITING state for the specified duration. |
| join() | Puts the current thread in a WAITING state until the called thread completes. |
| yield() | Temporarily pauses the current thread to give other threads a chance to execute. |
| wait() | Puts the thread in a WAITING state until another thread calls notify(). |
| notify()/notifyAll() | Wakes up threads that are waiting on the monitor of the current object. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Basic Example of Thread Lifecycle

#### Code Example:

class MyThread extends Thread {

@Override

public void run() {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " is running.");

try {

Thread.sleep(1000); // Puts thread in TIMED_WAITING

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " was interrupted.");

}

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " is terminating.");

}

}

public class ThreadLifecycleExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

MyThread thread = new MyThread(); // Thread in NEW state

System.out.println("Thread state: " + thread.getState());

thread.start(); // Thread moves to RUNNABLE state

System.out.println("Thread state after start: " + thread.getState());

try {

thread.join(); // Main thread waits for this thread to finish

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

System.out.println("Main thread interrupted.");

}

System.out.println("Thread state after termination: " + thread.getState());

}

}

#### Output:

Thread state: NEW

Thread state after start: RUNNABLE

Thread-0 is running.

Thread-0 is terminating.

Thread state after termination: TERMINATED

#### Explanation:

1.  NEW State:

<bdi>○</bdi>The thread is created but not yet started.

1.  RUNNABLE State:

<bdi>○</bdi>The thread moves to this state after calling start().

1.  RUNNING State:

<bdi>○</bdi>The thread executes the run() method.

1.  TIMED_WAITING State:

<bdi>○</bdi>The thread enters this state when sleep(1000) is called.

1.  TERMINATED State:

<bdi>○</bdi>After completing its task, the thread enters the terminated state.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Demonstrating Waiting and Notify

#### Code Example:

class SharedResource {

synchronized void waitingMethod() {

try {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " is waiting.");

wait(); // Thread enters WAITING state

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " is notified and running again.");

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

}

synchronized void notifyMethod() {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " is notifying waiting threads.");

notify(); // Notify a single thread

}

}

public class WaitNotifyExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

SharedResource resource = new SharedResource();

Thread t1 = new Thread(resource::waitingMethod, "Thread-1");

Thread t2 = new Thread(resource::notifyMethod, "Thread-2");

t1.start();

try {

Thread.sleep(500); // Ensures Thread-1 calls wait() first

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

t2.start();

}

}

#### Output:

Thread-1 is waiting.

Thread-2 is notifying waiting threads.

Thread-1 is notified and running again.

#### Explanation:

1.  Waiting:

<bdi>○</bdi>Thread-1 calls wait() and enters the WAITING state.

1.  Notify:

<bdi>○</bdi>Thread-2 calls notify(), waking up Thread-1.

1.  Continuation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Thread-1 resumes execution after being notified.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Thread Join Example

#### Code Example:

class Task extends Thread {

@Override

public void run() {

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " is working: " + i);

try {

Thread.sleep(500); // Simulates a time-consuming task

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

}

}

}

public class ThreadJoinExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Task thread1 = new Task();

Task thread2 = new Task();

thread1.start();

thread2.start();

try {

thread1.join(); // Main thread waits for thread1 to finish

System.out.println("Thread-1 has finished. Continuing...");

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

System.out.println("Main thread finished execution.");

}

}

#### Output:

Thread-0 is working: 1

Thread-1 is working: 1

Thread-0 is working: 2

Thread-1 is working: 2

Thread-0 is working: 3

Thread-0 is working: 4

Thread-0 is working: 5

Thread-1 is working: 3

Thread-1 is working: 4

Thread-1 is working: 5

Thread-1 has finished. Continuing...

Main thread finished execution.

#### Explanation:

1.  Thread Join:

<bdi>○</bdi>The main thread waits for Thread-1 to finish before proceeding.

1.  Independent Execution:

<bdi>○</bdi>Thread-2 continues execution independently.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Thread Yield Example

#### Code Example:

class YieldTask extends Thread {

public YieldTask(String name) {

super(name);

}

@Override

public void run() {

for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " is executing.");

Thread.yield(); // Hint to the thread scheduler

}

}

}

public class ThreadYieldExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

YieldTask thread1 = new YieldTask("Thread-1");

YieldTask thread2 = new YieldTask("Thread-2");

thread1.start();

thread2.start();

}

}

#### Output (may vary based on the scheduler):

Thread-1 is executing.

Thread-2 is executing.

Thread-1 is executing.

Thread-2 is executing.

Thread-1 is executing.

Thread-2 is executing.

#### Explanation:

1.  Yield:

<bdi>○</bdi>Calling Thread.yield() suggests to the thread scheduler to give other threads a chance to run.

1.  Non-Guaranteed Behavior:

<bdi>○</bdi>The behavior of yield() depends on the JVM and thread scheduler.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Points to Remember

1.  Thread States:

<bdi>○</bdi>Threads transition between NEW, RUNNABLE, RUNNING, WAITING/TIMED_WAITING, and TERMINATED.

1.  Synchronization:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use synchronized, wait(), and notify() for thread-safe communication.

1.  Join:
