<bdi>○</bdi>Ensures one thread completes before others continue.

1.  Yield:

<bdi>○</bdi>Offers a hint to the scheduler to give other threads CPU time.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

The thread lifecycle is a fundamental concept in multithreaded programming. Understanding and leveraging the thread states and methods like start(), join(), sleep(), and notify() ensures efficient thread management and concurrency handling in Java applications

## Creating Threads

In Java, threads are fundamental for enabling concurrent execution of tasks. Java provides multiple ways to create threads, offering flexibility depending on the use case. These methods include using the Thread class, implementing the Runnable interface, and using the Callable interface.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Methods to Create Threads

1.  Extending the Thread Class:

<bdi>○</bdi>Create a subclass of Thread and override the run() method.

<bdi>○</bdi>Start the thread using the start() method.

1.  Implementing the Runnable Interface:

<bdi>○</bdi>Create a class that implements Runnable.

<bdi>○</bdi>Pass an instance of this class to a Thread object and start the thread.

1.  Using the Callable Interface (Introduced in Java 5):

<bdi>○</bdi>Create a class that implements Callable and defines the call() method.

<bdi>○</bdi>Use an ExecutorService to execute the callable task.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Creating Threads by Extending the Thread Class

#### Code Example:

class MyThread extends Thread {

@Override

public void run() {

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " - Count: " + i);

try {

Thread.sleep(500); // Pause for 500ms

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

}

}

}

public class ThreadExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

MyThread thread1 = new MyThread();

MyThread thread2 = new MyThread();

thread1.start(); // Start the first thread

thread2.start(); // Start the second thread

}

}

#### Output (Output order may vary due to multithreading):

Thread-0 - Count: 1

Thread-1 - Count: 1

Thread-0 - Count: 2

Thread-1 - Count: 2

Thread-0 - Count: 3

Thread-1 - Count: 3

Thread-0 - Count: 4

Thread-1 - Count: 4

Thread-0 - Count: 5

Thread-1 - Count: 5

#### Explanation:

1.  Extending Thread:

<bdi>○</bdi>MyThread is a subclass of Thread.

<bdi>○</bdi>The run() method contains the logic executed by the thread.

1.  Start Threads:

<bdi>○</bdi>The start() method creates a new thread and invokes its run() method.

1.  Concurrency:

<bdi>○</bdi>Both threads run concurrently, interleaving their outputs.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Creating Threads by Implementing the Runnable Interface

#### Code Example:

class MyRunnable implements Runnable {

@Override

public void run() {

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " - Count: " + i);

try {

Thread.sleep(500);

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

}

}

}

public class RunnableExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Thread thread1 = new Thread(new MyRunnable());

Thread thread2 = new Thread(new MyRunnable());

thread1.start();

thread2.start();

}

}

#### Output:

Thread-0 - Count: 1

Thread-1 - Count: 1

Thread-0 - Count: 2

Thread-1 - Count: 2

Thread-0 - Count: 3

Thread-1 - Count: 3

Thread-0 - Count: 4

Thread-1 - Count: 4

Thread-0 - Count: 5

Thread-1 - Count: 5

#### Explanation:

1.  Implementing Runnable:

<bdi>○</bdi>The run() method of MyRunnable contains the thread's logic.

1.  Thread Creation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Instances of MyRunnable are passed to the Thread constructor.

1.  Separation of Concerns:

<bdi>○</bdi>Using Runnable allows the class to inherit from other classes since multiple inheritance is not supported in Java.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Creating Threads Using the Callable Interface

Unlike Runnable, the Callable interface allows returning a value and throwing checked exceptions.

#### Code Example:

import java.util.concurrent.Callable;

import java.util.concurrent.ExecutorService;

import java.util.concurrent.Executors;

import java.util.concurrent.Future;

class MyCallable implements Callable<String> {

private String name;

public MyCallable(String name) {

this.name = name;

}

@Override

public String call() throws Exception {

for (int i = 1; i <= 3; i++) {

System.out.println(name + " - Count: " + i);

Thread.sleep(500);

}

return name + " finished execution.";

}

}

public class CallableExample {

public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {

ExecutorService executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(2);

MyCallable task1 = new MyCallable("Thread-1");

MyCallable task2 = new MyCallable("Thread-2");

Future<String> result1 = executor.submit(task1);

Future<String> result2 = executor.submit(task2);

System.out.println(result1.get());

System.out.println(result2.get());

executor.shutdown();

}

}

#### Output:

Thread-1 - Count: 1

Thread-2 - Count: 1

Thread-1 - Count: 2

Thread-2 - Count: 2

Thread-1 - Count: 3

Thread-2 - Count: 3

Thread-1 finished execution.

Thread-2 finished execution.

#### Explanation:

1.  Using Callable:

<bdi>○</bdi>The call() method can return a value and throw exceptions.

1.  ExecutorService:

<bdi>○</bdi>Manages thread creation and execution.

1.  Future:

<bdi>○</bdi>Future.get() retrieves the result of the call() method.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Anonymous Threads

You can create threads anonymously using inline implementations of Runnable.

#### Code Example:

public class AnonymousThreadExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Thread thread1 = new Thread(() -> {

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " - Count: " + i);

try {

Thread.sleep(500);

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

}

});

Thread thread2 = new Thread(() -> {

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " - Count: " + i);

try {

Thread.sleep(500);

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

}

});

thread1.start();

thread2.start();

}

}

#### Output:

Thread-0 - Count: 1

Thread-1 - Count: 1

Thread-0 - Count: 2

Thread-1 - Count: 2

Thread-0 - Count: 3

Thread-1 - Count: 3

Thread-0 - Count: 4

Thread-1 - Count: 4

Thread-0 - Count: 5

Thread-1 - Count: 5

#### Explanation:

1.  Anonymous Implementation:

<bdi>○</bdi>The thread logic is defined inline using lambda expressions or anonymous inner classes.

1.  Conciseness:

<bdi>○</bdi>Reduces boilerplate code.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Comparison of Methods

| Method | Advantages | Disadvantages |
| Extending Thread | Easy to implement, as the thread logic resides in the same class. | Cannot inherit from another class. |
| Implementing Runnable | Allows inheritance from other classes, separates logic from thread management. | Slightly more boilerplate compared to Thread. |
| Using Callable | Supports return values and exceptions, integrates with ExecutorService. | More complex setup with Future and Executor. |
| Anonymous Threads | Quick and concise for simple tasks. | Harder to debug and maintain for complex tasks. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Points

1.  Thread States:

<bdi>○</bdi>After creation (NEW), threads move to RUNNABLE, then RUNNING, and finally TERMINATED.

1.  Concurrency:

<bdi>○</bdi>Threads run independently and may interleave outputs.

1.  Thread Safety:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use synchronization or concurrent utilities to avoid race conditions.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

Java offers various ways to create threads, each suited to different scenarios:

1.  Thread class: Simplest for quick thread creation.

1.  Runnable interface: Recommended for separating logic and thread management.

1.  Callable interface: Useful for tasks that return results.

1.  Anonymous threads: Ideal for short-lived, simple tasks.

## Synchronization

Synchronization in Java is a mechanism that controls access to shared resources in a multithreaded environment. It prevents thread interference and ensures consistency by allowing only one thread to access a critical section of code or data at a time.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Synchronization

1.  Thread Safety:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensures that shared resources are accessed by only one thread at a time.

1.  Prevents Data Inconsistency:

<bdi>○</bdi>Avoids race conditions where multiple threads modify shared data simultaneously.

1.  Monitors:

<bdi>○</bdi>Every object in Java has an implicit monitor lock that can be used for synchronization.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Types of Synchronization

1.  Process Synchronization:

<bdi>○</bdi>Coordinates processes in distributed systems (not covered here).

1.  Thread Synchronization:

<bdi>○</bdi>Coordinates threads in a single program.

#### Thread Synchronization Categories:

<bdi>●</bdi>Mutual Exclusion:

<bdi>○</bdi>Synchronized blocks/methods to ensure one thread accesses a resource at a time.

<bdi>●</bdi>Inter-thread Communication:

<bdi>○</bdi>Methods like wait(), notify(), and notifyAll() for coordination.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Synchronized Methods

When a method is declared synchronized, the thread acquires the object's monitor lock before executing it.

#### Code Example:

class Counter {

private int count = 0;

public synchronized void increment() {

count++;

}

public synchronized int getCount() {

return count;

}

}

public class SynchronizedMethodExample {

public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {

Counter counter = new Counter();

// Create threads

Thread t1 = new Thread(() -> {

for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {

counter.increment();

}

});

Thread t2 = new Thread(() -> {

for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {

counter.increment();

}

});

t1.start();

t2.start();

t1.join(); // Wait for t1 to finish

t2.join(); // Wait for t2 to finish

System.out.println("Final Count: " + counter.getCount());

}

}

#### Output:

Final Count: 2000

#### Explanation:

1.  Synchronized Method:

<bdi>○</bdi>increment() is synchronized, ensuring only one thread can execute it at a time.

1.  Thread Coordination:

<bdi>○</bdi>Both threads increment the shared count variable without interfering, resulting in a consistent final value.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Synchronized Blocks

Synchronized blocks lock a specific object, reducing the scope of synchronization compared to synchronized methods.

#### Code Example:

class Counter {

private int count = 0;

public void increment() {

synchronized (this) { // Synchronized block

count++;

}

}

public int getCount() {

synchronized (this) {

return count;

}

}

}

public class SynchronizedBlockExample {

public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {

Counter counter = new Counter();

Thread t1 = new Thread(() -> {

for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {

counter.increment();

}

});

Thread t2 = new Thread(() -> {

for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {

counter.increment();

}

});

t1.start();

t2.start();

t1.join();

t2.join();

System.out.println("Final Count: " + counter.getCount());

}

}

#### Output:

Final Count: 2000

#### Explanation:

1.  Synchronized Block:

<bdi>○</bdi>Only the critical section (count++) is synchronized, improving efficiency compared to synchronizing the entire method.

1.  Object Locking:

<bdi>○</bdi>The this keyword ensures that the instance's monitor lock is used.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Static Synchronization

Static synchronization locks the class instead of an object, ensuring thread safety for static variables or methods.

#### Code Example:

class Counter {

private static int count = 0;

public static synchronized void increment() {

count++;

}

public static int getCount() {

synchronized (Counter.class) {

return count;

}

}

}

public class StaticSynchronizationExample {

public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {

Thread t1 = new Thread(() -> {

for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {

Counter.increment();

}

});

Thread t2 = new Thread(() -> {

for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {

Counter.increment();

}

});

t1.start();

t2.start();

t1.join();

t2.join();

System.out.println("Final Count: " + Counter.getCount());

}

}

#### Output:

Final Count: 2000

#### Explanation:

1.  Static Synchronization:

<bdi>○</bdi>increment() is synchronized on the Counter class, ensuring consistency across all threads accessing static members.

1.  Class-Level Locking:

<bdi>○</bdi>Only one thread can execute a static synchronized method at a time.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Inter-Thread Communication

Java provides wait(), notify(), and notifyAll() for coordinating threads that share resources.

#### Code Example:

class SharedResource {

private int value = 0;

private boolean isProduced = false;

public synchronized void produce(int value) throws InterruptedException {

while (isProduced) {

wait(); // Wait until the consumer consumes

}

this.value = value;

isProduced = true;

System.out.println("Produced: " + value);

notify(); // Notify the consumer

}

public synchronized void consume() throws InterruptedException {

while (!isProduced) {

wait(); // Wait until the producer produces

}

System.out.println("Consumed: " + value);

isProduced = false;

notify(); // Notify the producer

}

}

public class InterThreadCommunicationExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

SharedResource resource = new SharedResource();

Thread producer = new Thread(() -> {

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

try {

resource.produce(i);

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

}

});

Thread consumer = new Thread(() -> {

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

try {

resource.consume();

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

}

});

producer.start();

consumer.start();

}

}

#### Output:

Produced: 1

Consumed: 1

Produced: 2

Consumed: 2

Produced: 3

Consumed: 3

Produced: 4

Consumed: 4

Produced: 5

Consumed: 5

#### Explanation:

1.  wait() and notify():

<bdi>○</bdi>The producer waits until the consumer consumes the previous value.

<bdi>○</bdi>The consumer waits until the producer produces a new value.

1.  Coordination:

<bdi>○</bdi>notify() signals the other thread to resume execution.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Deadlocks in Synchronization

A deadlock occurs when two threads are waiting for each other's resources indefinitely.

#### Code Example:

class Deadlock {

private final Object lock1 = new Object();

private final Object lock2 = new Object();

public void method1() {

synchronized (lock1) {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " locked lock1");

synchronized (lock2) {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " locked lock2");

}

}

}

public void method2() {

synchronized (lock2) {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " locked lock2");

synchronized (lock1) {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " locked lock1");

}

}

}

}

public class DeadlockExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Deadlock deadlock = new Deadlock();

Thread t1 = new Thread(deadlock::method1, "Thread-1");

Thread t2 = new Thread(deadlock::method2, "Thread-2");

t1.start();

t2.start();

}

}

#### Output:

Thread-1 locked lock1

Thread-2 locked lock2

#### Explanation:

1.  Deadlock:

<bdi>○</bdi>Thread-1 locks lock1 and waits for lock2.

<bdi>○</bdi>Thread-2 locks lock2 and waits for lock1.

<bdi>○</bdi>Both threads are stuck, resulting in a deadlock.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices

1.  Minimize Synchronized Scope:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use synchronized blocks instead of methods to reduce contention.

1.  Avoid Nested Locks:

<bdi>○</bdi>Prevent deadlocks by avoiding nested synchronized blocks.

1.  Use Concurrent Classes:

<bdi>○</bdi>Prefer ReentrantLock, Semaphore, and java.util.concurrent utilities for complex scenarios.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Synchronization Mechanisms:

<bdi>○</bdi>Synchronized methods, synchronized blocks, and static synchronization.

<bdi>●</bdi>Inter-Thread Communication:

<bdi>○</bdi>Achieved using wait(), notify(), and notifyAll().

<bdi>●</bdi>Deadlocks:

<bdi>○</bdi>Avoid by ensuring proper lock acquisition order.

<bdi>●</bdi>Thread Safety:

<bdi>○</bdi>Synchronization ensures consistency and prevents race conditions.

## Executors Framework

The Executors Framework is part of the java.util.concurrent package introduced in Java 5. It provides a powerful mechanism for managing and controlling threads in a concurrent application. Using the Executors Framework, developers can manage a pool of threads and execute tasks asynchronously, rather than manually creating and managing individual threads.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Executors Framework

1.  Thread Management:

<bdi>○</bdi>Automatically manages thread creation, reuse, and termination.

1.  Task Submission:

<bdi>○</bdi>Allows submission of tasks using Runnable, Callable, or a collection of tasks.

1.  Thread Pooling:

<bdi>○</bdi>Reuses existing threads to reduce overhead.

1.  Concurrency Utilities:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides flexible tools for handling concurrent tasks, such as scheduled execution and delayed tasks.

1.  Scalability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Optimizes thread usage for better performance in multi-core systems.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Core Components of the Executors Framework

| Component | Description |
| Executor | The core interface for executing tasks. |
| ExecutorService | A subinterface of Executor that provides methods to manage task execution and lifecycle control. |
| ScheduledExecutorService | A subinterface of ExecutorService for scheduling tasks to run after a delay or periodically. |
| ThreadPoolExecutor | A concrete class for managing thread pools. |
| ForkJoinPool | A specialized thread pool for tasks that can be divided into smaller sub-tasks. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Common Executors Methods

| Method | Description |
| newFixedThreadPool(int nThreads) | Creates a thread pool with a fixed number of threads. |
| newCachedThreadPool() | Creates a thread pool that dynamically adjusts to the workload. |
| newSingleThreadExecutor() | Creates a single-threaded executor. |
| newScheduledThreadPool(int nThreads) | Creates a thread pool for scheduling tasks with a delay or periodically. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Basic Usage of Executors

#### Code Example:

import java.util.concurrent.ExecutorService;

import java.util.concurrent.Executors;

class Task implements Runnable {

private final String name;

public Task(String name) {

this.name = name;

}

@Override

public void run() {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " is executing task: " + name);

try {

Thread.sleep(1000); // Simulate task execution time

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

}

}

public class ExecutorsExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

ExecutorService executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(3);

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

executor.submit(new Task("Task-" + i));

}

executor.shutdown(); // Gracefully shut down the executor

}

}

#### Output (Output may vary due to thread scheduling):

pool-1-thread-1 is executing task: Task-1

pool-1-thread-2 is executing task: Task-2

pool-1-thread-3 is executing task: Task-3

pool-1-thread-1 is executing task: Task-4

pool-1-thread-2 is executing task: Task-5

#### Explanation:

1.  Thread Pool:

<bdi>○</bdi>A fixed thread pool with 3 threads is created using Executors.newFixedThreadPool(3).

1.  Task Submission:

<bdi>○</bdi>Tasks are submitted using submit(), which places them in a queue.

1.  Thread Reuse:

<bdi>○</bdi>The 3 threads are reused to execute the 5 tasks.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Using Callable with Executors

The Callable interface allows tasks to return results or throw exceptions.

#### Code Example:

import java.util.concurrent.Callable;

import java.util.concurrent.ExecutorService;

import java.util.concurrent.Executors;

import java.util.concurrent.Future;

class FactorialTask implements Callable<Integer> {

private final int number;

public FactorialTask(int number) {

this.number = number;

}

@Override

public Integer call() throws Exception {

int result = 1;

for (int i = 1; i <= number; i++) {

result *= i;

}

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " calculated factorial of " + number);

return result;

}

}

public class CallableExample {

public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {

ExecutorService executor = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(2);

Future<Integer> future1 = executor.submit(new FactorialTask(5));

Future<Integer> future2 = executor.submit(new FactorialTask(7));

System.out.println("Factorial of 5: " + future1.get());

System.out.println("Factorial of 7: " + future2.get());

executor.shutdown();

}

}

#### Output:

pool-1-thread-1 calculated factorial of 5

pool-1-thread-2 calculated factorial of 7

Factorial of 5: 120

Factorial of 7: 5040

#### Explanation:

1.  Callable:

<bdi>○</bdi>FactorialTask implements Callable, and its call() method computes the factorial of a number.

1.  Future:

<bdi>○</bdi>submit() returns a Future object that holds the result of the task.

1.  Result Retrieval:

<bdi>○</bdi>future.get() blocks until the task is complete and retrieves the result.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Scheduled Tasks

The ScheduledExecutorService allows scheduling tasks to run after a delay or periodically.

#### Code Example:

import java.util.concurrent.Executors;

import java.util.concurrent.ScheduledExecutorService;

import java.util.concurrent.TimeUnit;

public class ScheduledExecutorExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

ScheduledExecutorService scheduler = Executors.newScheduledThreadPool(1);

Runnable task = () -> System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " is executing a scheduled task");

scheduler.scheduleAtFixedRate(task, 1, 3, TimeUnit.SECONDS);

try {

Thread.sleep(10000); // Let the task run for 10 seconds

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

scheduler.shutdown();

}

}

#### Output (Approximate):

pool-1-thread-1 is executing a scheduled task

pool-1-thread-1 is executing a scheduled task

pool-1-thread-1 is executing a scheduled task

pool-1-thread-1 is executing a scheduled task

#### Explanation:

1.  scheduleAtFixedRate:

<bdi>○</bdi>The task starts after an initial delay of 1 second and repeats every 3 seconds.

1.  Shutdown:

<bdi>○</bdi>After 10 seconds, the scheduler is shut down.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Using Cached Thread Pool

A cached thread pool dynamically creates new threads as needed and reuses idle threads.

#### Code Example:

import java.util.concurrent.ExecutorService;

import java.util.concurrent.Executors;

public class CachedThreadPoolExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

ExecutorService executor = Executors.newCachedThreadPool();

for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {

final int taskId = i;

executor.submit(() -> {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " is executing task: " + taskId);

try {

Thread.sleep(1000);

} catch (InterruptedException e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

});

}

executor.shutdown();

}

}

#### Output (Output may vary due to thread reuse):

pool-1-thread-1 is executing task: 1

pool-1-thread-2 is executing task: 2

pool-1-thread-3 is executing task: 3

pool-1-thread-4 is executing task: 4

pool-1-thread-5 is executing task: 5

#### Explanation:

1.  Dynamic Thread Creation:

<bdi>○</bdi>A new thread is created if no idle threads are available.

1.  Thread Reuse:

<bdi>○</bdi>Idle threads are reused for subsequent tasks.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Comparison of Thread Pools

| Thread Pool | Description | Use Case |
| Fixed Thread Pool | Creates a fixed number of threads. | Predictable number of threads required. |
| Cached Thread Pool | Creates threads dynamically and reuses idle threads. | Short-lived, bursty tasks. |
| Single Thread Executor | Executes tasks sequentially in a single thread. | Ensuring task order. |
| Scheduled Thread Pool | Executes tasks after a delay or periodically. | Scheduled or recurring tasks. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices

1.  Reuse Threads:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use thread pools to avoid the overhead of creating and destroying threads repeatedly.

1.  Shutdown Executors:

<bdi>○</bdi>Always call shutdown() or shutdownNow() to release resources after task execution.

1.  Exception Handling:

<bdi>○</bdi>Handle exceptions in tasks to avoid thread termination.

1.  Avoid Overloading:

<bdi>○</bdi>Monitor the thread pool size and queue capacity to prevent resource exhaustion.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

The Executors Framework simplifies thread management and provides powerful abstractions for concurrent programming:

1.  Core Components:

<bdi>○</bdi>Executors, ExecutorService, ScheduledExecutorService, and Callable.

1.  Thread Pools:

<bdi>○</bdi>Fixed, cached, single-threaded, and scheduled thread pools for different use cases.

1.  Key Benefits:

<bdi>○</bdi>Thread reuse, task scheduling, and flexible configuration.

## Lambda Expressions

Lambda expressions are a feature introduced in Java 8 that enable developers to write cleaner and more concise code by representing anonymous methods in a functional style. They are often used with functional interfaces to enable functional programming constructs.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Lambda Expressions

1.  Conciseness:

<bdi>○</bdi>Simplifies the creation of anonymous classes by reducing boilerplate code.

1.  Functional Programming:

<bdi>○</bdi>Facilitates a functional programming style using functional interfaces.

1.  Improved Readability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Allows inline definition of behavior, improving code clarity.

1.  Integration with Streams:

<bdi>○</bdi>Works seamlessly with the Java Streams API for data processing.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Syntax of Lambda Expressions

(parameter list) -> { body }

<bdi>●</bdi>Parameter List: Comma-separated list of parameters.

<bdi>●</bdi>Arrow Token (->): Separates parameters from the body.

<bdi>●</bdi>Body: Contains the logic or expression to execute.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Functional Interface

A functional interface is an interface with a single abstract method. Lambda expressions can provide the implementation of this abstract method.

Common Functional Interfaces in Java:

1.  Runnable - Method: void run()

1.  Callable<T> - Method: T call()

1.  Comparator<T> - Method: int compare(T o1, T o2)

1.  Predicate<T> - Method: boolean test(T t)

1.  Function<T, R> - Method: R apply(T t)

1.  Consumer<T> - Method: void accept(T t)

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Lambda Expression with Functional Interface

#### Code Example:

@FunctionalInterface

interface Greeting {

void sayHello(String name);

}

public class LambdaExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Lambda expression for the Greeting interface

Greeting greeting = name -> System.out.println("Hello, " + name + "!");

greeting.sayHello("Alice");

greeting.sayHello("Bob");

}

}

#### Output:

Hello, Alice!

Hello, Bob!

#### Explanation:

1.  Functional Interface:

<bdi>○</bdi>Greeting has a single abstract method sayHello(String name).

1.  Lambda Expression:

<bdi>○</bdi>Implements the sayHello method in a concise way.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Lambda with Multiple Parameters

#### Code Example:

@FunctionalInterface

interface Calculator {

int operate(int a, int b);

}

public class LambdaMultipleParameters {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Addition using lambda

Calculator addition = (a, b) -> a + b;

// Multiplication using lambda

Calculator multiplication = (a, b) -> a * b;

System.out.println("Addition: " + addition.operate(5, 3));

System.out.println("Multiplication: " + multiplication.operate(5, 3));

}

}

#### Output:

Addition: 8

Multiplication: 15

#### Explanation:

1.  Multiple Parameters:

<bdi>○</bdi>(a, b) specifies two parameters for the operate method.

1.  Behavior:

<bdi>○</bdi>The lambda expression defines different behaviors for addition and multiplication.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Using Lambdas with Collections

#### Code Example:

import java.util.Arrays;

import java.util.List;

public class LambdaWithCollections {

public static void main(String[] args) {

List<String> names = Arrays.asList("Alice", "Bob", "Charlie");

// Iterating with lambda

names.forEach(name -> System.out.println(name));

// Sorting with lambda

names.sort((a, b) -> b.compareTo(a));

System.out.println("Sorted List: " + names);

}

}

#### Output:

Alice

Bob

Charlie

Sorted List: [Charlie, Bob, Alice]

#### Explanation:

1.  forEach:

<bdi>○</bdi>Accepts a lambda expression to define the action for each element.

1.  Custom Sorting:

<bdi>○</bdi>sort() uses a lambda to specify a descending order comparator.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Using Predicates with Streams

The Predicate functional interface is commonly used with the Streams API to filter data.

#### Code Example:

import java.util.Arrays;

import java.util.List;

import java.util.stream.Collectors;

public class LambdaWithPredicate {

public static void main(String[] args) {

List<Integer> numbers = Arrays.asList(1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6);

// Filtering even numbers

List<Integer> evenNumbers = numbers.stream()

.filter(n -> n % 2 == 0)

.collect(Collectors.toList());

System.out.println("Even Numbers: " + evenNumbers);

}

}

#### Output:

Even Numbers: [2, 4, 6]

#### Explanation:

1.  Predicate:

<bdi>○</bdi>The lambda n -> n % 2 == 0 defines a condition to filter even numbers.

1.  Stream API:

<bdi>○</bdi>Processes the data in a functional and declarative style.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Returning Values with Lambdas

#### Code Example:

@FunctionalInterface

interface Square {

int calculate(int x);

}

public class LambdaReturnValues {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Lambda expression with return value

Square square = x -> x * x;

System.out.println("Square of 4: " + square.calculate(4));

System.out.println("Square of 7: " + square.calculate(7));

}

}

#### Output:

Square of 4: 16

Square of 7: 49

#### Explanation:

1.  Return Value:

<bdi>○</bdi>The lambda returns the square of the input x.

1.  Conciseness:

<bdi>○</bdi>The single-expression body does not require braces or an explicit return statement.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Method References with Lambdas

Method references are a shorthand for lambdas that call a specific method.

#### Code Example:

import java.util.Arrays;

import java.util.List;

public class LambdaMethodReference {

public static void main(String[] args) {

List<String> names = Arrays.asList("Alice", "Bob", "Charlie");

// Using method reference

names.forEach(System.out::println);

}

}

#### Output:

Alice

Bob

Charlie

#### Explanation:

1.  Method Reference:

<bdi>○</bdi>System.out::println is a shorthand for name -> System.out.println(name).

1.  Streamlined Code:

<bdi>○</bdi>Improves readability and reduces boilerplate.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 7\. Combining Functional Interfaces

#### Code Example:

import java.util.function.Predicate;

public class LambdaCombiningPredicates {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Predicate<Integer> isEven = n -> n % 2 == 0;

Predicate<Integer> isPositive = n -> n > 0;

int number = 4;

if (isEven.and(isPositive).test(number)) {

System.out.println(number + " is positive and even.");

}

}

}

#### Output:

4 is positive and even.

#### Explanation:

1.  Combining Predicates:

<bdi>○</bdi>and() combines the isEven and isPositive conditions.

1.  Declarative Condition:

<bdi>○</bdi>The combined condition improves readability.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Advantages of Lambda Expressions

1.  Conciseness:

<bdi>○</bdi>Eliminates boilerplate code for anonymous classes.

1.  Improved Readability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Inline behavior definition enhances code clarity.

1.  Functional Programming:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enables use of Streams and declarative coding style.

1.  Efficient Use of Functional Interfaces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Simplifies the implementation of common behaviors.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices

1.  Use Functional Interfaces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensure the target interface is a functional interface.

1.  Keep Lambdas Simple:

<bdi>○</bdi>Avoid complex logic inside lambda expressions.

1.  Prefer Method References When Possible:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use method references for improved readability.

1.  Avoid Side Effects:

<bdi>○</bdi>Keep lambda expressions stateless for better maintainability.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Syntax:

<bdi>○</bdi>(parameters) -> { body } is the basic syntax for lambda expressions.

<bdi>●</bdi>Functional Interfaces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Work seamlessly with interfaces like Runnable, Predicate, Function, and more.

<bdi>●</bdi>Integration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Lambdas enhance Java's Streams API for functional programming.

<bdi>●</bdi>Examples:

<bdi>○</bdi>Includes sorting, filtering, custom behaviors, and method references.

Lambda expressions are a cornerstone of modern Java, making it more expressive and concise. They allow developers to embrace functional programming while retaining the object-oriented roots of Java.

## Functional Interfaces

A functional interface in Java is an interface that contains exactly one abstract method. These interfaces serve as the foundation for lambda expressions and method references introduced in Java 8, enabling functional programming constructs.

Functional interfaces may have other methods, such as default and static methods, but they must have only one abstract method to qualify as functional interfaces.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Characteristics of Functional Interfaces

1.  Single Abstract Method (SAM):

<bdi>○</bdi>Functional interfaces are also called SAM interfaces because they have one abstract method.

1.  @FunctionalInterface Annotation:

<bdi>○</bdi>It is not mandatory but highly recommended to annotate functional interfaces with @FunctionalInterface to enforce the constraint at compile time.

1.  Lambda Support:

<bdi>○</bdi>Functional interfaces can be implemented with lambda expressions for concise code.

1.  Predefined Interfaces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Java provides many predefined functional interfaces in the java.util.function package.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Defining a Functional Interface

#### Code Example:

@FunctionalInterface

interface Greeting {

void sayHello(String name);

}

#### Explanation:

1.  Single Abstract Method:

<bdi>○</bdi>The interface has only one abstract method sayHello.

1.  @FunctionalInterface Annotation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensures that no additional abstract methods are accidentally added.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Built-In Functional Interfaces

The java.util.function package provides many predefined functional interfaces, categorized as follows:

| Interface | Abstract Method | Description |
| Predicate<T> | boolean test(T t) | Represents a condition. Returns true or false. |
| Function<T, R> | R apply(T t) | Transforms an input of type T into a result of type R. |
| Consumer<T> | void accept(T t) | Performs an action on an input of type T. |
| Supplier<T> | T get() | Supplies a value of type T. |
| UnaryOperator<T> | T apply(T t) | A specialization of Function<T, R> where T and R are the same. |
| BinaryOperator<T> | T apply(T t1, T t2) | A specialization of BiFunction<T, T, T>. Operates on two inputs of type T. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Custom Functional Interface Example

#### Code Example:

@FunctionalInterface

interface Calculator {

int operate(int a, int b);

}

public class FunctionalInterfaceExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Using a lambda expression

Calculator addition = (a, b) -> a + b;

Calculator subtraction = (a, b) -> a - b;

System.out.println("Addition: " + addition.operate(10, 5));

System.out.println("Subtraction: " + subtraction.operate(10, 5));

}

}

#### Output:

Addition: 15

Subtraction: 5

#### Explanation:

1.  Lambda Expressions:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provide concise implementations for the operate method.

1.  Reusability:

<bdi>○</bdi>The same interface is used for different operations.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Using Predefined Functional Interfaces

#### Predicate (Conditional Check)

#### Code Example:

import java.util.function.Predicate;

public class PredicateExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Predicate<Integer> isEven = n -> n % 2 == 0;

System.out.println("Is 4 even? " + isEven.test(4));

System.out.println("Is 7 even? " + isEven.test(7));

}

}

#### Output:

Is 4 even? true

Is 7 even? false

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Function (Transformation)

#### Code Example:

import java.util.function.Function;

public class FunctionExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Function<String, Integer> stringLength = str -> str.length();

System.out.println("Length of 'hello': " + stringLength.apply("hello"));

System.out.println("Length of 'functional interfaces': " + stringLength.apply("functional interfaces"));

}

}

#### Output:

Length of 'hello': 5

Length of 'functional interfaces': 21

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Consumer (Action on Input)

#### Code Example:

import java.util.function.Consumer;

public class ConsumerExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Consumer<String> printMessage = message -> System.out.println("Message: " + message);

printMessage.accept("Hello, world!");

printMessage.accept("Functional programming in Java.");

}

}

#### Output:

Message: Hello, world!

Message: Functional programming in Java.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Supplier (Value Generation)

#### Code Example:

import java.util.function.Supplier;

public class SupplierExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Supplier<Double> randomValue = () -> Math.random();

System.out.println("Random Value 1: " + randomValue.get());

System.out.println("Random Value 2: " + randomValue.get());

}

}

#### Output:

Random Value 1: 0.123456789

Random Value 2: 0.987654321

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Combining Functional Interfaces

#### Code Example:

import java.util.function.Function;

import java.util.function.Predicate;

public class CombiningFunctionalInterfaces {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Predicate<Integer> isPositive = n -> n > 0;

Function<Integer, Integer> square = n -> n * n;

int number = 4;

if (isPositive.test(number)) {

System.out.println("Square of " + number + ": " + square.apply(number));

}

int negativeNumber = -3;

if (!isPositive.test(negativeNumber)) {

System.out.println(negativeNumber + " is not positive.");

}

}

}

#### Output:

Square of 4: 16

-3 is not positive.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Points About Functional Interfaces

1.  Default and Static Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>Functional interfaces can have default and static methods but only one abstract method.

1.  Integration with Lambdas:

<bdi>○</bdi>Lambda expressions simplify the implementation of functional interfaces.

1.  Reusable Utilities:

<bdi>○</bdi>The java.util.function package provides a rich set of predefined interfaces.

1.  Custom Interfaces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Developers can define their own functional interfaces using the @FunctionalInterface annotation.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices

1.  Use Predefined Interfaces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Prefer built-in functional interfaces over custom ones to improve code consistency and readability.

1.  Keep Lambdas Simple:

<bdi>○</bdi>Avoid complex logic in lambda expressions; use method references or helper methods if needed.

1.  Annotate Functional Interfaces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use @FunctionalInterface to enforce the single-method constraint.

1.  Avoid Side Effects:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensure lambdas and functional interfaces are stateless and side-effect-free.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Definition:

<bdi>○</bdi>Functional interfaces have a single abstract method and support lambdas.

<bdi>●</bdi>Predefined Interfaces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Common examples include Predicate, Function, Consumer, and Supplier.

<bdi>●</bdi>Custom Interfaces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Allow developers to define reusable and expressive functional constructs.

<bdi>●</bdi>Integration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Work seamlessly with lambda expressions, streams, and the Java Collections Framework.

Functional interfaces are a cornerstone of modern Java programming, enabling a concise and functional programming style.

## Annotations

Annotations in Java provide metadata about the code to the compiler, JVM, or tools. They do not affect the execution of the program but provide instructions for processing the code. Introduced in Java 5, annotations are used for purposes like compiler checking, runtime processing, and code generation.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Annotations

1.  Metadata Provider:

<bdi>○</bdi>Annotations provide metadata about classes, methods, variables, etc.

1.  Customizable:

<bdi>○</bdi>Developers can create their own annotations.

1.  Annotation Processors:

<bdi>○</bdi>Tools and frameworks can process annotations at compile time or runtime to perform specific actions.

1.  Integration with Frameworks:

<bdi>○</bdi>Widely used in frameworks like Spring, Hibernate, and JUnit.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Built-in Annotations

| Annotation | Description |
| @Override | Indicates a method overrides a superclass method. |
| @Deprecated | Marks a method, class, or field as deprecated. |
| @SuppressWarnings | Suppresses compiler warnings for specific issues. |
| @FunctionalInterface | Ensures an interface has exactly one abstract method. |
| @SafeVarargs | Suppresses warnings for varargs operations. |
| @Retention | Specifies how long an annotation is retained (runtime, compile-time, etc.). |
| @Target | Specifies the element types an annotation can be applied to (class, method, etc.). |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Built-in Annotations Example

#### Code Example:

class Parent {

@Deprecated

public void oldMethod() {

System.out.println("This method is deprecated.");

}

}

class Child extends Parent {

@Override

public void oldMethod() {

System.out.println("Overriding deprecated method.");

}

}

public class BuiltInAnnotationsExample {

@SuppressWarnings("deprecation")

public static void main(String[] args) {

Parent parent = new Child();

parent.oldMethod();

}

}

#### Output:

Overriding deprecated method.

#### Explanation:

1.  @Deprecated:

<bdi>○</bdi>Marks oldMethod as deprecated, signaling that it should no longer be used.

1.  @Override:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensures that the method correctly overrides a superclass method.

1.  @SuppressWarnings:

<bdi>○</bdi>Suppresses warnings about using deprecated methods.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Retention and Target Annotations

Annotations can specify how long they are retained and where they can be applied using @Retention and @Target.

#### Code Example:

import java.lang.annotation.ElementType;

import java.lang.annotation.Retention;

import java.lang.annotation.RetentionPolicy;

import java.lang.annotation.Target;

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)

@Target({ElementType.METHOD, ElementType.TYPE})

@interface CustomAnnotation {

String value();

}

@CustomAnnotation(value = "Class Annotation")

class AnnotatedClass {

@CustomAnnotation(value = "Method Annotation")

public void annotatedMethod() {

System.out.println("This method is annotated.");

}

}

public class RetentionTargetExample {

public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {

// Access class annotation

CustomAnnotation classAnnotation = AnnotatedClass.class.getAnnotation(CustomAnnotation.class);

System.out.println("Class Annotation: " + classAnnotation.value());

// Access method annotation

CustomAnnotation methodAnnotation = AnnotatedClass.class

.getMethod("annotatedMethod")

.getAnnotation(CustomAnnotation.class);

System.out.println("Method Annotation: " + methodAnnotation.value());

}

}

#### Output:

Class Annotation: Class Annotation

Method Annotation: Method Annotation

#### Explanation:

1.  @Retention:

<bdi>○</bdi>RUNTIME indicates the annotation is available at runtime through reflection.

1.  @Target:

<bdi>○</bdi>Specifies that the annotation can only be applied to classes and methods.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Creating Custom Annotations

#### Code Example:

import java.lang.annotation.Retention;

import java.lang.annotation.RetentionPolicy;

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)

@interface Info {

String author();

String date();

int version() default 1;

}

@Info(author = "Alice", date = "2023-12-03", version = 2)

public class CustomAnnotationExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Info annotation = CustomAnnotationExample.class.getAnnotation(Info.class);

System.out.println("Author: " + annotation.author());

System.out.println("Date: " + annotation.date());

System.out.println("Version: " + annotation.version());

}

}

#### Output:

Author: Alice

Date: 2023-12-03

Version: 2

#### Explanation:

1.  Default Values:

<bdi>○</bdi>The version attribute has a default value, so it is optional during annotation use.

1.  Retention:

<bdi>○</bdi>The annotation is retained at runtime, allowing it to be accessed via reflection.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Marker Annotations

A marker annotation is an annotation without any attributes, used as a marker or flag.

#### Code Example:

import java.lang.annotation.ElementType;

import java.lang.annotation.Retention;

import java.lang.annotation.RetentionPolicy;

import java.lang.annotation.Target;

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)

@Target(ElementType.METHOD)

@interface Marker {}

class MarkerDemo {

@Marker

public void markedMethod() {

System.out.println("This method is marked.");

}

}

public class MarkerAnnotationExample {

public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {

if (MarkerDemo.class.getMethod("markedMethod").isAnnotationPresent(Marker.class)) {

System.out.println("Marker annotation is present.");

}

}

}

#### Output:

Marker annotation is present.

#### Explanation:

1.  Marker Annotation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Used to indicate that a method is marked for special behavior.

1.  Reflection:

<bdi>○</bdi>The presence of the annotation is checked at runtime.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Repeatable Annotations

Java 8 introduced repeatable annotations, which allow the same annotation to be applied multiple times to a single element.

#### Code Example:

import java.lang.annotation.Repeatable;

import java.lang.annotation.Retention;

import java.lang.annotation.RetentionPolicy;

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)

@Repeatable(Schedules.class)

@interface Schedule {

String day();

}

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)

@interface Schedules {

Schedule[] value();

}

@Schedule(day = "Monday")

@Schedule(day = "Wednesday")

@Schedule(day = "Friday")

class RepeatableExample {}

public class RepeatableAnnotationExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Schedule[] schedules = RepeatableExample.class.getAnnotationsByType(Schedule.class);

for (Schedule schedule : schedules) {

System.out.println("Day: " + schedule.day());

}

}

}

#### Output:

Day: Monday

Day: Wednesday

Day: Friday

#### Explanation:

1.  Repeatable Annotations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Defined using @Repeatable and a container annotation (@Schedules).

1.  Reflection:

<bdi>○</bdi>All instances of the annotation are retrieved using getAnnotationsByType().

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Meta-Annotations

Meta-annotations are annotations applied to other annotations to define their behavior.

| Meta-Annotation | Description |
| @Retention | Specifies how long an annotation is retained (e.g., SOURCE, CLASS, RUNTIME). |
| @Target | Specifies where an annotation can be applied (e.g., class, method, field). |
| @Documented | Indicates that the annotation should be included in the Javadoc. |
| @Inherited | Allows an annotation to be inherited by subclasses. |
| @Repeatable | Allows the annotation to be repeated on the same element. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Advantages of Annotations

1.  Improved Code Clarity:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provide metadata about the behavior of code elements.

1.  Integration with Tools:

<bdi>○</bdi>Frameworks and tools (e.g., Spring, Hibernate) use annotations for configuration.

1.  Runtime Processing:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enable dynamic behavior and code generation.

1.  Reduced Boilerplate:

<bdi>○</bdi>Replace external configuration files with in-code annotations.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices

1.  Use Descriptive Names:

<bdi>○</bdi>Define meaningful names for custom annotations.

1.  Leverage Built-In Annotations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use standard annotations like @Override and @Deprecated whenever possible.

1.  Retention Policy:

<bdi>○</bdi>Set an appropriate retention policy (RUNTIME, CLASS, or SOURCE) based on usage.

1.  Avoid Overuse:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use annotations judiciously to prevent clutter and maintain readability.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Annotations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provide metadata for the compiler, tools, or frameworks.

<bdi>●</bdi>Built-in Annotations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Includes @Override, @Deprecated, @FunctionalInterface, etc.

<bdi>●</bdi>Custom Annotations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Allow developers to define reusable metadata.

<bdi>●</bdi>Repeatable Annotations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enable applying the same annotation multiple times.

<bdi>●</bdi>Meta-Annotations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Control the behavior and applicability of annotations.

Annotations enhance Java's capabilities by enabling declarative programming, simplifying configuration, and improving readability.

## Custom Annotations

Custom annotations are developer-defined annotations used to provide metadata about program elements like classes, methods, fields, etc. These annotations can be processed at runtime, compile time, or by tools like annotation processors.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Components of a Custom Annotation

1.  Annotation Declaration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Declared using @interface.

1.  Attributes:

<bdi>○</bdi>Can define key-value pairs within the annotation.

1.  Retention Policy:

<bdi>○</bdi>Specifies how long the annotation is retained (SOURCE, CLASS, RUNTIME).

1.  Target:

<bdi>○</bdi>Specifies where the annotation can be applied (e.g., class, method, field).

1.  Defaults:

<bdi>○</bdi>Default values can be provided for attributes.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Meta-Annotations

Meta-annotations are used to define the behavior of custom annotations:

| Meta-Annotation | Description |
| @Retention | Defines how long the annotation is retained (SOURCE, CLASS, RUNTIME). |
| @Target | Specifies where the annotation can be applied (METHOD, FIELD, etc.). |
| @Documented | Indicates that the annotation should appear in Javadoc. |
| @Inherited | Specifies that the annotation can be inherited by subclasses. |
| @Repeatable | Allows the annotation to be applied multiple times to the same element. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Basic Custom Annotation

#### Code Example:

import java.lang.annotation.Retention;

import java.lang.annotation.RetentionPolicy;

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME) // Retain annotation at runtime

@interface MyAnnotation {

String value();

}

public class CustomAnnotationExample {

@MyAnnotation(value = "Example Annotation")

public void myMethod() {

System.out.println("Annotated Method.");

}

public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {

CustomAnnotationExample example = new CustomAnnotationExample();

example.myMethod();

// Accessing annotation at runtime

MyAnnotation annotation = CustomAnnotationExample.class

.getMethod("myMethod")

.getAnnotation(MyAnnotation.class);

System.out.println("Annotation Value: " + annotation.value());

}

}

#### Output:

Annotated Method.

Annotation Value: Example Annotation

#### Explanation:

1.  Declaration:

<bdi>○</bdi>@interface MyAnnotation defines a custom annotation.

1.  Attribute:

<bdi>○</bdi>The value attribute stores metadata.

1.  Reflection:

<bdi>○</bdi>The annotation is accessed using reflection at runtime.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Annotation with Multiple Attributes

#### Code Example:

import java.lang.annotation.Retention;

import java.lang.annotation.RetentionPolicy;

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)

@interface Info {

String author();

String date();

int version() default 1; // Default value for version

}

@Info(author = "Alice", date = "2023-12-03", version = 2)

public class MultiAttributeAnnotationExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Info info = MultiAttributeAnnotationExample.class.getAnnotation(Info.class);

System.out.println("Author: " + info.author());

System.out.println("Date: " + info.date());

System.out.println("Version: " + info.version());

}

}

#### Output:

Author: Alice

Date: 2023-12-03

Version: 2

#### Explanation:

1.  Multiple Attributes:

<bdi>○</bdi>The Info annotation has three attributes: author, date, and version.

1.  Default Values:

<bdi>○</bdi>If version is not specified, the default value 1 is used.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Restricting Annotation Targets

#### Code Example:

import java.lang.annotation.ElementType;

import java.lang.annotation.Retention;

import java.lang.annotation.RetentionPolicy;

import java.lang.annotation.Target;

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)

@Target({ElementType.FIELD, ElementType.METHOD}) // Restrict usage to fields and methods

@interface FieldMethodAnnotation {

String description();

}

public class TargetAnnotationExample {

@FieldMethodAnnotation(description = "Annotated Field")

private String field;

@FieldMethodAnnotation(description = "Annotated Method")

public void annotatedMethod() {

System.out.println("Annotated Method.");

}

public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {

TargetAnnotationExample example = new TargetAnnotationExample();

FieldMethodAnnotation fieldAnnotation = TargetAnnotationExample.class

.getDeclaredField("field")

.getAnnotation(FieldMethodAnnotation.class);

System.out.println("Field Annotation: " + fieldAnnotation.description());

FieldMethodAnnotation methodAnnotation = TargetAnnotationExample.class

.getMethod("annotatedMethod")

.getAnnotation(FieldMethodAnnotation.class);

System.out.println("Method Annotation: " + methodAnnotation.description());

}

}

#### Output:

Field Annotation: Annotated Field

Method Annotation: Annotated Method

#### Explanation:

1.  @Target:

<bdi>○</bdi>Restricts the annotation usage to fields and methods.

1.  Reflection:

<bdi>○</bdi>Accesses annotations on fields and methods.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Marker Annotations

A marker annotation has no attributes and acts as a flag.

#### Code Example:

import java.lang.annotation.ElementType;

import java.lang.annotation.Retention;

import java.lang.annotation.RetentionPolicy;

import java.lang.annotation.Target;

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)

@Target(ElementType.METHOD)

@interface Marker {}

class MarkerExample {

@Marker

public void markedMethod() {

System.out.println("This method is marked.");

}

}

public class MarkerAnnotationDemo {

public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {

MarkerExample example = new MarkerExample();

if (MarkerExample.class.getMethod("markedMethod").isAnnotationPresent(Marker.class)) {

System.out.println("Marker annotation is present.");

}

}

}

#### Output:

Marker annotation is present.

#### Explanation:

1.  Marker Annotation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Acts as a flag without any attributes.

1.  Reflection:

<bdi>○</bdi>Checks the presence of the annotation.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Repeatable Annotations

Java 8 introduced repeatable annotations, allowing the same annotation to be applied multiple times.

#### Code Example:

import java.lang.annotation.Repeatable;

import java.lang.annotation.Retention;

import java.lang.annotation.RetentionPolicy;

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)

@Repeatable(Schedules.class)

@interface Schedule {

String day();

}

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)

@interface Schedules {

Schedule[] value();

}

@Schedule(day = "Monday")

@Schedule(day = "Wednesday")

@Schedule(day = "Friday")

class RepeatableAnnotationExample {}

public class RepeatableDemo {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Schedule[] schedules = RepeatableAnnotationExample.class.getAnnotationsByType(Schedule.class);

for (Schedule schedule : schedules) {

System.out.println("Day: " + schedule.day());

}

}

}

#### Output:

Day: Monday

Day: Wednesday

Day: Friday

#### Explanation:

1.  Repeatable Annotation:

<bdi>○</bdi>The @Repeatable annotation allows @Schedule to be used multiple times.

1.  Container Annotation:

<bdi>○</bdi>@Schedules acts as a container for multiple @Schedule annotations.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices for Custom Annotations

1.  Use Meta-Annotations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Always define retention and target using @Retention and @Target.

1.  Document Annotations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use @Documented to include annotations in Javadoc.

1.  Default Values:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provide default values for optional attributes.

1.  Avoid Overuse:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use annotations judiciously to avoid clutter.

1.  Process Annotations Efficiently:

<bdi>○</bdi>Leverage tools like reflection or annotation processors for runtime or compile-time processing.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Definition:

<bdi>○</bdi>Custom annotations are defined using @interface and can have attributes.

<bdi>●</bdi>Meta-Annotations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Control the behavior and applicability of custom annotations using @Retention, @Target, etc.

<bdi>●</bdi>Usage:

<bdi>○</bdi>Used for metadata, runtime processing, or configuration in frameworks.

<bdi>●</bdi>Advanced Features:

<bdi>○</bdi>Support for marker annotations, repeatable annotations, and multiple attributes.

Custom annotations enhance the flexibility of Java, enabling developers to implement powerful metadata-driven programming constructs.

# Java Development Tools

## Debugging Java Applications

Debugging is the process of identifying and fixing issues or errors in a program. In Java, debugging involves analyzing code execution to understand the behavior, identify bugs, and resolve them effectively.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Tools and Techniques for Debugging in Java

1.  Using Debugging Tools:

<bdi>○</bdi>Integrated Development Environments (IDEs) like Eclipse, IntelliJ IDEA, and NetBeans have built-in debugging tools.

<bdi>○</bdi>Debuggers allow breakpoints, stepping through code, and inspecting variables.

1.  Using Logs:

<bdi>○</bdi>Logging frameworks like SLF4J, Log4j, or java.util.logging are used to print diagnostic information.

1.  Using System.out.println:

<bdi>○</bdi>A simple way to debug by printing variables and messages to the console.

1.  Exception Handling:

<bdi>○</bdi>Analyze stack traces to identify errors and problematic code segments.

1.  Analyzing Core Dumps:

<bdi>○</bdi>Helps diagnose issues in the JVM or native code.

1.  Using Profilers:

<bdi>○</bdi>Tools like VisualVM and JProfiler help in performance debugging and memory analysis.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Debugging Using an IDE (Breakpoints)

#### Code Example:

public class DebuggingExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int number = 10;

int result = calculateFactorial(number);

System.out.println("Factorial of " + number + " is: " + result);

}

public static int calculateFactorial(int n) {

if (n == 1) {

return 1;

}

return n * calculateFactorial(n - 1); // Recursive call

}

}

#### Steps for Debugging in an IDE:

1.  Set a Breakpoint:

<bdi>○</bdi>Place a breakpoint on the recursive call n * calculateFactorial(n - 1).

1.  Start Debugging:

<bdi>○</bdi>Run the program in debug mode.

1.  Step Over:

<bdi>○</bdi>Execute the next line without going into method calls.

1.  Step Into:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enter the method being called to debug its internal execution.

1.  Inspect Variables:

<bdi>○</bdi>View the values of n and intermediate results in the debugger's variable view.

#### Output in Debugger:

<bdi>●</bdi>You will see the recursion stack unfold step by step.

<bdi>●</bdi>Values of n:

<bdi>○</bdi>n = 10

<bdi>○</bdi>n = 9

<bdi>○</bdi>...

<bdi>○</bdi>n = 1

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Debugging with System.out.println

#### Code Example:

public class DebuggingWithPrint {

public static void main(String[] args) {

int number = 10;

int result = calculateFactorial(number);

System.out.println("Factorial of " + number + " is: " + result);

}

public static int calculateFactorial(int n) {

System.out.println("Calculating factorial for n = " + n);

if (n == 1) {

return 1;

}

int partialResult = n * calculateFactorial(n - 1);

System.out.println("Intermediate result for n = " + n + ": " + partialResult);

return partialResult;

}

}

#### Output:

Calculating factorial for n = 10

Calculating factorial for n = 9

Calculating factorial for n = 8

...

Intermediate result for n = 2: 2

Intermediate result for n = 3: 6

...

Factorial of 10 is: 3628800

#### Explanation:

1.  Print Statements:

<bdi>○</bdi>Print the current value of n and intermediate results.

1.  Tracing Execution:

<bdi>○</bdi>Helps trace the flow of recursion and verify correctness.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Debugging with Logging Frameworks

#### Code Example:

import org.slf4j.Logger;

import org.slf4j.LoggerFactory;

public class LoggingExample {

private static final Logger logger = LoggerFactory.getLogger(LoggingExample.class);

public static void main(String[] args) {

int number = 10;

int result = calculateFactorial(number);

logger.info("Factorial of {} is {}", number, result);

}

public static int calculateFactorial(int n) {

logger.debug("Calculating factorial for n = {}", n);

if (n == 1) {

return 1;

}

int partialResult = n * calculateFactorial(n - 1);

logger.debug("Intermediate result for n = {}: {}", n, partialResult);

return partialResult;

}

}

#### Output (Log):

DEBUG [main] - Calculating factorial for n = 10

DEBUG [main] - Calculating factorial for n = 9

...

DEBUG [main] - Intermediate result for n = 2: 2

DEBUG [main] - Intermediate result for n = 3: 6

INFO [main] - Factorial of 10 is 3628800

#### Explanation:

1.  Logging Levels:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use DEBUG for detailed execution flow and INFO for key results.

1.  Advantages:

<bdi>○</bdi>Logs can be controlled with configurations to show or hide specific levels.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Debugging with Exception Handling

#### Code Example:

public class ExceptionHandlingExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

try {

int result = divideNumbers(10, 0);

System.out.println("Result: " + result);

} catch (ArithmeticException e) {

System.err.println("Error: Division by zero.");

e.printStackTrace();

}

}

public static int divideNumbers(int a, int b) {

return a / b; // Division by zero occurs here

}

}

#### Output:

Error: Division by zero.

java.lang.ArithmeticException: / by zero

<bdi>at ExceptionHandlingExample.divideNumbers(ExceptionHandlingExample.java:10)</bdi>

<bdi>at ExceptionHandlingExample.main(ExceptionHandlingExample.java:6)</bdi>

#### Explanation:

1.  Stack Trace:

<bdi>○</bdi>Pinpoints the exact location of the exception (divideNumbers at line 10).

1.  Error Handling:

<bdi>○</bdi>Prevents program termination by catching exceptions.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Using Profilers for Performance Debugging

#### Profilers:

<bdi>●</bdi>Tools like VisualVM, JProfiler, or YourKit are used to analyze memory, CPU usage, and thread performance.

#### Steps:

1.  Attach the profiler to the running application.

1.  Monitor:

<bdi>○</bdi>CPU-intensive methods.

<bdi>○</bdi>Memory usage and garbage collection.

<bdi>○</bdi>Deadlocks or thread contention.

1.  Optimize:

<bdi>○</bdi>Identify bottlenecks and improve them.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Analyzing Deadlocks

#### Code Example:

class SharedResource {

synchronized void methodA() {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " locked methodA");

try { Thread.sleep(100); } catch (InterruptedException e) {}

synchronized (this) {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " locked methodB");

}

}

synchronized void methodB() {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " locked methodB");

try { Thread.sleep(100); } catch (InterruptedException e) {}

synchronized (this) {

System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + " locked methodA");

}

}

}

public class DeadlockDebuggingExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

SharedResource resource = new SharedResource();

Thread t1 = new Thread(() -> resource.methodA(), "Thread-1");

Thread t2 = new Thread(() -> resource.methodB(), "Thread-2");

t1.start();

t2.start();

}

}

#### Steps to Debug Deadlock:

1.  Observe Hanging Threads:

<bdi>○</bdi>If the program doesn't terminate, check for deadlocks.

1.  Use IDE or Profiler:

<bdi>○</bdi>Modern IDEs and profilers detect and display deadlocks.

1.  Thread Dumps:

<bdi>○</bdi>Analyze thread dumps using tools like jstack to identify the cause.

#### Output:

Thread-1 locked methodA

Thread-2 locked methodB

(Hangs due to deadlock)

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 7\. Common Debugging Commands

| Command | Description |
| jstack | Prints thread dump of a Java process. |
| jmap | Prints memory usage and heap statistics. |
| jconsole | Monitors JVM performance and memory. |
| jvisualvm | Visualizes thread activity and memory consumption. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices for Debugging

1.  Understand the Problem:

<bdi>○</bdi>Analyze the symptoms and isolate the issue.

1.  Start Small:

<bdi>○</bdi>Debug small code segments or unit tests.

1.  Use Logs:

<bdi>○</bdi>Add meaningful logging to critical sections.

1.  Leverage IDE Tools:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use breakpoints, watches, and step-through execution.

1.  Avoid Overuse of Print Statements:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use logging frameworks instead for scalability.

1.  Analyze Exceptions:

<bdi>○</bdi>Always read the full stack trace to locate the issue.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Techniques:

<bdi>○</bdi>Breakpoints, logs, print statements, and exception handling are essential debugging tools.

<bdi>●</bdi>Tools:

<bdi>○</bdi>IDE debuggers, profilers, and monitoring tools like VisualVM are invaluable.

<bdi>●</bdi>Focus Areas:

<bdi>○</bdi>Logical errors, performance issues, and concurrency bugs (e.g., deadlocks).

<bdi>●</bdi>Best Practices:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use systematic approaches and tools to streamline the debugging process.

Debugging is a critical skill in Java development, and mastering these tools and techniques ensures efficient and error-free coding.

## Using Build Tools in Java: Maven and Gradle

Build tools like Maven and Gradle automate the process of compiling, packaging, testing, and managing dependencies in Java projects. They also support version control, deployment, and integration tasks.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### What Are Build Tools?

1.  Build Automation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Simplify repetitive tasks like compilation, testing, packaging, and deploying applications.

1.  Dependency Management:

<bdi>○</bdi>Automatically download and manage libraries required for the project.

1.  Project Structure Standardization:

<bdi>○</bdi>Enforce consistent project layouts across teams.

1.  Integration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Seamlessly integrate with CI/CD pipelines, IDEs, and repositories.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Comparison of Maven and Gradle

| Feature | Maven | Gradle |
| Configuration | XML-based (pom.xml). | Groovy/Kotlin DSL-based (build.gradle). |
| Performance | Sequential build steps. | Faster, supports incremental builds. |
| Dependency Management | Relies on centralized repositories. | More flexible dependency resolution. |
| Customizability | Limited to plugins. | Highly customizable with rich DSL. |
| Ease of Use | Beginner-friendly. | Requires learning DSL syntax. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

## 1\. Using Maven

### Setup

#### Install Maven

1.  Download and install Maven from the [](https://maven.apache.org/) [Apache Maven website](https://maven.apache.org/).

1.  Add Maven's bin directory to your system's PATH.

#### Verify Installation

Run:

mvn -v

You should see the installed Maven version.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Creating a Maven Project

#### Command to Create a New Project:

mvn archetype:generate -DgroupId=com.example -DartifactId=MyMavenApp -DarchetypeArtifactId=maven-archetype-quickstart -DinteractiveMode=false

This command:

<bdi>●</bdi>groupId: Identifier for your organization (e.g., com.example).

<bdi>●</bdi>artifactId: Name of your project.

<bdi>●</bdi>maven-archetype-quickstart: Template for a simple Java project.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Project Structure

MyMavenApp/

├── pom.xml

├── src/

├── main/

│ ├── java/

│ │ └── com/example/App.java

└── test/

└── java/

└── com/example/AppTest.java

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Editing pom.xml

The pom.xml (Project Object Model) file defines the build configuration and dependencies.

#### Example pom.xml:

<project xmlns="http://maven.apache.org/POM/4.0.0">

<modelVersion>4.0.0</modelVersion>

<groupId>com.example</groupId>

<artifactId>MyMavenApp</artifactId>

<version>1.0-SNAPSHOT</version>

<dependencies>

<!-- JUnit dependency for testing -->

<dependency>

<groupId>junit</groupId>

<artifactId>junit</artifactId>

<version>4.13.2</version>

<scope>test</scope>

</dependency>

</dependencies>

</project>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Running Maven Commands

#### 1\. Compile the Project:

mvn compile

#### 2\. Run Unit Tests:

mvn test

#### 3\. Build the Project (Creates a JAR):

mvn package

Output:

[INFO] Building jar: MyMavenApp/target/MyMavenApp-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar

#### 4\. Run the Application:

java -cp target/MyMavenApp-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar com.example.App

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Adding Dependencies

Dependencies are added to the <dependencies> section in pom.xml.

#### Example: Adding Gson Library:

<dependency>

<groupId>com.google.code.gson</groupId>

<artifactId>gson</artifactId>

<version>2.8.9</version>

</dependency>

Run mvn compile to download and include the dependency.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

## 2\. Using Gradle

### Setup

#### Install Gradle

1.  Download and install Gradle from the [](https://gradle.org/) [Gradle website](https://gradle.org/).

1.  Add Gradle's bin directory to your system's PATH.

#### Verify Installation

Run:

gradle -v

You should see the installed Gradle version.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Creating a Gradle Project

#### Command to Create a New Project:

gradle init --type java-application

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Project Structure

MyGradleApp/

├── build.gradle

├── settings.gradle

├── src/

├── main/

│ ├── java/

│ │ └── com/example/App.java

└── test/

└── java/

└── com/example/AppTest.java

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Editing build.gradle

The build.gradle file defines build configuration and dependencies.

#### Example build.gradle:

plugins {

id 'java'

id 'application'

}

group 'com.example'

version '1.0-SNAPSHOT'

repositories {

mavenCentral()

}

dependencies {

// JUnit for testing

testImplementation 'junit:junit:4.13.2'

}

application {

mainClassName = 'com.example.App'

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Running Gradle Commands

#### 1\. Compile the Project:

gradle build

#### 2\. Run Unit Tests:

gradle test

#### 3\. Run the Application:

gradle run

Output:

> Task :run

Hello, Gradle World!

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Adding Dependencies

Dependencies are added to the dependencies block in build.gradle.

#### Example: Adding Gson Library:

dependencies {

implementation 'com.google.code.gson:gson:2.8.9'

}

Run gradle build to download and include the dependency.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Comparison of Outputs

| Build Tool | Command | Output |
| Maven | mvn package | target/MyMavenApp-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar |
| Gradle | gradle build | build/libs/MyGradleApp-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices for Build Tools

1.  Use Dependency Management:

<bdi>○</bdi>Avoid manually downloading JAR files; let the build tool handle it.

1.  Version Control:

<bdi>○</bdi>Keep pom.xml or build.gradle under version control.

1.  Profiles/Tasks:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use Maven profiles or Gradle tasks to customize builds for different environments (e.g., dev, prod).

1.  Continuous Integration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Integrate with CI/CD pipelines like Jenkins, GitHub Actions, or GitLab.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Maven:

<bdi>○</bdi>XML-based, structured, and beginner-friendly.

<bdi>○</bdi>Ideal for conventional builds with standard layouts.

<bdi>●</bdi>Gradle:

<bdi>○</bdi>Groovy/Kotlin-based, flexible, and faster.

<bdi>○</bdi>Suited for complex, customized builds.

Both tools streamline project management, improve productivity, and are indispensable for modern Java development.

## Unit Testing with JUnit

JUnit is a popular testing framework for Java used to write and run repeatable tests. It ensures code correctness by validating individual components (units) of your application, such as methods and classes.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of JUnit

1.  Annotation-Based Testing:

<bdi>○</bdi>Simplifies test writing using annotations like @Test, @BeforeEach, and @AfterEach.

1.  Assertions:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides methods like assertEquals, assertTrue, and assertThrows for validating test outcomes.

1.  Test Suites:

<bdi>○</bdi>Group multiple test classes to run them together.

1.  Integration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Seamlessly integrates with build tools (e.g., Maven, Gradle) and IDEs.

1.  Test Lifecycle Management:

<bdi>○</bdi>Supports setup and teardown methods for initializing and cleaning resources.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### JUnit Versions

| Version | Description |
| JUnit 4 | Annotation-based testing with support for Java 5+. |
| JUnit 5 | Modular architecture and more powerful extensions. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Getting Started with JUnit 5

#### Dependency Configuration

Add the JUnit 5 dependency to your build tool.

##### Maven:

<dependency>

<groupId>org.junit.jupiter</groupId>

<artifactId>junit-jupiter</artifactId>

<version>5.9.2</version>

<scope>test</scope>

</dependency>

##### Gradle:

dependencies {

testImplementation 'org.junit.jupiter:junit-jupiter:5.9.2'

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Writing Your First Test

#### Code Example:

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;

import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

public class CalculatorTest {

@Test

void testAddition() {

Calculator calculator = new Calculator();

int result = calculator.add(2, 3);

assertEquals(5, result, "2 + 3 should equal 5");

}

}

class Calculator {

public int add(int a, int b) {

return a + b;

}

}

#### Output:

Test passed.

#### Explanation:

1.  @Test:

<bdi>○</bdi>Marks the method as a test case.

1.  assertEquals:

<bdi>○</bdi>Validates that the result matches the expected value.

1.  Test Result:

<bdi>○</bdi>The test passes because 2 + 3 = 5.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Testing Multiple Methods

#### Code Example:

import org.junit.jupiter.api.Test;

import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertEquals;

import static org.junit.jupiter.api.Assertions.assertThrows;

public class CalculatorTest {

@Test

void testAddition() {

Calculator calculator = new Calculator();

assertEquals(5, calculator.add(2, 3));

}

@Test

void testDivision() {

Calculator calculator = new Calculator();

assertEquals(2, calculator.divide(6, 3));

}

@Test

void testDivisionByZero() {

Calculator calculator = new Calculator();

assertThrows(ArithmeticException.class, () -> calculator.divide(6, 0));

}

}

class Calculator {

public int add(int a, int b) {

return a + b;

}

public int divide(int a, int b) {

return a / b; // Will throw ArithmeticException if b == 0

}

}

#### Output:

All tests passed.

#### Explanation:

1.  assertThrows:

<bdi>○</bdi>Validates that the correct exception is thrown for invalid inputs.

1.  Test Cases:

<bdi>○</bdi>Covers addition, division, and handling of exceptional cases.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Test Lifecycle Annotations

JUnit provides lifecycle annotations to control setup and teardown logic.

| Annotation | Purpose |
| @BeforeEach | Executes before each test method. |
| @AfterEach | Executes after each test method. |
| @BeforeAll | Executes once before all test methods in the class. |
| @AfterAll | Executes once after all test methods in the class. |

#### Code Example:

import org.junit.jupiter.api.*;

public class TestLifecycleExample {

@BeforeAll

static void setupAll() {

System.out.println("Executed once before all tests.");

}

@BeforeEach

void setupEach() {

System.out.println("Setup for a test.");

}

@Test

void testOne() {

System.out.println("Executing Test One.");

}

@Test

void testTwo() {

System.out.println("Executing Test Two.");

}

@AfterEach

void teardownEach() {

System.out.println("Teardown after a test.");

}

@AfterAll

static void teardownAll() {

System.out.println("Executed once after all tests.");

}

}

#### Output:

Executed once before all tests.

Setup for a test.

Executing Test One.

Teardown after a test.

Setup for a test.

Executing Test Two.

Teardown after a test.

Executed once after all tests.

#### Explanation:

1.  @BeforeAll and @AfterAll:

<bdi>○</bdi>Useful for expensive setup/teardown tasks (e.g., database connections).

1.  @BeforeEach and @AfterEach:

<bdi>○</bdi>Used for tasks like resetting variables or cleaning test data.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Assertions
