<bdi>○</bdi>Provides centralized configuration for database connections.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### How Connection Pooling Works

1.  Initialization:

<bdi>○</bdi>At application startup, a predefined number of connections are created and stored in the pool.

1.  Request Handling:

<bdi>○</bdi>When a request for a database connection is made, the pool provides an available connection.

1.  Recycling:

<bdi>○</bdi>After use, the connection is returned to the pool instead of being closed.

1.  Dynamic Management:

<bdi>○</bdi>The pool can grow or shrink based on demand, up to a configured limit.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Popular Connection Pooling Libraries

| Library | Description |
| HikariCP | High-performance connection pool. |
| Apache DBCP | Connection pool from the Apache Commons project. |
| C3P0 | Easy-to-use library with advanced features. |
| Tomcat JDBC | Connection pool used in Apache Tomcat server. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Connection Pooling with HikariCP

HikariCP is one of the most popular and high-performance connection pooling libraries for Java applications.

#### Setup

Add the following Maven dependencies:

<dependency>

<groupId>com.zaxxer</groupId>

<artifactId>HikariCP</artifactId>

<version>5.0.1</version>

</dependency>

<dependency>

<groupId>mysql</groupId>

<artifactId>mysql-connector-java</artifactId>

<version>8.0.33</version>

</dependency>

#### Code Example:

import com.zaxxer.hikari.HikariConfig;

import com.zaxxer.hikari.HikariDataSource;

import java.sql.Connection;

import java.sql.PreparedStatement;

import java.sql.ResultSet;

public class HikariCPExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// HikariCP configuration

HikariConfig config = new HikariConfig();

config.setJdbcUrl("jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/testdb");

config.setUsername("root");

config.setPassword("password");

config.setMaximumPoolSize(10); // Maximum connections in the pool

config.setMinimumIdle(2); // Minimum idle connections in the pool

config.setIdleTimeout(30000); // Idle timeout in milliseconds

config.setMaxLifetime(1800000); // Maximum lifetime of a connection

// Initialize the connection pool

HikariDataSource dataSource = new HikariDataSource(config);

try (Connection conn = dataSource.getConnection()) {

String sql = "SELECT * FROM employees";

PreparedStatement pstmt = conn.prepareStatement(sql);

ResultSet rs = pstmt.executeQuery();

while (rs.next()) {

System.out.println("ID: " + rs.getInt("id"));

System.out.println("Name: " + rs.getString("name"));

System.out.println("Position: " + rs.getString("position"));

System.out.println("---------------------------");

}

} catch (Exception e) {

e.printStackTrace();

} finally {

// Close the data source

dataSource.close();

}

}

}

#### Output:

ID: 1

Name: Alice

Position: Manager

---------------------------

ID: 2

Name: Bob

Position: Engineer

---------------------------

#### Explanation:

1.  HikariConfig:

<bdi>○</bdi>Configures the database URL, credentials, and pool properties.

1.  Connection Pool:

<bdi>○</bdi>HikariDataSource manages the connections.

1.  Dynamic Management:

<bdi>○</bdi>Reuses or dynamically creates connections based on the load.

1.  Performance:

<bdi>○</bdi>HikariCP ensures fast and reliable connection management.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Connection Pooling with Apache DBCP

Apache DBCP (Database Connection Pooling) is a stable and widely used connection pooling library.

#### Setup

Add the following Maven dependencies:

<dependency>

<groupId>org.apache.commons</groupId>

<artifactId>commons-dbcp2</artifactId>

<version>2.10.0</version>

</dependency>

<dependency>

<groupId>mysql</groupId>

<artifactId>mysql-connector-java</artifactId>

<version>8.0.33</version>

</dependency>

#### Code Example:

import org.apache.commons.dbcp2.BasicDataSource;

import java.sql.Connection;

import java.sql.PreparedStatement;

import java.sql.ResultSet;

public class DBCPExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Configure the connection pool

BasicDataSource dataSource = new BasicDataSource();

dataSource.setUrl("jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/testdb");

dataSource.setUsername("root");

dataSource.setPassword("password");

dataSource.setMinIdle(2);

dataSource.setMaxIdle(5);

dataSource.setMaxOpenPreparedStatements(100);

try (Connection conn = dataSource.getConnection()) {

String sql = "SELECT * FROM employees";

PreparedStatement pstmt = conn.prepareStatement(sql);

ResultSet rs = pstmt.executeQuery();

while (rs.next()) {

System.out.println("ID: " + rs.getInt("id"));

System.out.println("Name: " + rs.getString("name"));

System.out.println("Position: " + rs.getString("position"));

System.out.println("---------------------------");

}

} catch (Exception e) {

e.printStackTrace();

} finally {

// Close the data source

try {

dataSource.close();

} catch (Exception e) {

e.printStackTrace();

}

}

}

}

#### Output:

ID: 1

Name: Alice

Position: Manager

---------------------------

ID: 2

Name: Bob

Position: Engineer

---------------------------

#### Explanation:

1.  BasicDataSource:

<bdi>○</bdi>Manages the connection pool for database interactions.

1.  Configuration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Set properties like minimum/maximum idle connections and maximum prepared statements.

1.  Efficiency:

<bdi>○</bdi>Apache DBCP manages connections effectively for multiple requests.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Connection Pooling with Tomcat JDBC Pool

Tomcat JDBC Pool is a lightweight connection pooling library that is part of the Tomcat project.

#### Setup

Add the following Maven dependencies:

<dependency>

<groupId>org.apache.tomcat</groupId>

<artifactId>tomcat-jdbc</artifactId>

<version>10.1.0</version>

</dependency>

<dependency>

<groupId>mysql</groupId>

<artifactId>mysql-connector-java</artifactId>

<version>8.0.33</version>

</dependency>

#### Code Example:

import org.apache.tomcat.jdbc.pool.DataSource;

import org.apache.tomcat.jdbc.pool.PoolProperties;

import java.sql.Connection;

import java.sql.PreparedStatement;

import java.sql.ResultSet;

public class TomcatJDBCExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

PoolProperties poolProperties = new PoolProperties();

poolProperties.setUrl("jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/testdb");

poolProperties.setUsername("root");

poolProperties.setPassword("password");

poolProperties.setInitialSize(5);

poolProperties.setMaxActive(10);

poolProperties.setMinIdle(2);

DataSource dataSource = new DataSource();

dataSource.setPoolProperties(poolProperties);

try (Connection conn = dataSource.getConnection()) {

String sql = "SELECT * FROM employees";

PreparedStatement pstmt = conn.prepareStatement(sql);

ResultSet rs = pstmt.executeQuery();

while (rs.next()) {

System.out.println("ID: " + rs.getInt("id"));

System.out.println("Name: " + rs.getString("name"));

System.out.println("Position: " + rs.getString("position"));

System.out.println("---------------------------");

}

} catch (Exception e) {

e.printStackTrace();

} finally {

dataSource.close();

}

}

}

#### Output:

ID: 1

Name: Alice

Position: Manager

---------------------------

ID: 2

Name: Bob

Position: Engineer

---------------------------

#### Explanation:

1.  Tomcat JDBC Pool:

<bdi>○</bdi>Lightweight and efficient pooling mechanism.

1.  PoolProperties:

<bdi>○</bdi>Configures properties like maximum connections and idle connections.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices for Connection Pooling

1.  Set Appropriate Limits:

<bdi>○</bdi>Avoid setting excessively high maxPoolSize to prevent resource exhaustion.

1.  Monitor Pool Performance:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use monitoring tools to observe connection usage and pool health.

1.  Secure Credentials:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use secure methods for storing and retrieving database credentials.

1.  Close Connections Properly:

<bdi>○</bdi>Always return connections to the pool using try-with-resources.

1.  Optimize Pool Configuration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Balance between performance and resource usage by tuning parameters like idleTimeout and maxLifetime.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Connection Pooling:

<bdi>○</bdi>Reuses connections to reduce latency and resource consumption.

<bdi>●</bdi>Popular Libraries:

<bdi>○</bdi>HikariCP, Apache DBCP, and Tomcat JDBC Pool.

<bdi>●</bdi>Configuration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Set properties for maximum connections, idle timeout, and lifetime.

<bdi>●</bdi>Best Practices:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensure secure, efficient, and scalable database interactions.

Connection pooling is an essential practice for optimizing database operations, particularly in high-concurrency environments. Properly configured pooling enhances performance, scalability, and resource management.

## Introduction to Hibernate

Hibernate is a powerful, open-source Object-Relational Mapping (ORM) framework for Java applications. It simplifies database interactions by mapping Java objects to database tables, enabling developers to work with databases using high-level object-oriented principles rather than SQL.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Hibernate

1.  Object-Relational Mapping (ORM):

<bdi>○</bdi>Maps Java classes to database tables and Java objects to rows.

1.  Database Independence:

<bdi>○</bdi>Supports multiple databases (e.g., MySQL, PostgreSQL, Oracle) without requiring code changes.

1.  HQL (Hibernate Query Language):

<bdi>○</bdi>A query language similar to SQL but operates on Java objects instead of database tables.

1.  Lazy Loading:

<bdi>○</bdi>Delays loading associated data until it is accessed, optimizing performance.

1.  Caching:

<bdi>○</bdi>Built-in caching mechanisms to improve query performance.

1.  Automatic Table Creation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Can generate database schema based on mappings.

1.  Transaction Management:

<bdi>○</bdi>Integrates with JTA and other transaction APIs for robust transaction handling.

1.  Annotations and XML Mapping:

<bdi>○</bdi>Supports configuration via annotations or XML files.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Why Use Hibernate?

1.  Simplified Development:

<bdi>○</bdi>Eliminates boilerplate code for JDBC and manual SQL handling.

1.  Portability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Easily switch between databases without changing application logic.

1.  Productivity:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides higher-level APIs for database operations, reducing development time.

1.  Maintainability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Centralized mapping and configuration simplify schema management.

1.  Performance:

<bdi>○</bdi>Features like caching and batch processing enhance application efficiency.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Core Components of Hibernate

| Component | Description |
| Configuration | Loads Hibernate settings and database connection properties. |
| SessionFactory | Factory for creating Session objects. Thread-safe and immutable. |
| Session | Represents a connection to the database for CRUD operations. |
| Transaction | Manages database transactions. |
| Query | Executes HQL or native SQL queries. |
| Criteria API | Creates dynamic queries programmatically. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Architecture of Hibernate

1.  Java Application:

<bdi>○</bdi>Contains the business logic and interacts with Hibernate.

1.  Hibernate Framework:

<bdi>○</bdi>Maps Java objects to database tables using XML or annotations.

1.  Database:

<bdi>○</bdi>The relational database where data is stored and retrieved.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Hibernate Workflow

1.  Configuration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Set up Hibernate configuration file or annotations to map Java classes to database tables.

1.  SessionFactory:

<bdi>○</bdi>Build a SessionFactory from the configuration.

1.  Session:

<bdi>○</bdi>Obtain a Session object to interact with the database.

1.  Transaction:

<bdi>○</bdi>Begin a transaction to ensure atomic operations.

1.  Operations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Perform CRUD operations using the session.

1.  Commit/Close:

<bdi>○</bdi>Commit the transaction and close the session to release resources.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Setting Up Hibernate

#### 1\. Add Dependencies

For Maven:

<dependency>

<groupId>org.hibernate</groupId>

<artifactId>hibernate-core</artifactId>

<version>5.6.16.Final</version>

</dependency>

<dependency>

<groupId>mysql</groupId>

<artifactId>mysql-connector-java</artifactId>

<version>8.0.33</version>

</dependency>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### 2\. Create Hibernate Configuration File

hibernate.cfg.xml:

<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>

<!DOCTYPE hibernate-configuration PUBLIC

"-//Hibernate/Hibernate Configuration DTD 3.0//EN"

"http://hibernate.sourceforge.net/hibernate-configuration-3.0.dtd">

<hibernate-configuration>

<session-factory>

<!-- Database Connection Settings -->

<property name="hibernate.connection.driver_class">com.mysql.cj.jdbc.Driver</property>

<property name="hibernate.connection.url">jdbc:mysql://localhost:3306/testdb</property>

<property name="hibernate.connection.username">root</property>

<property name="hibernate.connection.password">password</property>

<!-- Hibernate Properties -->

<property name="hibernate.dialect">org.hibernate.dialect.MySQLDialect</property>

<property name="hibernate.show_sql">true</property>

<property name="hibernate.hbm2ddl.auto">update</property>

<!-- Mapping Class -->

<mapping class="com.example.Employee"/>

</session-factory>

</hibernate-configuration>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### 3\. Create a Java Class

Employee.java:

package com.example;

import javax.persistence.*;

@Entity

@Table(name = "employees")

public class Employee {

@Id

@GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.IDENTITY)

private int id;

@Column(name = "name")

private String name;

@Column(name = "position")

private String position;

@Column(name = "salary")

private double salary;

// Getters and Setters

public int getId() {

return id;

}

public void setId(int id) {

this.id = id;

}

public String getName() {

return name;

}

public void setName(String name) {

this.name = name;

}

public String getPosition() {

return position;

}

public void setPosition(String position) {

this.position = position;

}

public double getSalary() {

return salary;

}

public void setSalary(double salary) {

this.salary = salary;

}

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### 4\. Perform CRUD Operations

Main.java:

package com.example;

import org.hibernate.Session;

import org.hibernate.SessionFactory;

import org.hibernate.Transaction;

import org.hibernate.cfg.Configuration;

public class Main {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Load Hibernate Configuration

Configuration config = new Configuration().configure();

SessionFactory sessionFactory = config.buildSessionFactory();

// Open Session

Session session = sessionFactory.openSession();

Transaction transaction = session.beginTransaction();

// Create an Employee

Employee employee = new Employee();

employee.setName("Alice");

employee.setPosition("Manager");

employee.setSalary(75000);

// Save the Employee

session.save(employee);

// Commit Transaction

transaction.commit();

System.out.println("Employee saved successfully!");

// Close the session and session factory

session.close();

sessionFactory.close();

}

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Output

When the application runs:

1.  A table named employees is created (if it doesn't exist).

A new record is inserted with the following values: ID: Auto-generated

Name: Alice

Position: Manager

Salary: 75000

1.  Hibernate logs SQL queries executed against the database.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Explanation

1.  Configuration:

<bdi>○</bdi>hibernate.cfg.xml contains database connection and Hibernate settings.

1.  Annotations:

<bdi>○</bdi>@Entity, @Table, and @Column map the Java class and fields to the database table and columns.

1.  Session:

<bdi>○</bdi>The Session object manages interactions with the database.

1.  Transaction:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ensures atomicity and consistency during operations.

1.  CRUD Operation:

<bdi>○</bdi>session.save() inserts the data into the database.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Advantages of Hibernate

1.  Ease of Use:

<bdi>○</bdi>Simplifies database operations by automating SQL generation.

1.  Portability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Works with various relational databases with minimal changes.

1.  High Performance:

<bdi>○</bdi>Optimizations like caching and lazy loading improve efficiency.

1.  Comprehensive API:

<bdi>○</bdi>Supports advanced database features like batch processing and pagination.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Disadvantages of Hibernate

1.  Learning Curve:

<bdi>○</bdi>Steeper learning curve compared to plain JDBC.

1.  Overhead:

<bdi>○</bdi>May introduce overhead for simple applications with minimal database interactions.

1.  Complexity:

<bdi>○</bdi>Configuration and debugging can be complex for large-scale applications.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Hibernate:

<bdi>○</bdi>A powerful ORM framework for Java that simplifies database interactions.

<bdi>●</bdi>Features:

<bdi>○</bdi>ORM, HQL, caching, and automatic table creation.

<bdi>●</bdi>Setup:

<bdi>○</bdi>Requires a configuration file, annotated Java classes, and session management.

<bdi>●</bdi>Use Case:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ideal for enterprise-level applications requiring frequent database interactions.

Hibernate is a must-learn for Java developers building database-driven applications, offering a robust alternative to manual JDBC-based operations.

# GUI Development

## Introduction to Swing

Swing is a part of Java's standard library used to build graphical user interfaces (GUIs) for desktop applications. It is a lightweight, platform-independent, and extensible framework that builds on the older Abstract Window Toolkit (AWT) by providing more sophisticated and flexible components.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Swing

1.  Rich Set of Components:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides components like buttons, tables, trees, and menus that can be customized.

1.  Lightweight:

<bdi>○</bdi>Swing components are not tied to native operating system (OS) components, making them portable.

1.  MVC Architecture:

<bdi>○</bdi>Follows the Model-View-Controller architecture for separating logic and presentation.

1.  Pluggable Look and Feel:

<bdi>○</bdi>Swing allows the GUI appearance to be changed dynamically (e.g., Windows, Metal, Nimbus).

1.  Event-Driven Programming:

<bdi>○</bdi>Uses listeners to handle user interactions like button clicks or mouse movements.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Swing vs. AWT

| Feature | Swing | AWT |
| Architecture | Lightweight (does not rely on OS components). | Heavyweight (uses native OS components). |
| Flexibility | Highly customizable components. | Limited flexibility. |
| Look and Feel | Pluggable Look and Feel. | Depends on OS appearance. |
| Components | Richer and more advanced (e.g., JTable, JTree). | Basic components. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Core Swing Components

| Component | Description |
| JFrame | Main container window. |
| JPanel | General-purpose container for organizing components. |
| JLabel | Displays text or an image. |
| JButton | Represents a clickable button. |
| JTextField | Single-line text input field. |
| JTextArea | Multi-line text area. |
| JComboBox | Drop-down list. |
| JTable | Displays tabular data. |
| JScrollPane | Adds scrollable functionality to components. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Basic Structure of a Swing Application

1.  Create a JFrame:

<bdi>○</bdi>The main window where other components are added.

1.  Add Components:

<bdi>○</bdi>Add components like buttons, labels, or text fields to the frame or panels.

1.  Handle Events:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use listeners to handle user interactions.

1.  Display the Window:

<bdi>○</bdi>Set the frame's visibility to true.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Creating a Simple Swing Application

#### Code Example:

import javax.swing.*;

public class SimpleSwingApp {

public static void main(String[] args) {

// Create a JFrame (Main Window)

JFrame frame = new JFrame("Simple Swing Application");

frame.setSize(400, 300);

frame.setDefaultCloseOperation(JFrame.EXIT_ON_CLOSE);

// Create a JLabel and add it to the frame

JLabel label = new JLabel("Welcome to Swing!", SwingConstants.CENTER);

frame.add(label);

// Make the frame visible

frame.setVisible(true);

}

}

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>A window titled "Simple Swing Application" opens with the message "Welcome to Swing!" centered inside it.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation:

1.  JFrame:

<bdi>○</bdi>The main application window.

1.  JLabel:

<bdi>○</bdi>Displays static text inside the frame.

1.  SwingConstants.CENTER:

<bdi>○</bdi>Aligns the label text to the center of the frame.

1.  setVisible(true):

<bdi>○</bdi>Makes the window visible.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Adding Components and Layout

#### Code Example:

import javax.swing.*;

import java.awt.*;

public class ComponentExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

JFrame frame = new JFrame("Swing Components");

frame.setSize(400, 300);

frame.setDefaultCloseOperation(JFrame.EXIT_ON_CLOSE);

frame.setLayout(new FlowLayout());

// Create Components

JLabel label = new JLabel("Enter your name:");

JTextField textField = new JTextField(20);

JButton button = new JButton("Submit");

// Add Components to the Frame

frame.add(label);

frame.add(textField);

frame.add(button);

// Display the Frame

frame.setVisible(true);

}

}

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>A window with a label "Enter your name:", a text field, and a button "Submit" arranged horizontally.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation:

1.  Layout Manager:

<bdi>○</bdi>FlowLayout arranges components horizontally, wrapping to the next line if needed.

1.  Components:

<bdi>○</bdi>JTextField: A single-line text input.

<bdi>○</bdi>JButton: A clickable button.

1.  Frame Setup:

<bdi>○</bdi>The components are added to the frame in the order they are declared.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Event Handling in Swing

#### Code Example:

import javax.swing.*;

import java.awt.event.ActionEvent;

import java.awt.event.ActionListener;

public class EventHandlingExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

JFrame frame = new JFrame("Event Handling");

frame.setSize(400, 300);

frame.setDefaultCloseOperation(JFrame.EXIT_ON_CLOSE);

JLabel label = new JLabel("Click the button:", SwingConstants.CENTER);

JButton button = new JButton("Click Me");

// Add ActionListener to the Button

button.addActionListener(new ActionListener() {

@Override

public void actionPerformed(ActionEvent e) {

label.setText("Button Clicked!");

}

});

// Add Components to the Frame

frame.setLayout(new BorderLayout());

frame.add(label, BorderLayout.CENTER);

frame.add(button, BorderLayout.SOUTH);

// Display the Frame

frame.setVisible(true);

}

}

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>A window with a label "Click the button:".

<bdi>●</bdi>Clicking the button changes the label to "Button Clicked!".

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation:

1.  Action Listener:

<bdi>○</bdi>Captures the button click event and executes the specified action.

1.  Event Handling:

<bdi>○</bdi>ActionEvent contains details about the button click.

<bdi>○</bdi>setText updates the label text dynamically.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Using Layout Managers

Swing provides various layout managers for arranging components:

| Layout Manager | Description |
| FlowLayout | Arranges components horizontally. |
| BorderLayout | Divides the container into five regions (NORTH, SOUTH, EAST, WEST, CENTER). |
| GridLayout | Arranges components in a grid of rows and columns. |
| BoxLayout | Arranges components either vertically or horizontally. |

#### Code Example with GridLayout:

import javax.swing.*;

import java.awt.*;

public class GridLayoutExample {

public static void main(String[] args) {

JFrame frame = new JFrame("GridLayout Example");

frame.setSize(400, 300);

frame.setDefaultCloseOperation(JFrame.EXIT_ON_CLOSE);

frame.setLayout(new GridLayout(2, 2)); // 2 rows, 2 columns

frame.add(new JButton("Button 1"));

frame.add(new JButton("Button 2"));

frame.add(new JButton("Button 3"));

frame.add(new JButton("Button 4"));

frame.setVisible(true);

}

}

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>A 2x2 grid with four buttons arranged in rows and columns.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Creating a Real-World Application

#### Code Example: Login Form

import javax.swing.*;

import java.awt.*;

import java.awt.event.ActionEvent;

import java.awt.event.ActionListener;

public class LoginForm {

public static void main(String[] args) {

JFrame frame = new JFrame("Login Form");

frame.setSize(300, 200);

frame.setDefaultCloseOperation(JFrame.EXIT_ON_CLOSE);

frame.setLayout(new GridLayout(3, 2));

// Create Components

JLabel userLabel = new JLabel("Username:");

JTextField userField = new JTextField();

JLabel passLabel = new JLabel("Password:");

JPasswordField passField = new JPasswordField();

JButton loginButton = new JButton("Login");

JLabel messageLabel = new JLabel("", SwingConstants.CENTER);

// Add ActionListener to Login Button

loginButton.addActionListener(new ActionListener() {

@Override

public void actionPerformed(ActionEvent e) {

String username = userField.getText();

String password = new String(passField.getPassword());

if (username.equals("admin") && password.equals("1234")) {

messageLabel.setText("Login Successful!");

} else {

messageLabel.setText("Invalid Credentials.");

}

}

});

// Add Components to the Frame

frame.add(userLabel);

frame.add(userField);

frame.add(passLabel);

frame.add(passField);

frame.add(loginButton);

frame.add(messageLabel);

frame.setVisible(true);

}

}

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>A login form with username, password fields, and a login button.

<bdi>●</bdi>Displays "Login Successful!" or "Invalid Credentials." based on input.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

1.  Swing Features:

<bdi>○</bdi>Lightweight, platform-independent, and rich GUI components.

1.  Core Components:

<bdi>○</bdi>Includes JFrame, JButton, JTextField, and more.

1.  Event Handling:

<bdi>○</bdi>Uses listeners like ActionListener for interaction.

1.  Layout Managers:

<bdi>○</bdi>Arrange components dynamically within a frame.

1.  Real-World Applications:

<bdi>○</bdi>Build forms, dashboards, and other desktop applications.

Swing is a versatile and widely-used library for developing desktop GUI applications in Java. It simplifies the creation of user-friendly and interactive interfaces.

## Introduction to JavaFX

JavaFX is a modern, powerful framework for building rich desktop and web applications in Java. It is the successor to Swing and provides a more sophisticated set of tools for creating visually appealing user interfaces with support for advanced graphics, animations, and media.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of JavaFX

1.  Rich UI Components:

<bdi>○</bdi>Includes advanced components like charts, tables, and trees.

1.  CSS Styling:

<bdi>○</bdi>Customize the appearance of applications using CSS.

1.  FXML:

<bdi>○</bdi>A declarative XML-based language to design UI layouts.

1.  Binding and Properties:

<bdi>○</bdi>Simplifies data synchronization between UI components and application logic.

1.  Multimedia Support:

<bdi>○</bdi>Built-in support for audio, video, and images.

1.  Animations and Effects:

<bdi>○</bdi>Supports transitions, animations, and graphical effects.

1.  Cross-Platform:

<bdi>○</bdi>Runs on Windows, macOS, and Linux.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### JavaFX Architecture

JavaFX follows a Stage-Scene-Node hierarchy:

<bdi>●</bdi>Stage:

<bdi>○</bdi>The top-level container (window).

<bdi>●</bdi>Scene:

<bdi>○</bdi>The container for all visual content (e.g., layout, controls, shapes).

<bdi>●</bdi>Nodes:

<bdi>○</bdi>Visual elements like buttons, labels, and layouts.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Setting Up JavaFX

Add Dependencies for Maven: Add the following dependencies to your pom.xml: <dependency>

<groupId>org.openjfx</groupId>

<artifactId>javafx-controls</artifactId>

<version>20</version>

</dependency>

<dependency>

<groupId>org.openjfx</groupId>

<artifactId>javafx-fxml</artifactId>

<version>20</version>

</dependency>

Configure VM Options: Add the following VM arguments when running JavaFX applications: --module-path <path-to-javafx-sdk/lib> --add-modules javafx.controls,javafx.fxml

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Basic Structure of a JavaFX Application

A JavaFX application extends the Application class and overrides the start method, where the UI is built and displayed.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Creating a Simple JavaFX Application

#### Code Example:

import javafx.application.Application;

import javafx.scene.Scene;

import javafx.scene.control.Label;

import javafx.stage.Stage;

public class SimpleJavaFXApp extends Application {

@Override

public void start(Stage stage) {

// Create a Label

Label label = new Label("Welcome to JavaFX!");

// Create a Scene

Scene scene = new Scene(label, 400, 300);

// Set the Stage

stage.setTitle("Simple JavaFX Application");

stage.setScene(scene);

stage.show();

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

launch();

}

}

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>A window titled "Simple JavaFX Application" with the text "Welcome to JavaFX!" displayed.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation:

1.  Application Class:

<bdi>○</bdi>Base class for all JavaFX applications.

1.  Stage:

<bdi>○</bdi>The main window for the application.

1.  Scene:

<bdi>○</bdi>Contains all UI components (e.g., Label).

1.  launch:

<bdi>○</bdi>Starts the JavaFX application.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Adding Multiple Components

#### Code Example:

import javafx.application.Application;

import javafx.scene.Scene;

import javafx.scene.control.Button;

import javafx.scene.control.Label;

import javafx.scene.control.TextField;

import javafx.scene.layout.VBox;

import javafx.stage.Stage;

public class MultiComponentApp extends Application {

@Override

public void start(Stage stage) {

// Create Components

Label label = new Label("Enter your name:");

TextField textField = new TextField();

Button button = new Button("Submit");

// Event Handling

button.setOnAction(e -> label.setText("Hello, " + textField.getText() + "!"));

// Layout

VBox layout = new VBox(10, label, textField, button);

// Create a Scene

Scene scene = new Scene(layout, 400, 200);

// Set the Stage

stage.setTitle("Multi-Component JavaFX App");

stage.setScene(scene);

stage.show();

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

launch();

}

}

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>A window with a label, a text field, and a button.

<bdi>●</bdi>Entering a name and clicking the button updates the label to "Hello, [Name]!".

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation:

1.  Event Handling:

<bdi>○</bdi>The setOnAction method listens for button clicks.

1.  Layouts:

<bdi>○</bdi>VBox arranges components vertically with a spacing of 10 pixels.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Styling with CSS

#### Code Example:

import javafx.application.Application;

import javafx.scene.Scene;

import javafx.scene.control.Button;

import javafx.scene.layout.StackPane;

import javafx.stage.Stage;

public class CSSExample extends Application {

@Override

public void start(Stage stage) {

// Create a Button

Button button = new Button("Click Me");

button.setId("myButton");

// Layout

StackPane layout = new StackPane(button);

// Create a Scene

Scene scene = new Scene(layout, 300, 200);

// Apply CSS

scene.getStylesheets().add("styles.css");

// Set the Stage

stage.setTitle("JavaFX with CSS");

stage.setScene(scene);

stage.show();

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

launch();

}

}

styles.css:

#myButton {

-fx-font-size: 16px;

-fx-background-color: #4CAF50;

-fx-text-fill: white;

-fx-padding: 10px;

-fx-border-radius: 5px;

-fx-background-radius: 5px;

}

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>A styled button with green background, white text, and rounded corners.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation:

1.  CSS Styling:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use external stylesheets to style components.

1.  Selectors:

<bdi>○</bdi>Components can be targeted by their id or class names.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Creating a Login Form with FXML

FXML is an XML-based language for defining the UI structure.

#### FXML File (login.fxml):

<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>

<?import javafx.scene.control.*?>

<?import javafx.scene.layout.*?>

<VBox xmlns:fx="http://javafx.com/fxml" spacing="10" alignment="CENTER">

<Label text="Username:" />

<TextField fx:id="usernameField" />

<Label text="Password:" />

<PasswordField fx:id="passwordField" />

<Button text="Login" onAction="#handleLogin" />

<Label fx:id="messageLabel" />

</VBox>

#### Code Example:

import javafx.application.Application;

import javafx.fxml.FXML;

import javafx.fxml.FXMLLoader;

import javafx.scene.Parent;

import javafx.scene.Scene;

import javafx.scene.control.Label;

import javafx.scene.control.PasswordField;

import javafx.scene.control.TextField;

import javafx.stage.Stage;

public class FXMLLoginApp extends Application {

@FXML

private TextField usernameField;

@FXML

private PasswordField passwordField;

@FXML

private Label messageLabel;

@Override

public void start(Stage stage) throws Exception {

Parent root = FXMLLoader.load(getClass().getResource("login.fxml"));

Scene scene = new Scene(root);

stage.setTitle("FXML Login Form");

stage.setScene(scene);

stage.show();

}

@FXML

private void handleLogin() {

String username = usernameField.getText();

String password = passwordField.getText();

if (username.equals("admin") && password.equals("1234")) {

messageLabel.setText("Login Successful!");

} else {

messageLabel.setText("Invalid Credentials.");

}

}

public static void main(String[] args) {

launch();

}

}

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>A login form with fields for username and password.

<bdi>●</bdi>Displays "Login Successful!" or "Invalid Credentials." based on input.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Explanation:

1.  FXML:

<bdi>○</bdi>Defines the UI structure in XML.

1.  Controller:

<bdi>○</bdi>Links UI components to Java logic using annotations like @FXML.

1.  Event Binding:

<bdi>○</bdi>The onAction attribute in FXML binds button clicks to Java methods.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>JavaFX Features:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides advanced GUI components, CSS styling, and FXML for declarative UI design.

<bdi>●</bdi>Core Components:

<bdi>○</bdi>Includes Stage, Scene, and various UI controls like Button and Label.

<bdi>●</bdi>Styling and FXML:

<bdi>○</bdi>CSS allows for modern UI designs, while FXML separates design and logic.

<bdi>●</bdi>Event Handling:

<bdi>○</bdi>Simplifies user interaction with event listeners.

JavaFX is a modern, versatile framework for building robust and visually appealing desktop applications in Java. Its powerful features make it an excellent choice for GUI development.

# Web Development with Java

## Introduction to Java Servlets

Java Servlets are server-side Java programs that handle client requests and generate dynamic web content. They act as the middleware between client requests (from browsers or other HTTP clients) and server-side resources (like databases).

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of Servlets

1.  Server-Side Execution:

<bdi>○</bdi>Executes on the server and generates dynamic web pages.

1.  Protocol Handling:

<bdi>○</bdi>Handles HTTP protocol effectively (e.g., GET, POST methods).

1.  Portability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Platform-independent, runs on any Java-compatible server.

1.  Session Management:

<bdi>○</bdi>Built-in support for managing user sessions.

1.  Integration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Can interact with databases, other APIs, and enterprise components like EJB.

1.  Efficient and Scalable:

<bdi>○</bdi>Supports multithreading and maintains a single instance for handling requests.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Servlet Lifecycle

1.  Loading and Instantiation:

<bdi>○</bdi>The servlet is loaded and instantiated by the servlet container.

1.  Initialization (init method):

<bdi>○</bdi>Called once when the servlet is initialized.

1.  Request Handling (service method):

<bdi>○</bdi>Called for each client request. Delegates work to doGet, doPost, etc., based on HTTP method.

1.  Destruction (destroy method):

<bdi>○</bdi>Called once when the servlet is taken out of service.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Setup for Servlet Development

1.  Install a Servlet Container:

<bdi>○</bdi>Apache Tomcat is a commonly used servlet container.

Add Dependencies: For Maven, include the servlet API dependency: <dependency>

<groupId>javax.servlet</groupId>

<artifactId>javax.servlet-api</artifactId>

<version>4.0.1</version>

<scope>provided</scope>

</dependency>

1.  Configure Deployment Descriptor (web.xml):

<bdi>○</bdi>Maps the servlet to a specific URL pattern.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Basic Structure of a Servlet

A servlet class extends HttpServlet and overrides methods like doGet or doPost.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. A Simple Servlet

#### Code Example:

import java.io.IOException;

import javax.servlet.ServletException;

import javax.servlet.annotation.WebServlet;

import javax.servlet.http.HttpServlet;

import javax.servlet.http.HttpServletRequest;

import javax.servlet.http.HttpServletResponse;

@WebServlet("/hello")

public class HelloServlet extends HttpServlet {

@Override

protected void doGet(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)

throws ServletException, IOException {

response.setContentType("text/html");

response.getWriter().println("<h1>Welcome to Java Servlets!</h1>");

}

}

#### Output:

When you visit http://localhost:8080/your-app/hello, the browser displays: Welcome to Java Servlets!

#### Explanation:

1.  @WebServlet Annotation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Maps the servlet to the URL /hello.

1.  doGet Method:

<bdi>○</bdi>Handles HTTP GET requests.

1.  Response Handling:

<bdi>○</bdi>Sets the content type to text/html and writes HTML output to the response.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Handling Form Data (POST Method)

#### Code Example:

import java.io.IOException;

import javax.servlet.ServletException;

import javax.servlet.annotation.WebServlet;

import javax.servlet.http.HttpServlet;

import javax.servlet.http.HttpServletRequest;

import javax.servlet.http.HttpServletResponse;

@WebServlet("/submit")

public class SubmitServlet extends HttpServlet {

@Override

protected void doPost(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)

throws ServletException, IOException {

String name = request.getParameter("name");

response.setContentType("text/html");

response.getWriter().println("<h1>Hello, " + name + "!</h1>");

}

}

HTML Form:

<form action="submit" method="POST">

<label for="name">Enter your name:</label>

<input type="text" id="name" name="name">

<button type="submit">Submit</button>

</form>

#### Output:

1.  The user submits their name via the form.

The servlet responds with: Hello, [Name]!

#### Explanation:

1.  doPost Method:

<bdi>○</bdi>Handles HTTP POST requests.

1.  getParameter:

<bdi>○</bdi>Retrieves form data sent in the request.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Servlet with Initialization Parameters

#### Code Example:

import java.io.IOException;

import javax.servlet.ServletConfig;

import javax.servlet.ServletException;

import javax.servlet.annotation.WebServlet;

import javax.servlet.http.HttpServlet;

import javax.servlet.http.HttpServletRequest;

import javax.servlet.http.HttpServletResponse;

@WebServlet("/config")

public class ConfigServlet extends HttpServlet {

private String appName;

@Override

public void init(ServletConfig config) throws ServletException {

super.init(config);

appName = config.getInitParameter("AppName");

}

@Override

protected void doGet(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)

throws ServletException, IOException {

response.setContentType("text/html");

response.getWriter().println("<h1>Welcome to " + appName + "!</h1>");

}

}

web.xml Configuration:

<servlet>

<servlet-name>ConfigServlet</servlet-name>

<servlet-class>ConfigServlet</servlet-class>

<init-param>

<param-name>AppName</param-name>

<param-value>My Awesome App</param-value>

</init-param>

</servlet>

<servlet-mapping>

<servlet-name>ConfigServlet</servlet-name>

<url-pattern>/config</url-pattern>

</servlet-mapping>

#### Output:

Visiting http://localhost:8080/your-app/config displays: Welcome to My Awesome App!

#### Explanation:

1.  Initialization Parameters:

<bdi>○</bdi>init retrieves configuration parameters from web.xml.

1.  Reusability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Parameters can be customized without changing the servlet code.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Managing Sessions in Servlets

#### Code Example:

import java.io.IOException;

import javax.servlet.ServletException;

import javax.servlet.annotation.WebServlet;

import javax.servlet.http.HttpServlet;

import javax.servlet.http.HttpServletRequest;

import javax.servlet.http.HttpServletResponse;

import javax.servlet.http.HttpSession;

@WebServlet("/session")

public class SessionServlet extends HttpServlet {

@Override

protected void doGet(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)

throws ServletException, IOException {

HttpSession session = request.getSession();

Integer count = (Integer) session.getAttribute("count");

if (count == null) {

count = 1;

} else {

count++;

}

session.setAttribute("count", count);

response.setContentType("text/html");

response.getWriter().println("<h1>Page Visits: " + count + "</h1>");

}

}

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>Each time the user refreshes the page, the number of visits increases.

#### Explanation:

1.  HttpSession:

<bdi>○</bdi>Manages user sessions across multiple requests.

1.  Attributes:

<bdi>○</bdi>getAttribute retrieves session data.

<bdi>○</bdi>setAttribute stores session data.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Redirects and Forwarding

#### Code Example (Redirect):

@WebServlet("/redirect")

public class RedirectServlet extends HttpServlet {

@Override

protected void doGet(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)

throws ServletException, IOException {

response.sendRedirect("https://example.com");

}

}

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>Redirects the user to https://example.com.

#### Code Example (Forwarding):

@WebServlet("/forward")

public class ForwardServlet extends HttpServlet {

@Override

protected void doGet(HttpServletRequest request, HttpServletResponse response)

throws ServletException, IOException {

request.getRequestDispatcher("/hello").forward(request, response);

}

}

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>Forwards the request to the /hello servlet.

#### Explanation:

1.  Redirect:

<bdi>○</bdi>Sends a new request to the client, changing the URL.

1.  Forward:

<bdi>○</bdi>Passes the request to another resource without changing the URL.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices for Servlets

1.  Use Annotations:

<bdi>○</bdi>Prefer @WebServlet over web.xml for cleaner code.

1.  Thread Safety:

<bdi>○</bdi>Avoid using instance variables to prevent data corruption in multi-threaded environments.

1.  Separate Logic:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use servlets for request handling and delegate business logic to separate classes.

1.  Use JSP for Views:

<bdi>○</bdi>Combine servlets with JSP for separating logic and presentation.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Servlet Features:

<bdi>○</bdi>Handles HTTP requests, manages sessions, and integrates with web servers.

<bdi>●</bdi>Core Methods:

<bdi>○</bdi>init, service, doGet, doPost, and destroy.

<bdi>●</bdi>Use Cases:

<bdi>○</bdi>Form handling, session tracking, dynamic content generation.

<bdi>●</bdi>Best Practices:

<bdi>○</bdi>Maintain thread safety, use annotations, and separate concerns.

Java Servlets are a fundamental technology for building dynamic web applications, providing robust and efficient tools for server-side programming.

## Java Server Pages (JSP)

Java Server Pages (JSP) is a server-side technology in Java that simplifies the creation of dynamic web content. JSP is built on top of Java Servlets and is primarily used to design the view layer of web applications, allowing HTML and Java code to coexist within a single file.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of JSP

1.  Simplifies Dynamic Content Generation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Combines HTML and Java code, making it easier to create web pages.

1.  Tag-Based Development:

<bdi>○</bdi>Reduces the need for explicit Java code through custom tags and predefined JSP tags.

1.  Seamless Integration with Servlets:

<bdi>○</bdi>JSP files are translated into servlets by the server.

1.  Session Management:

<bdi>○</bdi>Built-in support for managing user sessions.

1.  Expression Language (EL):

<bdi>○</bdi>Simplifies data access in JSP by providing concise syntax for accessing server-side objects.

1.  Custom Tags and Tag Libraries:

<bdi>○</bdi>Allows for reusable components and modular design.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### How JSP Works

1.  Request Handling:

<bdi>○</bdi>A client sends an HTTP request for a .jsp file.

1.  JSP Translation:

<bdi>○</bdi>The server compiles the JSP file into a servlet (only done once).

1.  Execution:

<bdi>○</bdi>The servlet processes the request and generates dynamic content.

1.  Response:

<bdi>○</bdi>The server sends the generated HTML to the client.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Setting Up JSP

1.  Add a Servlet Container:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use a container like Apache Tomcat to run JSP files.

1.  Project Structure:

<bdi>○</bdi>Place JSP files in the webapp directory of your project.

1.  Configure Deployment Descriptor (optional for newer versions):

<bdi>○</bdi>Add web.xml if using custom configurations.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Basic JSP Syntax

#### JSP Syntax Elements

| Element | Syntax | Purpose |
| Directives | <%@ directive %> | Provides global information to the JSP. |
| Scriptlets | <% Java code %> | Embeds Java code. |
| Declarations | <%! Java code %> | Declares variables or methods. |
| Expressions | <%= expression %> | Outputs the value of an expression. |
| Comments | <%-- comment --%> | Adds comments that are not visible in HTML. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Basic JSP File

index.jsp:

<%@ page language="java" contentType="text/html; charset=UTF-8" %>

<!DOCTYPE html>

<html>

<head>

<title>Basic JSP</title>

</head>

<body>

<h1>Welcome to JSP!</h1>

<p>The current date and time is: <%= new java.util.Date() %></p>

</body>

</html>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Output:

Displays: Welcome to JSP!

The current date and time is: [Current Date and Time]

#### Explanation:

1.  Directives:

<bdi>○</bdi><%@ page ... %> specifies the page's content type and scripting language.

1.  Expression:

<bdi>○</bdi><%= new java.util.Date() %> evaluates the Java expression and outputs the result.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Handling Form Data

JSP can process form data submitted by clients using the request object.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Handling Form Submission

HTML Form:

<form action="process.jsp" method="POST">

<label for="name">Enter your name:</label>

<input type="text" id="name" name="name">

<button type="submit">Submit</button>

</form>

process.jsp:

<%@ page language="java" contentType="text/html; charset=UTF-8" %>

<!DOCTYPE html>

<html>

<head>

<title>Form Data</title>

</head>

<body>

<h1>Form Submission</h1>

<p>Hello, <%= request.getParameter("name") %>!</p>

</body>

</html>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Output:

When the user submits their name: Hello, [Name]!

#### Explanation:

1.  request.getParameter:

<bdi>○</bdi>Retrieves form data sent in the HTTP request.

1.  Dynamic Content:

<bdi>○</bdi>The submitted name is dynamically displayed on the page.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Using JSP Directives

JSP directives provide metadata and configuration for the page.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Types of Directives

| Directive | Purpose |
| page | Configures the page (e.g., language, encoding). |
| include | Includes another file during translation. |
| taglib | Declares a tag library for custom tags. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Including Files

header.jsp:

<!DOCTYPE html>

<html>

<head>

<title>Reusable Header</title>

</head>

<body>

<h1>Welcome to My Website</h1>

</body>

</html>

index.jsp:

<%@ include file="header.jsp" %>

<p>This is the main content of the page.</p>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Output:

Displays: Welcome to My Website

This is the main content of the page.

#### Explanation:

1.  include Directive:

<bdi>○</bdi>Embeds the content of header.jsp into index.jsp at translation time.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Expression Language (EL)

Expression Language (EL) simplifies access to server-side objects like request, session, and application.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Using EL

index.jsp:

<%@ page language="java" contentType="text/html; charset=UTF-8" %>

<% request.setAttribute("username", "John Doe"); %>

<!DOCTYPE html>

<html>

<head>

<title>Expression Language</title>

</head>

<body>

<h1>Welcome, ${username}!</h1>

</body>

</html>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Output:

Displays: Welcome, John Doe!

#### Explanation:

1.  EL Syntax:

<bdi>○</bdi>${username} retrieves the value of the username attribute.

1.  Cleaner Code:

<bdi>○</bdi>Reduces the need for Java code in JSP files.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Custom Tags with JSTL

The JSP Standard Tag Library (JSTL) provides reusable, tag-based functionality for common tasks.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Iteration with JSTL

Add JSTL Dependency for Maven:

<dependency>

<groupId>javax.servlet</groupId>

<artifactId>jstl</artifactId>

<version>1.2</version>

</dependency>

index.jsp:

<%@ taglib uri="http://java.sun.com/jsp/jstl/core" prefix="c" %>

<!DOCTYPE html>

<html>

<head>

<title>JSTL Example</title>

</head>

<body>

<h1>Items List</h1>

<ul>

<c:forEach var="item" items="${['Apple', 'Banana', 'Cherry']}">

<li>${item}</li>

</c:forEach>

</ul>

</body>

</html>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Output:

Displays: Items List

- Apple

- Banana

- Cherry

#### Explanation:

1.  JSTL:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides forEach for iterating over collections.

1.  EL and JSTL:

<bdi>○</bdi>Work together for cleaner and modular code.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Session Management in JSP

#### Code Example: Counting Page Visits

index.jsp:

<%@ page language="java" contentType="text/html; charset=UTF-8" %>

<%

Integer count = (Integer) session.getAttribute("count");

if (count == null) {

count = 1;

} else {

count++;

}

session.setAttribute("count", count);

%>

<!DOCTYPE html>

<html>

<head>

<title>Session Example</title>

</head>

<body>

<h1>You have visited this page <%= count %> times.</h1>

</body>

</html>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Output:

Displays: You have visited this page [X] times.

#### Explanation:

1.  Session Object:

<bdi>○</bdi>Tracks user interactions across multiple requests.

1.  Persistent Data:

<bdi>○</bdi>Data is retained across multiple page loads.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Best Practices for JSP

1.  Minimize Java Code:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use EL and JSTL to reduce Java code in JSP.

1.  Separation of Concerns:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use JSP for the view layer and servlets for the controller logic.

1.  Reusability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Include reusable headers, footers, and components.

1.  Error Handling:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use errorPage and isErrorPage directives for exception handling.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>JSP Features:

<bdi>○</bdi>Combines HTML and Java for dynamic web pages.

<bdi>●</bdi>Core Elements:

<bdi>○</bdi>Directives, scriptlets, EL, and JSTL.

<bdi>●</bdi>Use Cases:

<bdi>○</bdi>Displaying data, processing forms, session management.

<bdi>●</bdi>Best Practices:

<bdi>○</bdi>Focus on cleaner, modular, and maintainable code.

JSP simplifies web development by seamlessly integrating Java with HTML, making it a key technology for Java-based web applications.

## JavaServer Faces (JSF)

JavaServer Faces (JSF) is a Java-based web application framework used for building user interfaces for server-side applications. It simplifies the development process by providing a component-based architecture and integrates seamlessly with Java EE technologies.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of JSF

1.  Component-Based Development:

<bdi>○</bdi>JSF provides reusable UI components like buttons, text fields, and data tables.

1.  Event-Driven Programming:

<bdi>○</bdi>Supports server-side event handling for UI interactions.

1.  Built-in Validation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Offers built-in validation mechanisms and integrates with custom validators.

1.  Integration with Java EE:

<bdi>○</bdi>Works seamlessly with technologies like EJB, CDI, and JPA.

1.  Facelets:

<bdi>○</bdi>A templating system for building JSF views using XHTML.

1.  Managed Beans:

<bdi>○</bdi>Simplifies the connection between UI components and backend logic.

1.  Rich Tool Support:

<bdi>○</bdi>Supported by IDEs like Eclipse and IntelliJ for faster development.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### JSF Architecture

JSF follows the Model-View-Controller (MVC) architecture:

1.  Model:

<bdi>○</bdi>Represents the application's data and business logic, managed using JavaBeans or managed beans.

1.  View:

<bdi>○</bdi>Defines the UI layout using Facelets (XHTML files).

1.  Controller:

<bdi>○</bdi>Handles requests and responses, managing navigation and event handling.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Core Components of JSF

| Component | Description |
| Managed Beans | Java classes that manage application state and behavior. |
| Facelets | XHTML-based view definition framework. |
| UI Components | Reusable components like <h:inputText> and <h:commandButton>. |
| Event Handlers | Handle user interactions like button clicks and input changes. |
| Navigation Rules | Define page navigation flows. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Setting Up JSF

#### 1\. Add Dependencies

For Maven:

<dependency>

<groupId>javax.faces</groupId>

<artifactId>javax.faces-api</artifactId>

<version>2.3</version>

</dependency>

<dependency>

<groupId>org.glassfish</groupId>

<artifactId>javax.faces</artifactId>

<version>2.3.9</version>

</dependency>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### 2\. Configure Web Application

web.xml:

<web-app xmlns="http://java.sun.com/xml/ns/javaee" version="3.0">

<context-param>

<param-name>javax.faces.PROJECT_STAGE</param-name>

<param-value>Development</param-value>

</context-param>

<servlet>

<servlet-name>FacesServlet</servlet-name>

<servlet-class>javax.faces.webapp.FacesServlet</servlet-class>

<load-on-startup>1</load-on-startup>

</servlet>

<servlet-mapping>

<servlet-name>FacesServlet</servlet-name>

<url-pattern>*.xhtml</url-pattern>

</servlet-mapping>

</web-app>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### 3\. Project Structure

src/main/

├── java/com/example/bean/ # Managed Beans

├── webapp/

│ ├── index.xhtml # Main JSF Page

│ ├── WEB-INF/

│ │ └── web.xml # Deployment Descriptor

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Creating a Basic JSF Application

#### Code Example

Managed Bean:

package com.example.bean;

import javax.enterprise.context.RequestScoped;

import javax.inject.Named;

@Named

@RequestScoped

public class HelloBean {

private String message = "Welcome to JSF!";

public String getMessage() {

return message;

}

public void setMessage(String message) {

this.message = message;

}

}

View (Facelets): index.xhtml:

<!DOCTYPE html>

<html xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1999/xhtml"

xmlns:h="http://java.sun.com/jsf/html">

<head>

<title>JSF Example</title>

</head>

<body>

<h:form>

<h1>#{helloBean.message}</h1>

</h:form>

</body>

</html>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Output:

Displays: Welcome to JSF!

#### Explanation:

1.  Managed Bean:

<bdi>○</bdi>The HelloBean class is a request-scoped bean with a property message.

1.  Expression Language (EL):

<bdi>○</bdi>#{helloBean.message} accesses the bean's message property dynamically.

1.  Facelets:

<bdi>○</bdi>The XHTML file defines the structure of the UI.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Handling User Input

#### Code Example

Managed Bean:

package com.example.bean;

import javax.enterprise.context.RequestScoped;

import javax.inject.Named;

@Named

@RequestScoped

public class UserBean {

private String name;

public String getName() {

return name;

}

public void setName(String name) {

this.name = name;

}

public String greet() {

return "greet"; // Navigation to greet.xhtml

}

}

Input Form (index.xhtml):

<!DOCTYPE html>

<html xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1999/xhtml"

xmlns:h="http://java.sun.com/jsf/html">

<head>

<title>JSF Form</title>

</head>

<body>

<h:form>

<h:outputLabel for="name" value="Enter your name:" />

<h:inputText id="name" value="#{userBean.name}" />

<h:commandButton value="Submit" action="#{userBean.greet}" />

</h:form>

</body>

</html>

Result Page (greet.xhtml):

<!DOCTYPE html>

<html xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1999/xhtml"

xmlns:h="http://java.sun.com/jsf/html">

<head>

<title>Greeting</title>

</head>

<body>

<h1>Hello, #{userBean.name}!</h1>

</body>

</html>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Output:

1.  User enters their name and clicks Submit.

The application navigates to greet.xhtml displaying: Hello, [Name]!

#### Explanation:

1.  Data Binding:

<bdi>○</bdi>#{userBean.name} binds the form input to the bean's name property.

1.  Navigation:

<bdi>○</bdi>The greet method in the managed bean determines the next page.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Using Validation

JSF provides built-in validators for common tasks like required fields, length constraints, and email validation.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example

Form with Validation:

<!DOCTYPE html>

<html xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1999/xhtml"

xmlns:h="http://java.sun.com/jsf/html">

<head>

<title>JSF Validation</title>

</head>

<body>

<h:form>

<h:outputLabel for="email" value="Email:" />

<h:inputText id="email" value="#{userBean.email}" required="true">

<f:validateRegex pattern="^[A-Za-z0-9._%+-]+@[A-Za-z0-9.-]+\.[A-Za-z]{2,6}$" />

</h:inputText>

<h:message for="email" style="color:red;" />

<br/>

<h:commandButton value="Submit" action="#{userBean.submit}" />

</h:form>

</body>

</html>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>Displays error messages if the email field is empty or invalid.

#### Explanation:

1.  Validation:

<bdi>○</bdi>required="true" ensures the field is mandatory.

<bdi>○</bdi><f:validateRegex> checks if the input matches the specified pattern.

1.  Error Display:

<bdi>○</bdi><h:message> shows validation error messages.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Navigation Rules

JSF supports navigation rules to manage page flows.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example

faces-config.xml:

<navigation-rule>

<from-view-id>/index.xhtml</from-view-id>

<navigation-case>

<from-outcome>success</from-outcome>

<to-view-id>/success.xhtml</to-view-id>

</navigation-case>

</navigation-rule>

Managed Bean:

public String navigate() {

return "success"; // Maps to success.xhtml

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>Navigation rules determine the next page based on the outcome.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Using PrimeFaces for Advanced Components

PrimeFaces is a popular JSF library that provides rich UI components like charts, carousels, and dialogs.

#### Code Example: Data Table with PrimeFaces

Add PrimeFaces Dependency:

<dependency>

<groupId>org.primefaces</groupId>

<artifactId>primefaces</artifactId>

<version>12.0.0</version>

</dependency>

PrimeFaces Table:

<p:dataTable value="#{userBean.users}" var="user">

<p:column headerText="ID">

<h:outputText value="#{user.id}" />

</p:column>

<p:column headerText="Name">

<h:outputText value="#{user.name}" />

</p:column>

</p:dataTable>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Key Features:

<bdi>○</bdi>Component-based, event-driven, and supports MVC.

<bdi>●</bdi>Core Elements:

<bdi>○</bdi>Managed beans, Facelets, and navigation rules.

<bdi>●</bdi>Advanced Tools:

<bdi>○</bdi>Use PrimeFaces for enhanced UI.

<bdi>●</bdi>Use Cases:

<bdi>○</bdi>Ideal for enterprise-level web applications.

JSF is a powerful framework for building robust and interactive web applications, offering both simplicity and scalability.

## RESTful APIs Using Java JAX-RS Framework

JAX-RS (Java API for RESTful Web Services) is a Java framework for building RESTful APIs. It is part of the Java EE (now Jakarta EE) platform and provides a standard way to create REST APIs using Java annotations.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of JAX-RS

1.  Annotation-Based Programming:

<bdi>○</bdi>Simplifies the development of RESTful services with annotations like @GET, @POST, @Path, and more.

1.  POJO-Based:

<bdi>○</bdi>Allows the use of Plain Old Java Objects (POJOs) to define API resources.

1.  Content Negotiation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Supports multiple formats (e.g., JSON, XML) through @Produces and @Consumes.

1.  Integration with Java EE:

<bdi>○</bdi>Works seamlessly with other Java EE technologies like CDI, EJB, and JPA.

1.  Asynchronous Processing:

<bdi>○</bdi>Supports asynchronous request and response handling for scalability.

1.  Exception Handling:

<bdi>○</bdi>Custom exceptions and error mappers for clean error management.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Setting Up JAX-RS

#### 1\. Add Dependencies

For Maven projects:

<dependency>

<groupId>jakarta.ws.rs</groupId>

<artifactId>jakarta.ws.rs-api</artifactId>

<version>3.0.0</version>

</dependency>

<dependency>

<groupId>org.glassfish.jersey.core</groupId>

<artifactId>jersey-common</artifactId>

<version>3.0.5</version>

</dependency>

<dependency>

<groupId>org.glassfish.jersey.containers</groupId>

<artifactId>jersey-container-servlet</artifactId>

<version>3.0.5</version>

</dependency>

<dependency>

<groupId>org.glassfish.jersey.media</groupId>

<artifactId>jersey-media-json-binding</artifactId>

<version>3.0.5</version>

</dependency>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### 2\. Configure web.xml

In traditional Java EE applications, map the JAX-RS application to a URL pattern:

<web-app>

<servlet>

<servlet-name>JAX-RS Application</servlet-name>

<servlet-class>org.glassfish.jersey.servlet.ServletContainer</servlet-class>

<init-param>

<param-name>jersey.config.server.provider.packages</param-name>

<param-value>com.example.resources</param-value>

</init-param>

<load-on-startup>1</load-on-startup>

</servlet>

<servlet-mapping>

<servlet-name>JAX-RS Application</servlet-name>

<url-pattern>/api/*</url-pattern>

</servlet-mapping>

</web-app>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Core Annotations in JAX-RS

| Annotation | Description |
| @Path | Defines the URI path for a resource class or method. |
| @GET | Maps a method to handle HTTP GET requests. |
| @POST | Maps a method to handle HTTP POST requests. |
| @PUT | Maps a method to handle HTTP PUT requests. |
| @DELETE | Maps a method to handle HTTP DELETE requests. |
| @Produces | Specifies the response format (e.g., JSON, XML). |
| @Consumes | Specifies the request format (e.g., JSON, XML). |
| @QueryParam | Extracts query parameters from the URI. |
| @PathParam | Extracts path parameters from the URI. |
| @FormParam | Extracts form parameters from a request body. |
| @Context | Injects contextual data like UriInfo, HttpHeaders, etc. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Creating a Simple REST API

#### Code Example: Hello World API

Resource Class:

package com.example.resources;

import jakarta.ws.rs.GET;

import jakarta.ws.rs.Path;

import jakarta.ws.rs.Produces;

import jakarta.ws.rs.core.MediaType;

@Path("/hello")

public class HelloResource {

@GET

@Produces(MediaType.TEXT_PLAIN)

public String sayHello() {

return "Hello, World!";

}

}

#### Explanation:

1.  @Path:

<bdi>○</bdi>Maps the resource class to the /hello path.

1.  @GET:

<bdi>○</bdi>Maps the sayHello method to HTTP GET requests.

1.  @Produces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Specifies the response format as plain text.

#### Output:

Accessing /api/hello returns: Hello, World!

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Returning JSON Data

#### Code Example: JSON Response

Model Class:

package com.example.models;

public class User {

private int id;

private String name;

public User(int id, String name) {

this.id = id;

this.name = name;

}

public int getId() {

return id;

}

public void setId(int id) {

this.id = id;

}

public String getName() {

return name;

}

public void setName(String name) {

this.name = name;

}

}

Resource Class:

package com.example.resources;

import com.example.models.User;

import jakarta.ws.rs.GET;

import jakarta.ws.rs.Path;

import jakarta.ws.rs.Produces;

import jakarta.ws.rs.core.MediaType;

@Path("/users")

public class UserResource {

@GET

@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)

public User getUser() {

return new User(1, "John Doe");

}

}

#### Explanation:

1.  JSON Binding:

<bdi>○</bdi>The response is automatically serialized to JSON.

1.  MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON:

<bdi>○</bdi>Specifies the response format as JSON.

#### Output:

Accessing /api/users returns: {

"id": 1,

"name": "John Doe"

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Handling Path Parameters

#### Code Example: Dynamic Path Parameter

Resource Class:

package com.example.resources;

import jakarta.ws.rs.GET;

import jakarta.ws.rs.Path;

import jakarta.ws.rs.PathParam;

import jakarta.ws.rs.Produces;

import jakarta.ws.rs.core.MediaType;

@Path("/users")

public class UserResource {

@GET

@Path("/{id}")

@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)

public String getUserById(@PathParam("id") int id) {

return "User ID: " + id;

}

}

#### Explanation:

1.  @PathParam:

<bdi>○</bdi>Extracts the {id} parameter from the URI.

1.  Dynamic URIs:

<bdi>○</bdi>Supports flexible endpoints.

#### Output:

Accessing /api/users/5 returns: User ID: 5

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Handling Query Parameters

#### Code Example: Query Parameters

Resource Class:

package com.example.resources;

import jakarta.ws.rs.GET;

import jakarta.ws.rs.Path;

import jakarta.ws.rs.Produces;

import jakarta.ws.rs.QueryParam;

import jakarta.ws.rs.core.MediaType;

@Path("/search")

public class SearchResource {

@GET

@Produces(MediaType.TEXT_PLAIN)

public String search(@QueryParam("q") String query) {

return "You searched for: " + query;

}

}

#### Explanation:

1.  @QueryParam:

<bdi>○</bdi>Extracts query parameters from the URI.

1.  Flexible Search:

<bdi>○</bdi>Handles dynamic user inputs.

#### Output:

Accessing /api/search?q=Java returns: You searched for: Java

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Handling POST Requests

#### Code Example: Accepting JSON Input

Resource Class:

package com.example.resources;

import com.example.models.User;

import jakarta.ws.rs.Consumes;

import jakarta.ws.rs.POST;

import jakarta.ws.rs.Path;

import jakarta.ws.rs.Produces;

import jakarta.ws.rs.core.MediaType;

@Path("/users")

public class UserResource {

@POST

@Consumes(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)

@Produces(MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON)

public User createUser(User user) {

user.setId(100); // Simulate assigning an ID

return user;

}

}

#### Explanation:

1.  @POST:

<bdi>○</bdi>Maps the method to HTTP POST requests.

1.  @Consumes:

<bdi>○</bdi>Specifies that the method accepts JSON input.

1.  @Produces:

<bdi>○</bdi>Returns the created user as JSON.

#### Input:

{

"name": "Jane Doe"

}

#### Output:

The server responds with: {

"id": 100,

"name": "Jane Doe"

}

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 6\. Exception Handling

#### Code Example: Custom Exception Mapper

Custom Exception:

package com.example.exceptions;

public class UserNotFoundException extends RuntimeException {

public UserNotFoundException(String message) {

super(message);

}

}

Exception Mapper:

package com.example.exceptions;

import jakarta.ws.rs.core.Response;

import jakarta.ws.rs.ext.ExceptionMapper;

import jakarta.ws.rs.ext.Provider;

@Provider

public class UserNotFoundExceptionMapper implements ExceptionMapper<UserNotFoundException> {

@Override

public Response toResponse(UserNotFoundException exception) {

return Response.status(Response.Status.NOT_FOUND)

.entity(exception.getMessage())

.build();

}

}

#### Explanation:

1.  Custom Exception:

<bdi>○</bdi>Represents application-specific errors.

1.  Exception Mapper:

<bdi>○</bdi>Maps exceptions to meaningful HTTP responses.

#### Output:

<bdi>●</bdi>A UserNotFoundException results in a 404 Not Found response with the error message.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Summary

<bdi>●</bdi>Core Concepts:

<bdi>○</bdi>JAX-RS provides an annotation-based approach for building REST APIs.

<bdi>●</bdi>Flexible Annotations:

<bdi>○</bdi>@Path, @GET, @POST, @Produces, and others simplify development.

<bdi>●</bdi>Content Negotiation:

<bdi>○</bdi>Handles JSON, XML, and other formats effortlessly.

<bdi>●</bdi>Scalability:

<bdi>○</bdi>Supports asynchronous processing and integration with Java EE features.

JAX-RS is a robust framework for building modern RESTful APIs, ensuring clean and maintainable code for enterprise applications.

## Introduction to the Spring Framework

The Spring Framework is a powerful, open-source, and feature-rich Java platform for building enterprise-level applications. It simplifies the development process by providing robust infrastructure support, integration with other frameworks, and a variety of tools for building scalable, maintainable, and testable applications.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Key Features of the Spring Framework

1.  Inversion of Control (IoC):

<bdi>○</bdi>Manages the lifecycle and dependencies of application objects, reducing the need for manual configurations.

1.  Dependency Injection (DI):

<bdi>○</bdi>Enables loose coupling by injecting dependencies into objects instead of hardcoding them.

1.  Aspect-Oriented Programming (AOP):

<bdi>○</bdi>Separates cross-cutting concerns (like logging, transactions) from the core logic.

1.  Data Access:

<bdi>○</bdi>Provides simplified access to databases using JDBC and ORM frameworks like Hibernate.

1.  Model-View-Controller (MVC):

<bdi>○</bdi>A framework for building robust and flexible web applications.

1.  Transaction Management:

<bdi>○</bdi>Manages transactions declaratively or programmatically.

1.  Integration:

<bdi>○</bdi>Seamlessly integrates with other technologies like JPA, JMS, and REST.

1.  Spring Boot:

<bdi>○</bdi>A simplified framework for building standalone applications with minimal configuration.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Core Modules of the Spring Framework

| Module | Description |
| Spring Core | Provides IoC and DI features. |
| Spring AOP | Supports Aspect-Oriented Programming. |
| Spring Data | Simplifies data access and integrates with ORM frameworks like Hibernate. |
| Spring MVC | Implements the Model-View-Controller pattern for web applications. |
| Spring Security | Offers authentication, authorization, and security features. |
| Spring Boot | Simplifies Spring application setup and development. |

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### Setting Up the Spring Framework

#### 1\. Add Dependencies

For Maven projects:

<dependency>

<groupId>org.springframework</groupId>

<artifactId>spring-context</artifactId>

<version>5.3.27</version>

</dependency>

<dependency>

<groupId>org.springframework</groupId>

<artifactId>spring-web</artifactId>

<version>5.3.27</version>

</dependency>

For Spring Boot (optional):

<dependency>

<groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>

<artifactId>spring-boot-starter</artifactId>

<version>3.1.0</version>

</dependency>

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 1\. Spring Core: Inversion of Control and Dependency Injection

#### Concept of IoC

<bdi>●</bdi>Inversion of Control (IoC):

<bdi>○</bdi>The control of object creation and lifecycle management is handed over to the Spring container.

<bdi>●</bdi>Dependency Injection (DI):

<bdi>○</bdi>Injects required dependencies into objects automatically.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Dependency Injection

Bean Class:

package com.example.beans;

public class HelloWorld {

private String message;

public void setMessage(String message) {

this.message = message;

}

public void displayMessage() {

System.out.println("Message: " + message);

}

}

Spring Configuration (XML):

<beans xmlns="http://www.springframework.org/schema/beans"

xmlns:xsi="http://www.w3.org/2001/XMLSchema-instance"

xsi:schemaLocation="http://www.springframework.org/schema/beans

http://www.springframework.org/schema/beans/spring-beans.xsd">

<bean id="helloWorld" class="com.example.beans.HelloWorld">

<property name="message" value="Welcome to Spring Framework!" />

</bean>

</beans>

Main Class:

package com.example;

import com.example.beans.HelloWorld;

import org.springframework.context.ApplicationContext;

import org.springframework.context.support.ClassPathXmlApplicationContext;

public class MainApp {

public static void main(String[] args) {

ApplicationContext context = new ClassPathXmlApplicationContext("Beans.xml");

HelloWorld helloWorld = (HelloWorld) context.getBean("helloWorld");

helloWorld.displayMessage();

}

}

#### Output:

Message: Welcome to Spring Framework!

#### Explanation:

1.  ApplicationContext:

<bdi>○</bdi>Loads the Spring configuration and manages the beans.

1.  Dependency Injection:

<bdi>○</bdi>The message property is injected into the HelloWorld bean from the XML configuration.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 2\. Spring Annotations

Spring provides annotations to simplify configurations and reduce the need for XML.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Annotations for DI

Bean Class:

package com.example.beans;

import org.springframework.stereotype.Component;

@Component

public class HelloWorld {

private String message = "Hello from Spring Annotations!";

public void displayMessage() {

System.out.println(message);

}

}

Main Class:

package com.example;

import com.example.beans.HelloWorld;

import org.springframework.context.ApplicationContext;

import org.springframework.context.annotation.AnnotationConfigApplicationContext;

public class MainApp {

public static void main(String[] args) {

ApplicationContext context = new AnnotationConfigApplicationContext("com.example.beans");

HelloWorld helloWorld = context.getBean(HelloWorld.class);

helloWorld.displayMessage();

}

}

#### Output:

Hello from Spring Annotations!

#### Explanation:

1.  @Component:

<bdi>○</bdi>Marks the class as a Spring-managed bean.

1.  AnnotationConfigApplicationContext:

<bdi>○</bdi>Scans for annotated components.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 3\. Spring MVC: Building Web Applications

Spring MVC provides a framework for building web applications based on the Model-View-Controller pattern.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Simple Spring MVC

Controller:

package com.example.controllers;

import org.springframework.stereotype.Controller;

import org.springframework.ui.Model;

import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.GetMapping;

@Controller

public class HelloController {

@GetMapping("/hello")

public String sayHello(Model model) {

model.addAttribute("message", "Hello, Spring MVC!");

return "hello";

}

}

View (hello.jsp):

<!DOCTYPE html>

<html>

<head>

<title>Hello Page</title>

</head>

<body>

<h1>${message}</h1>

</body>

</html>

Spring Configuration:

<beans xmlns="http://www.springframework.org/schema/beans"

xmlns:xsi="http://www.w3.org/2001/XMLSchema-instance"

xsi:schemaLocation="http://www.springframework.org/schema/beans

http://www.springframework.org/schema/beans/spring-beans.xsd">

<bean class="org.springframework.web.servlet.view.InternalResourceViewResolver">

<property name="prefix" value="/WEB-INF/views/" />

<property name="suffix" value=".jsp" />

</bean>

</beans>

#### Output:

Visiting /hello displays: Hello, Spring MVC!

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 4\. Spring Boot: Simplifying Spring Applications

Spring Boot eliminates much of the boilerplate configuration in traditional Spring applications.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Simple Spring Boot Application

Application Class:

package com.example;

import org.springframework.boot.SpringApplication;

import org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.SpringBootApplication;

import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.GetMapping;

import org.springframework.web.bind.annotation.RestController;

@SpringBootApplication

@RestController

public class SpringBootApp {

public static void main(String[] args) {

SpringApplication.run(SpringBootApp.class, args);

}

@GetMapping("/")

public String home() {

return "Welcome to Spring Boot!";

}

}

#### Output:

Accessing http://localhost:8080/ returns: Welcome to Spring Boot!

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

### 5\. Spring AOP: Aspect-Oriented Programming

Spring AOP provides a way to modularize cross-cutting concerns like logging or security.

￼![Rectangle Rectangle](images/image.png)

#### Code Example: Logging Aspect

Aspect Class:

package com.example.aspects;

import org.aspectj.lang.annotation.Aspect;

import org.aspectj.lang.annotation.Before;

import org.springframework.stereotype.Component;

@Aspect

@Component

public class LoggingAspect {

@Before("execution(* com.example.services.*.*(..))")

public void logBefore() {

System.out.println("Logging before method execution");

}

}

Service Class:

package com.example.services;

import org.springframework.stereotype.Service;

@Service
